We test this practice on a 28-quant cohort of Qwen3.6-35B-A3B and a 41-quant cohort of Devstral-Small-2-24B, evaluated across a suite of downstream benchmarks.
We find that KLD is strongly correlated with benchmark score over the full cohort ( ρ = − 0.72 \rho=-0.72 on Qwen and ρ = − 0.86 \rho=-0.86 on Devstral, both with p < 0.001 p<0.001 ).
However, this relationship collapses to non-significance in the near-baseline silent zone ( ρ = + 0.00 \rho=+0.00 on Qwen and ρ = − 0.24 \rho=-0.24 , p = 0.36 p=0.36 , on Devstral).
This collapse persists across 14 measurement variants, including different KLD aggregations, perplexity formulations, top-1 agreement, calibration corpora, and context lengths.
At the per-prompt level, KLD has only weak failure-prediction power on code, with failed-vs-passed geometric-mean ratios in [ 1.08 , 1.22 ] [1.08,1.22] across five models on LiveCodeBench, and fails as a cross-model router, achieving only 42.3–49.4% accuracy on disagreement prompts.
We trace the collapse to a structural decomposition: KLD primarily measures the volume of disagreement with the reference, with silent-zone composite ρ = + 0.94 \rho=+0.94 ( p < 0.001 p<0.001 ) on Qwen and + 0.55 +0.55 ( p = 0.03 p=0.03 ) on Devstral, while its relationship to the direction of those disagreements is weak and task-conditional.
For Qwen3.6-35B-A3B ( Qwen, 2026 ) alone, the community published hundreds of GGUF quants, made by different publishers, with different calibration recipes, and very different output qualities.
Running a full benchmark suite on every candidate to judge quality is an expensive endeavour, so practitioners have resorted to using lower-cost metrics, in particular fidelity metrics like KL divergence (KLD) or Perplexity (PPL) against a BF16 reference.
At first glance, these metrics appear to work: across the full cohort, KLD and PPL correlate strongly with composite benchmark score, with Spearman ρ ⁡ ( KLD , composite quality ) = − 0.72 \rho(\text{KLD},\text{composite quality})=-0.72 on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B and − 0.86 -0.86 on Devstral-2-Small-24B (both with p < 0.001 p<0.001 ), and similar values across every fidelity-metric variant we tested.
When we restrict the analysis to near-baseline candidates, which are the models practitioners are most likely to choose from, the correlation collapses, with Spearman ρ ⁡ ( KLD , composite score ) = + 0.00 \rho(\text{KLD},\text{composite score})=+0.00 for Qwen and − 0.24 -0.24 ( p = 0.36 p=0.36 ) for Devstral.
Inside the silent zone, fidelity metrics fail at three levels: they do not 1) reliably rank models, 2) separate correct from incorrect responses, or 3) route a prompt to the better of two models when models disagree.
Silent-zone vs Lossy-zone characterization: We identify a near-baseline silent zone where KLD/PPL lose ranking power, and a degraded lossy zone where they remain useful as coarse predictors (Table 2 ).
Decompose score into volume and direction: We build on the work of ( Dutta et al., 2024 ) by extending the observed flips into leapfrogs (quantized model is correct where the reference is not) and drops (the reverse), and derive a closed-form decomposition score = ref score + vol ⋅ ( 2 ​ f − 1 ) / N \text{score}=\text{ref}_{\text{score}}+\text{vol}\cdot(2f-1)/N .
KLD universally tracks volume; whether it also tracks direction is task-conditional (Table 5 ).
Per-prompt KLD has no useful model-selection signal: At the per-prompt level on LiveCodeBench, KLD only weakly separates a model’s own passes from its failures (fail-vs-pass geometric-mean ratios in [ 1.08 , 1.22 ] [1.08,1.22] across five models) and fails to route between quants ( 50.6 − 57.7 % 50.6-57.7\% error rate).
The collapse is metric-invariant: The non-significant silent-zone correlation ( | ρ | ≤ 0.25 |\rho|\leq 0.25 , all p > 0.10 p>0.10 ) holds across 14 variants of the per-token comparison metric, including PPL ratio, Δ \Delta log-PPL, top-1 agreement, longer contexts, and on-task calibration (Table 3 ).
2 Related Work Quantization is widely used to reduce the memory, bandwidth, and compute cost of deploying large language models.
To preserve quality at lower bit-widths, prior work has introduced practical post-training quantization methods such as GPTQ, AWQ, and SmoothQuant ( Frantar et al., 2023 ; Lin et al., 2024 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ) .
Further compression can be achieved with mixed-precision quantization, where precision is assigned at finer granularity than the whole network, such as per tensor, layer, channel, or feature, e.g., ( Zheng et al., 2024 ; Nikolić et al., 2024 ) .
More recent studies evaluate the accuracy-performance trade-offs of quantized LLMs across model families, bit-widths, hardware targets, and downstream tasks ( Lee et al., 2025 ; Jaiswal et al., 2024 ; Kurtic et al., 2025 ) .
2.1 Beyond PPL and KLD The limitations of PPL and related fidelity metrics for evaluating compressed models have been noted before.
Jaiswal et al. (2024) show that pruned and quantized models can preserve perplexity while losing downstream capabilities, and introduce a broader benchmark suite for compressed models.
Deiseroth et al. (2024) similarly argue that perplexity and aggregate accuracy do not fully capture generation degradation, and propose divergent-token metrics as a more generation-sensitive diagnostic for pruning and quantization.
We split flips into leapfrogs (quantized model correct, reference wrong) and drops (reference correct, quantized model wrong), and decompose score as ref score + vol ⋅ ( 2 ​ f − 1 ) / N \text{ref}_{\text{score}}+\text{vol}\cdot(2f-1)/N .
3 Methodology We evaluate two quantized-model cohorts with deliberately different architectures (Table 1 ): 28 GGUF quantizations of Qwen3.6-35B-A3B ( Qwen, 2026 ) , a mixture-of-experts model, and 41 quantizations of the dense, code-specialized Devstral-Small-2-24B-Instruct-2512 ( Mistral AI, 2025 ) .
All fidelity metrics and leapfrog/drop counts are computed relative to the corresponding BF16 reference.
Item Qwen cohort Devstral cohort Reference model Qwen3.6-35B-A3B Devstral-Small-2-24B Architecture MoE (35B/3B active) dense (24B) Specialization general / coding-capable code-specialized Cohort size 28 quants 41 quants Quant sources 5 3 bpw range 2.17–6.14 1.88–9.84 Silent-zone τ \tau KLD ≤ 0.064 \leq 0.064 KLD ≤ 0.027 \leq 0.027 Silent / lossy split 17 / 11 16 / 25 The cohorts draw from multiple public quantization sources and span a broad range of compression, from near-reference high-bpw variants to aggressively compressed low-bpw ones.
3.1 Downstream Quality Evaluation We evaluate downstream quality on benchmarks spanning coding (HumanEval ( Chen et al., 2021 ) , LiveCodeBench v6 ( Jain et al., 2025 ) ), instruction following (IFEval ( Zhou et al., 2023 ) ), tool calling (BFCL v3 ( Patil et al., 2025 ) ), math reasoning (GSM8K ( Cobbe et al., 2021 ) ), math and visual reasoning (GSM8K-v ( Yuan et al., 2025 ) ), and multitask knowledge (MMLU ( Hendrycks et al., 2021 ) ); the Devstral cohort adds MATH-500 ( Lightman et al., 2023 ) .
All benchmarks run in non-thinking mode, except GSM8K-v, which on the thinking-capable Qwen3.6 cohort we run in both modes.
We report per-benchmark scores and a composite (unweighted mean of normalized scores), oriented so higher is better and compared against the BF16 reference; per-prompt pass/fail outcomes feed the leapfrog/drop and routing analyses.
All evaluations use the EvalScope framework ( ModelScope, 2024 ) , serving each GGUF model with llama.cpp ( Gerganov, 2023 ) (build b8855 for Qwen3.6, b7744 for Devstral).
3.2 Fidelity Metrics Unless otherwise stated, KLD denotes KL ( p BF16 ∥ p quant ) \mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{BF16}}\,\|\,p_{\mathrm{quant}}) over next-token distributions.
Response-only measurements score only response tokens (excluding the prompt); two diagnostics use this form with top- k k truncation, defined in Appendix B : the validation-set variants in Table 3 (a held-out instruction mixture, Appendix D ) and the per-prompt analyses (benchmark prompt-response pairs).
The default and headline metric is WikiText ( Merity et al., 2017 ) at 512-token context, which defines the main silent-zone split; we also test 8192-token context and the response-only variants above to probe sensitivity to context length and corpus.
4 The Silent Zone Our first finding is that every fidelity metric we tested exhibits a silent zone : a near-baseline region where the metric no longer ranks downstream benchmark quality.
For each cohort, we report a descriptive silent-zone threshold τ \tau that marks the near-baseline cluster of quants: a KLD cutoff below which the within-zone Spearman correlation between KLD and composite quality is statistically non-significant ( p > 0.10 p>0.10 ), i.e., a regime with no detectable monotone KLD–quality signal.
For default WikiText KLD at 512-token context, τ = 0.064 \tau=0.064 for Qwen and τ = 0.027 \tau=0.027 for Devstral.
Because τ \tau is not intended as a general decision rule, Appendix C repeats the analysis with a KLD-threshold-free quality definition, where near-baseline quants are those that retain at least 99 % 99\% of the BF16 composite score. What we usually see as the full-cohort KLD-vs-quality correlation is the lossy zone leaking into the cohort average through the inclusion of 2–3-bit models that have noticeably degraded quality.

5 Disagreement Volume and Direction What does KLD actually measure, if not quality?
For a benchmark of N N prompts where the reference (BF16) passes N p N_{p} and a candidate quant passes N q N_{q} , define: (1) leapfrogs ℓ \ell : prompts where quant passes and reference fails; (2) drops d d : prompts where quant fails and reference passes; (3) volume vol = ℓ + d \text{vol}=\ell+d : total disagreement count; and (4) direction f = ℓ / vol f=\ell/\text{vol} : fraction of disagreements that are improvements.
By construction: score q = score ref + vol ⋅ ( 2 ​ f − 1 ) N \vskip-5.0pt\text{score}_{q}\;=\;\text{score}_{\text{ref}}+\frac{\text{vol}\cdot(2f-1)}{N}\vskip-5.0pt (1) where score q \text{score}_{q} and score r ​ e ​ f \text{score}_{ref} are benchmark scores of the quant and BF16.
The identity is exact for benchmarks whose official score is one binary outcome per prompt (HumanEval, LCB, IFEval, GSM8K, GSM8K-v, MATH-500), and approximate for BFCL_v3 and MMLU, whose official scores macro-average over sub-categories with a weighting different from the per-prompt count we use for leap/drop accounting.

===== 2606.26650 (127 条) =====
G Discussion of Limitations License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2606.26650v1 [cs.CL] 25 Jun 2026 CAT-Q: Cost-efficient and Accurate Ternary Quantization for LLMs Shigeng Wang Affiliation: Intel Labs China.
We show that, for pre-trained LLMs with 1.7B to 8B parameters, CAT-Q can efficiently quantize them into ternary models using only 512 calibration samples, while achieving superior performance than the seminal BitNet 1.58-bit v1 and v2 families (with 1.3B to 7B parameters) trained with 100B tokens, yielding about a 100,000 × \times reduction in training tokens.
Moreover, we show for the first time that CAT-Q can quantize much larger pre-trained LLMs having 14B to 235B parameters into leading ternary models within just 8 to 60 hours on 8 A100-80GB GPUs.
Various techniques ( Zafrir et al., 2019 ; Ma et al., 2023 ; Sun et al., 2024 ; Sanh et al., 2019 ; Hsieh et al., 2023 ; Zhang et al., 2021 ; Hsu et al., 2022 ) have been proposed to reduce model size and accelerate inference, among which quantization is particularly appealing due to its effectiveness and ease of implementation.
Most existing methods ( Shen et al., 2020 ; Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Yao et al., 2022 ; Frantar et al., 2023 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ; Shao et al., 2024 ; Lin et al., 2024b ; Chee et al., 2024 ; Ashkboos et al., 2024b ; Sun et al., 2025 ; Wang et al., 2026 ) rely on PTQ, which quantizes pre-trained LLMs using a small set of calibration samples and does not require the highly expensive training or re-training pipeline used in QAT, and are therefore easy to use in practice.
These PTQ methods are generally effective in 8-bit quantization, and some, such as QuaRot ( Ashkboos et al., 2024b ) , FlatQuant ( Sun et al., 2025 ) and SliderQuant ( Wang et al., 2026 ) , attain favorable performance even at 4-bit precision.
However, they still exhibit poor performance in ultra-low-bit settings, such as 2-bit quantization.
In this paper, we focus on ternary (also known as 1.58-bit) quantization, an extreme form of quantization, which maps high-precision weights to { 1 , 0 , − 1 } \{1,0,-1\} .
It brings more than a 10 × \times reduction in memory consumption relative to its FP16 counterpart.
Moreover, by introducing sparsity through the zero state, ternary quantization retains the hardware-friendly arithmetic advantage of binary quantization ( Courbariaux et al., 2015 ; Rastegari et al., 2016 ; Xu et al., 2024 ; Huang et al., 2024 ; Shang et al., 2024 ; Li et al., 2025 ) , whereby most expensive floating-point multiplication operations can be replaced by far more energy-efficient integer additions and subtractions, while offering richer expressive capacity.
For ternary quantization, it is challenging to devise an effective optimization scheme that can accurately approximate high-precision weights by only using values of { 1 , 0 , − 1 } \{1,0,-1\} due to the high risk of information loss.
To alleviate severe accuracy drop in a more straightforward manner, existing state-of-the-art ternary quantization methods, such as BitNet 1.58-bit ( Ma et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2025 ) , TriLM ( Kaushal et al., 2025 ) and Tequila ( Huang et al., 2026 ) , unanimously adopt QAT, which enables the model to adapt its parameters to 1.58-bit precision by simulating ternarization during full-precision training.
Tequila ( Huang et al., 2026 ) , the most recent ternary quantization method, still requires 10B tokens to quantize a pre-trained LLM into a ternary model via re-training.
Furthermore, Tequila is merely evaluated on two small-scale models: the 1B and 3B instances of the Llama3 family ( Grattafiori et al., 2024 ) .
Given a small calibration set (e.g., 512 sequences of length 2048 tokens randomly sampled following common practice in the PTQ field, rather than using the original training data) and a pre-trained LLM with arbitrary architecture and model scale, we identify that two major challenges are closely related to the severe performance degradation observed when minimizing reconstruction error of ternary weight quantization.
To address this extreme discretization problem, existing ternary quantization methods for LLMs follow previous strategies ( Li et al., 2016 ; Zhu et al., 2017 ) originally designed for ternarizing convolutional neural networks containing significantly fewer parameters.
We conduct comprehensive experiments to evaluate the efficacy of CAT-Q across 10 pre-trained LLMs spanning diverse architectures (including dense and mixture of experts (MoE) models) and model scales (1.7B to 235B parameters) ( Yang et al., 2024 ; Yang et al., 2025 ; Touvron et al., 2023 ) .
We show that, under similar model scales (1.7B to 8B parameters), ternary weight models generated by CAT-Q using only 512 calibration samples (about 1 million tokens) outperform the seminal BitNet 1.58-bit v1 and v2 families trained with 100B tokens, bringing an approximately 100,000 × \times reduction in training tokens.
Moreover, we show for the first time that CAT-Q can readily scale to much larger pre-trained LLMs having 14B to 235B parameters.
When quantizing LLMs with 1.7B to 235B parameters, CAT-Q requires only 1 to 60 hours on 8 A100-80GB GPUs.
In addition, our models with ternary weights and 8-bit activations get performance comparable to their ternary-weight-only counterparts, further demonstrating the broader applicability of our method.
Figure 1 : Overview of the CAT-Q’s learning flow for ternarizing the weights of a linear layer in any pre-trained LLM and its hardware-friendly weight reconstruction for ternary model deployment.
2.1 Preliminary Concepts and Motivation The concept of ternary weight quantization was originally proposed in TWN ( Li et al., 2016 ) to train convolutional neural networks from scratch for computer vision tasks.
TernaryBERT ( Zhang et al., 2020 ) makes an early research effort to ternarize the weights of small BERT-based language models ( Devlin et al., 2019 ) during fine-tuning, which uses transformer distillation ( Jiao et al., 2020 ) to compensate for severe accuracy degradation.
Instead, existing state-of-the-art ternary LLMs, such as BitNet 1.58-bit ( Ma et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2025 ) , TriLM ( Kaushal et al., 2025 ) and Tequila ( Huang et al., 2026 ) , rely on QAT.
Under the same settings, we use the 4 t ​ h 4^{th} layer of Qwen3-4B for an illustration.
Recent ternary LLMs, such as BitNet 1.58-bit ( Ma et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2025 ) , TriLM ( Kaushal et al., 2025 ) and Tequila ( Huang et al., 2026 ) , simply use the absmean ternarization: α = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n | W i | \alpha=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|W_{i}| and Δ = α 2 \Delta=\frac{\alpha}{2} .
We notice that some prior works ( Rastegari et al., 2016 ; Zhu et al., 2017 ; Chen et al., 2024b ) directly treat the scaling factor α \alpha and or the threshold Δ \Delta as learnable parameters to train binary or ternary neural networks, while others ( Esser et al., 2020 ; Gong et al., 2019 ; Liu et al., 2025b ) extend such learning paradigm to estimate quantization step sizes under different bit-width settings.
However, in the PTQ regime, we empirically find that directly learning α \alpha and Δ \Delta still suffers from the distributional misalignment between the resulting ternary weights and their pre-trained high-precision counterparts, showing only modest improvement over static approximation to alleviate severe accuracy degradation Figure 3 : Illustration of the softened ternarization (ST) process.
For a linear layer, taking its pre-trained weights W as the initialization point ( t = 0 t=0 ), ST employs a learnable two-stage relay of differentiable ternarization and hard ternarization to ensure stable convergence.
It relies on a novel smooth transition function f ⁡ ( ⋅ ) f(\cdot) to gradually evolve from the identity mapping to differentiable ternarization via a sequence of continuous mappings with progressively increasing sharpness along the normalized calibration time state 0 < t ≤ γ 0<t\leq\gamma .
This disentangled learning strategy not only preserves the original hardware-friendly implementation property of TWN but also yields better ternarization performance than the counterpart that approximates W ≈ α ​ T + μ \textbf{W}\approx\alpha\textbf{T}+\mu , as validated by an ablation shown in Table 10 .
Mathematically, following the form of the ternarization function Q ⁡ ( ⋅ ) Q(\cdot) defined in Equation 2 , in the LM component, the i t ​ h i^{th} element of the ternary weights T is obtained by: T i = Q ⁡ ( W ^ i , δ Δ ​ Δ 0 ) .
T_{i}=Q(\hat{W}_{i};\delta_{\Delta}\Delta_{0}). (4) 2.3 Softened Ternarization Due to its extreme low-bit form and non-differentiable nature, ternary weight quantization is inherently difficult to optimize and converge.
Following TWN ( Li et al., 2016 ) , existing QAT-based ternarization methods for LLMs ( Ma et al., 2024 ; Wang et al., 2025 ; Kaushal et al., 2025 ; Huang et al., 2026 ) perform hard ternarization using the straight-through estimator for gradient approximation, where convergence is largely facilitated by the quantization-aware training process, particularly for LLMs with small model scales.
Although, in Figure 2 , we have shown that learning the scaling factor α \alpha and the threshold Δ \Delta with the LM component achieves better performance than static approximation and direct learning methods, it still performs hard ternarization (facing the non-differentiability issue) throughout the calibration learning, lacking a technical guarantee for good convergence.
This transition function has several appealing properties: (1) for any choice of s s , it has a symmetric output curve about the origin and is differentiable in terms of W W ; (2) when s → 0 s\to 0 , it approximates the identity mapping f ⁡ ( ⋅ ) = W f(\cdot)=W ; (3) when s s increases from a small positive value to a sufficiently large value with a uniform step size, it behaves as a finite sequence of continuous mappings with progressively increasing sharpness, and asymptotically converges toward the ternary output in a differentiable manner.
Empirically, when s = 4.95 s=4.95 , the output is already scaled within [ − 1 , 1 ] [-1,1] ; (4) when s → ∞ s\to\infty , it approaches the hard ternarization defined in Equation 2 .
We couple this transition function with the LM component, and adapt them across calibration learning iterations, forming our softened ternarization process: T i = { W i , i ​ f ​ t = 0 f ⁡ ( W ^ i , t γ ​ s 0 , δ Δ ​ Δ 0 ) , i ​ f ​ 0 < t ≤ γ Q ⁡ ( W ^ i , δ Δ ​ Δ 0 ) , i ​ f ​ γ < t ≤ 1 . \vskip 0.0ptT_{i}=\begin{cases}W_{i},&if\ t=0\\ f(\hat{W}_{i};\frac{t}{\gamma}s_{0},\delta_{\Delta}\Delta_{0}),&if\ 0<t\leq\gamma\\ Q(\hat{W}_{i};\delta_{\Delta}\Delta_{0}),&if\ \gamma<t\leq 1.\end{cases}\vskip 0.0pt (6) Let m m denote the total number of calibration epochs (by default, we set m = 60 m=60 when using 512 calibration samples) for ternarizing the weights of a pre-trained LLM.
Here, γ \gamma denotes the calibration epoch ratio allocated for differentiable ternarization based on a finite sequence of continuous mappings, 1 − γ 1-\gamma denotes the calibration epoch ratio allocated for hard ternarization, t t denotes the normalized time state of the current calibration epoch, and s 0 > 0 s_{0}>0 is a constant to initialize the sharpness (by default, we set s 0 = 30 s_{0}=30 ) of the transition function f ⁡ ( ⋅ ) f(\cdot) .
In the first stage, ST produces an asymptotic ternary output by performing continuous quantization from the identity mapping to differentiable ternarization of the transformed weights W ^ \hat{\textbf{W}} via a finite sequence of continuous mappings, which is governed by the transition function f ⁡ ( ⋅ ) f(\cdot) with progressively increasing sharpness along the normalized calibration time state 0 < t ≤ γ 0<t\leq\gamma .
2.4 Sliding-Layer Ternarization Optimization In implementation, we adopt a sliding-layer output reconstruction scheme instead of the predominant layer-wise weight reconstruction used in existing LLM ternarization methods.
We are inspired by recent works ( Liu et al., 2024b ; Ding et al., 2025 ; Wang et al., 2026 ) that show quantizing multiple layers together tends to yield reduced quantization errors against quantizing a single layer as it makes neighboring layers be aware of each other, enabling to use layer dependencies to mitigate information loss.
Let 𝒲 = { W 1 , … ​ W l } \mathcal{W}=\{\textbf{W}_{1},...\textbf{W}_{l}\} denote the set of high-precision weights for the current sliding window consisting of l l layers in a pre-trained LLM, and let X denote its input feature corresponding to a small set of calibration samples.
3.1 Setup To ensure a comprehensive evaluation, we apply our method to a diverse set of LLMs, varying in architecture and model size.
Specifically, we select 7 dense models from the Llama2 and Qwen3 families ( Touvron et al., 2023 ; Yang et al., 2025 ) , as well as 3 sparsely-gated mixture of experts (MoE) models including Qwen3-30B-A3B, Qwen3-235B-A22B, and Ring-flash-2.0 (100B-A6.1B) ( Team et al., 2025 ) , covering a wide model size range from 1.7B to 235B parameters.
By default, we use 512 samples randomly selected from C4 ( Raffel et al., 2020 ) for calibration, each with a length of 2048 tokens.
Following common practices in QAT-based ternarization research, all models are primarily evaluated in a zero-shot setting on five widely adopted commonsense reasoning benchmarks: PIQA ( Bisk et al., 2020 ) , ARC-Easy (ARC-e) and ARC-Challenge (ARC-c) ( Clark et al., 2018 ) , HellaSwag (HS) ( Zellers et al., 2019 ) , and Winogrande (WG) ( Sakaguchi et al., 2021 ) .
Table 1 : Performance of different ternary LLMs produced by CAT-Q in the PTQ regime and evaluated on five zero-shot commonsense reasoning benchmarks.
First, we compare CAT-Q with existing state-of-the-art 1.58-bit quantization methods that rely on quantization-aware training (QAT), including BitNet 1.58-bit v1 ( Ma et al., 2024 ) , BitNet 1.58-bit v2 ( Wang et al., 2025 ) , TernaryLLM ( Chen et al., 2024b ) , TriLM ( Kaushal et al., 2025 ) and Tequila ( Huang et al., 2026 ) .
Second, we compare CAT-Q with some recent PTQ methods under their ultra-low-bit configurations: 2-bit methods including GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) , AWQ ( Lin et al., 2024b ) , OmniQuant ( Shao et al., 2024 ) , EfficientQAT ( Chen et al., 2025 ) and SliderQuant ( Wang et al., 2026 ) , as well as dual 1-bit methods including BiLLM ( Huang et al., 2024 ) , PB-LLM ( Shang et al., 2024 ) and DB-LLM ( Chen et al., 2024a ) .
3.3 Main Results Ternary Quantization Results of CAT-Q.
The performance degradation caused by quantization decreases as the model size increases, with Llama2-70B exhibiting only a 3.81% drop.
Notably, we demonstrate for the first time that CAT-Q can quantize LLMs having up to 235B parameters into leading ternary weight models within 60 hours on 8 A100-80GB GPUs.

VI Discussion VII Conclusion References License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2607.08734v2 [cs.AI] 21 Aug 2026 The Illusion of Equivalency: Statistical Characterization of Quantization Effects in LLMs Baha Rababah 1,2 , Shahzeb Qamar 3 , Lorenz Sparrenberg 3 , Rafet Sifa 3 Murat Kantarcioglu 4 , Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora 5 , Carson K.
We use this metric across multiple models and quantization bit levels (8-bit to 2-bit), and we find that the base and quantized variants usually have a shift in behavior even when accuracy and perplexity are preserved.
It has emerged as an effective AI approach to reduce memory footprint, accelerate inference, and reduce energy consumption [ 3 ] .
However, quantization can degrade performance and change model behavior, especially under low-bit precision settings such as 2-bits [ 4 ] .
These properties are especially vulnerable to quantization, as small numerical changes in model parameters may lead to unexpected behavioral changes [ 4 ] .
Nowadays, existing quantization evaluations frameworks [ 5 , 6 , 3 ] use surface-level metrics such as accuracy, loss, and perplexity.
For example, A model could achieve 75 % 75\% accuracy at base model and 75 % 75\% accuracy at quantized model does not necessarily mean that the same predictions were preserved.
For example, 15 % 15\% of the questions that were correctly answered at based could become incorrect at quantized, while another 15 % 15\% of previously incorrect questions could become correct.
We also provide a deep analysis of weight changes across a wide range of quantization levels (8-bit to 2-bit).
II Background and Related Work Quantization reduces the numerical precision of neural network weights from floating point to lower bit representations in order to decrease model size, memory bandwidth usage, and inference cost on resource-constrained hardware [ 7 ] .
Existing approaches fall into two categories: Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) and Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) [ 8 ] .
In this work, we focus on two quantization families implemented in llama.cpp : legacy quantization and K-quantization [ 13 ] , which together cover the full precision range from 8-bit to 2-bit [ 12 ] .
Q8_0 , Q5_0 , Q4_0 ) use block-wise uniform linear quantization with per-block scaling over fixed blocks of k = 256 k=256 weights.
Several studies focus on evaluating quantized LLMs that can be categorized into: (1) knowledge and capacity assessment, measuring correctness and reasoning ability [ 14 , 15 ] ; and (2) alignment evaluation, assessing adherence to human preferences and safety [ 16 , 17 ] .
Jin et al. [ 3 ] proposed a structured evaluation framework spanning knowledge, alignment, and efficiency, showing that 4-bit quantization can maintain model performance.
Another work by Kurtic et al. [ 5 ] analyzed FP8, INT8, and INT4 quantization across the LLaMA-3.1 family, illustrating that FP8 is effectively lossless, INT8 results in minimal accuracy reduction, and INT4 weight only quantization performs better than expected.

However, aggregate accuracy completely ignores instance-level behavioral stability that is, whether M q M_{q} preserves correct predictions on the exact same individual prompts relative to M 0 M_{0} .

Unlike aggregate task accuracy, CA \mathrm{CA} explicitly quantifies preserved correct choices between M 0 M_{0} and M q M_{q} , isolating true internal decision persistence from coincidental accuracy matching.

1: Aggregated statistical metrics across legacy and K-quantization methods.
Figure 1 shows that higher-bit quantization schemes (8 to 5 bits) commonly preserve the statistical properties of attention weights, with near-zero mean, low skewness, stable kurtosis, and baseline level standard deviation across models.
On the other hand, low-bit quantization (4 to 2 bits), particularly Q3_K and Q2_K , introduces large distortions, including increased skewness and standared deviation, reduced kurtosis, and systematic mean shifts.
To study the impact of quantization on individual attention projection component, we calculate statistics independently for each attention projection type ( K K , Q Q , V V , O O ) by averaging s ⁡ ( W i L ) s(W_{i}^{L}) across all N N blocks, as defined in Eq. ( 7 ).
For legacy quantization schemes ( Q8_0 , Q5_0 , Q4_0 ), layer-wise statistics remain almost identical to the base model across all metrics, which indicates that legacy quantization has minimal structural impact.

Moderate K-quantization levels ( Q6_K – Q4_K ) preserve attention-layer statistics across Q Q , K K , V V , and O O , maintaining stable distribution shape, tail behavior, and variance.
However, aggressive low-bit settings ( Q3_K and Q2_K ) lead to large distortions, especially in the Q Q and K K projections, where skewness becomes volatile, kurtosis collapses, and mean and variance deviate sharply from the base model.

Cosine similarity remains almost stable across high bit quantizations (Q8_0 through Q5_K), drops slightly at Q4_0, recovers at Q4_K and Q3_K, and drop sharply at Q2_K.

Both Q Q and K K have consistent, lower divergence baseline ( 0.75) across Q5_K, Q4_K, and Q2_K.

We introduce MXSens, a training-free method that assigns mixed mantissa bitwidths (4/6/8) based on column- and layer-wise sensitivity, naturally leveraging the block-wise structure of MXINT.
Under the W4A4KV4 setting, MXSens achieves perplexities of 3.77 and 7.63 on LLaMA-2-70B and LLaMA-3-8B, respectively, substantially improving over existing baselines on WikiText-2.
1 Introduction Quantization has emerged as a critical technique for enabling time- and energy-efficient inference in large language models (LLMs), significantly reducing memory footprint and accelerating execution by using low-precision numerical formats.
Despite its effectiveness, LLM quantization remains challenging due to the presence of systematic outliers in activations ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ) .
These high-magnitude values distort the distribution within quantization blocks and complicate the selection of appropriate scaling factors–particularly in low-bitwidth regimes such as 4-bit quantization.
Recent methods tackle this problem in two main ways: (1) reshaping the tensor distributions using rotation matrices, as in QuaRot ( Ashkboos et al., 2024b ) , RRS ( Yi et al., 2025 ) , and SpinQuant ( Liu et al., 2024 ) , or (2) applying mixed-precision quantization, as done in Atom ( Zhao et al., 2024 ) , QoQ ( Lin et al., 2024b ) , and QUIK ( Ashkboos et al., 2024a ) .
All of these approaches use INT4 as the primary number format, but to maintain accuracy, they rely on sub-channel or per-group quantization with software-handled FP32 scaling.
This approach requires frequent dequantization operations to and from FP32 during inference, which can incur up to 90% dequantization overhead relative to pure INT4 baselines ( Lin et al., 2024b ) , significantly undermining the benefits of low-bit quantization.
Microscaling formats eliminate this overhead by embedding power-of-two scale factors directly into the numeric representation ( Rouhani et al., 2023a ; Rouhani et al., 2023c ) .
Instead of relying on software-handled FP32 scales, microscaling formats embed a shared power-of-two scale per small block (e.g., 32 values), enabling low-bit inference using fixed-point MACs in the case of MXINT, or low-bit floating-point in the case of MXFP.
As shown in AMXFP ( Lee et al., 2025 ) , applying rotations on MXFP/MXINT tensors introduces group-wise asymmetry, which degrades quantization quality and cancels out the accuracy benefits of fine-grained scaling.
Consistent with recent studies that categorize outliers into different types ( Lin et al., 2024a ; Yi et al., 2025 ) , our analysis reveals a broader spectrum of activation sensitivity.
Outliers vary in magnitude, ranging from rare, extremely high values to more moderate deviations, which we refer to as mild outliers and still cause significant quantization error under 4-bit formats.
Building on recent advances in microscaling formats, MXSens is the next step toward practical, high-accuracy MXINT4 deployment: our sensitivity analysis shows that selective precision allocation is both necessary for accuracy and naturally supported by MXINT’s block-wise structure.
Our contributions are as follows: • We introduce a sensitivity-guided triplet quantization method that uses three distinct bitwidths (e.g., 4, 6, and 8 bits) for weights and activations, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy at comparable compression rates without modifying the model architecture or requiring retraining or fine-tuning. • We adopt the MXINT numeric format for our quantization method, leveraging its block-wise structure and shared exponent to enable efficient mixed-precision implementation. • MXSens outperforms SOTA quantization methods across a range of models and tasks.
In particular, under the W4A4KV4 setting, MXSens achieves perplexities of 3.77 and 7.63 on LLaMA-2-70B and LLaMA-3-8B, respectively, on WikiText-2, substantially improving over existing baselines.
2 Background Microscaling formats ( Rouhani et al., 2023b ) , such as MXFP and MXINT, have recently emerged as promising numeric representations for low-bit inference.
They strike a balance between dynamic range and hardware efficiency by applying a shared power-of-two scale to fixed-size blocks of values (typically 32), encoded as a floating-point-style exponent.
Despite this progress, quantization at 4-bit precision still suffers from substantial accuracy loss, primarily due to outliers, which are defined as a small number of disproportionately large values in the tensor ( Timkey and Van Schijndel, 2021 ; Bondarenko et al., 2021 ; Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Wei et al., 2022 ; Puccetti et al., 2022 ) .
Rotation methods aim to reshape the input distribution before quantization ( Ashkboos et al., 2024b ; Yi et al., 2025 ; Lin et al., 2024a ; Liu et al., 2024 ) .
For example, SpinQuant’s rotations are particularly limited on models such as Gemma-2 ( Mesnard et al., 2024 ) , which feature both pre-norm and post-norm structures ( Liu et al., 2024 ) .
Instead of reshaping distributions, it assigns higher precision to blocks or channels with high quantization sensitivity ( Zhao et al., 2024 ; Ashkboos et al., 2024a ; Lin et al., 2024b ; Liu et al., 2025 ; Ramachandran et al., 2025 ) .
This makes mixed-precision MXINT a practical and scalable approach to high-accuracy low-bit inference, preserving the compactness and compatibility of MX formats while addressing the limitations of uniform 4-bit quantization.
3 Sensitivity Metrics for Mixed-Precision Quantizing specific components of a model, such as certain columns and layers, can have a disproportionate impact on overall model accuracy.
3.1 Column-wise Sensitivity Column-wise sensitivity refers to the variation in quantization error across columns.
This sensitivity is largely driven by systematic outliers ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Lee et al., 2024 ) —values that consistently deviate from the bulk distribution across samples—which distort the shared scale within a quantization block.
In microscaling formats like MXINT, where blocks of 32 values share a power-of-two scale, such distortion leads to underutilized dynamic range and higher quantization error.
We observe that while outliers are the primary cause of column-wise sensitivity, their varying magnitudes ( Yi et al., 2025 ; Lin et al., 2024a ) significantly influence the degree of this sensitivity.
Prior work has shown that within linear layers, a small subset of highly sensitive columns accounts for a disproportionate share of the overall quantization error ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Lee et al., 2024 ) , underscoring the importance of column-level granularity.
The quantization-induced error on output channel i i is approximated as ( Lee et al., 2024 ) : E i = ∥ W i , : X − W ^ i , : X ∥ 2 2 ≈ 1 2 Δ W i , : T H Δ W i , : E_{i}=\|W_{i,:}X-\hat{W}_{i,:}X\|_{2}^{2}\approx\frac{1}{2}\Delta W_{i,:}^{T}H\Delta W_{i,:} (1) Here, H = X T ​ X H=X^{T}X denotes the empirical Hessian matrix of the loss function with respect to the weights, computed from input activations.
When both weights and activations are quantized, the same Hessian diagonal H j , j = ∑ k = 1 n X k , j 2 H_{j,j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}X_{k,j}^{2} serves as a sensitivity indicator.
3.2 Layer-wise Sensitivity Layer-wise sensitivity is another critical factor, as layers exhibit varying sensitivity to quantization under identical bitwidth allocation.
To capture this variability, we quantify layer-wise sensitivity by measuring the L 2 L_{2} error at the model’s final output when quantization is applied to a single layer in isolation.
As shown in Figure , quantization errors introduced in earlier layers of the LLaMA-2-7B model ( Meta, 2024 ) compound as they propagate through the network, amplifying their effect on the final output.
This position-dependent amplification leads to systematically higher sensitivity in early layers across all linear layer types (Same holds for LLaMA-3-8B, and Mistral-7B-v0.3, while middle layers are more sensitive for Qwen1.5-7B as shown in Appendix ).
Figure 1: MXINT4 error propagation in q-proj for single-layer quantization on LLaMA-2-7B.
Figure 2: Perplexity dynamics of bitwidth allocation strategies on Llama-3-8B.
4 MXSens: Sensitivity-Guided Triplet Quantization In this section, we introduce MXSens , a novel quantization method that selectively assigns bitwidths to columns and layers based on their sensitivity to quantization.
Unlike prior methods that apply uniform or layer-wise fixed precision, MXSens jointly leverages column- and layer-wise sensitivity to allocate three precision levels—4, 6, and 8 bits—optimally under a given average bitwidth budget.
This sensitivity stems from the presence of both extreme and mild outliers : Extreme outliers are rare but high-magnitude values ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Lin et al., 2024a ; Yi et al., 2025 ) that dominate the dynamic range within a block and require high precision, e.g., 8 bits, to avoid severe quantization error.
Mild outliers are more frequent and have smaller magnitudes, but they still cause non-negligible degradation when quantized to low bitwidths like MXINT4.
Assigning 8-bit precision to the most sensitive columns, with extreme outliers, significantly improves accuracy.
However, extending 8-bit allocation to additional columns yields diminishing returns, as only a small fraction of columns actually contain such extreme values.
A broader subset of columns, exhibiting moderate sensitivity due to mild outliers, benefits more from intermediate precision, such as 6 bits, enabling more efficient use of the overall bitwidth.
4.1 Triplet Quantization To address the full spectrum of sensitivity across columns, we propose triplet quantization, which assigns three precision levels—4, 6, 8 bits—based on quantization sensitivity.
We observe that in each layer, the number of columns containing extreme outliers is typically smaller than the block size of MXINT (32).
This makes MXINT particularly well-suited for a mixed-mantissa strategy: we assign 8-bit precision to the top 32 most sensitive columns in each layer, ensuring that all extreme outliers are captured within one high-precision block per layer.
Mild outliers are assigned 6-bit mantissas, again in full blocks, while the rest default to 4 bits.
Figure shows that triplet quantization (4-6-8) consistently outperforms the two-precision method (4-8) across a wide range of average bitwidths.
The blue curve corresponds to a baseline scheme where the 32 most sensitive columns per layer are assigned 8-bit precision, and all remaining columns default to 4 bits.
In contrast, the orange curve illustrates our proposed strategy: the top 32 columns are assigned 8 bits, while the remaining columns are divided between 6 and 4 bits based on column sensitivity and average bitwidth constraints.
Moreover, as shown in Appendix , columns with extreme outliers consistently require 8-bit precision to prevent significant accuracy loss, reinforcing the need for dedicated high-precision blocks in each layer.
4.2 Combining Column- and Layer-wise Sensitivity While triplet quantization effectively captures column-wise sensitivity by introducing an intermediate 6-bit level, it does not account for global variation across layers.
The method retains the use of 8-bit mantissas for the 32 most sensitive columns in each layer, capturing all extreme outliers while aligning with MXINT’s block size.
For the remaining columns, MXSens distributes 6- and 4-bit precision in a layer-aware fashion: layers with higher overall sensitivity are assigned more 6-bit columns, while less sensitive layers receive fewer.
Algorithm 2 Sensitivity-Based Bitwidth Allocation 1: Set of column sensitivities S c ​ o ​ l = { s 1 , s 2 , … } S_{col}=\{s_{1},s_{2},\dots\} , layer sensitivity vector S l ​ a ​ y ​ e ​ r S_{layer} , average bit-width B B .
2: Map of column indices ℬ i ​ n ​ d ​ i ​ c ​ e ​ s \mathcal{B}_{indices} for each bitwidth per layer.
Next, the number of 6-bit columns is computed in proportion to the layer’s sensitivity score, scaled by a global factor R R to meet the target average bitwidth B B .

8 Conclusions References A Benchmarks and Models Benchmarks.
1 Introduction A language model is evaluated at full precision and typically deployed compressed.
Post-training quantization shrinks the weights before release, often by a factor of 3 to 14, so the build a user interacts with is usually not the one the evaluation measured.
Quantization compresses an aligned model from 16 to about one bit per weight.
Second, we give the mechanism (Section 7 ): quantizers allocate precision to calibration data that carries no bias signal, leaving the bias-relevant weights fragile, and we show when reasoning before answering suppresses the effect and when it does not: reasoning halves the open-ended bias on Qwen but leaves it unchanged on Gemma.
We introduce QuantiBias , a benchmark that pairs a generative, multilingual stereotype probe with the refusal and multiple-choice controls that establish the effect is specific to open-ended generation, contrasts each build with and without reasoning, and rates the content severity of what a model generates (Section 4 ).
2 Related Work Quantization is known to change model behavior beyond the capability benchmarks used to validate it, and its effect on safety and bias has been studied unevenly.
Xu et al. (2024) evaluate pruning and quantization along degeneration harm, representational harm, and dialect bias, and show that generation-side safety can erode while task accuracy holds.
Closest to our setting, Marcuzzi et al. (2026) extend this across 13 benchmarks and report that aggressive quantization slightly raises stereotyping and bias in generative tasks while lowering toxicity and leaving sentiment flat.
Rath and Maliakkal (2026) find that three-bit quantization turns 6 to 21% of previously unbiased BBQ items stereotypical, and Hua et al. (2026) show that such flips often cancel in aggregate, masking large per-group swings.
On the safety side, Kharinaev et al. (2025) document alignment degradation across 66 quantized variants, Egashira et al. (2024) show that quantization can be weaponized adversarially so the artifact a user evaluates is not the one they deploy, and Al Hakim et al. (2026) protect bias-critical weights and find the damage worst in non-English settings.
First, we isolate a selectivity effect that aggregate reporting obscures: the short-form safeguards these evaluations rely on, refusal and multiple-choice bias avoidance, stay flat while the bias a model volunteers in open-ended generation rises ( Jin et al., 2025 ) , so a build passes the checks and reaches users more biased.
Throughout, we index results to effective bits per weight measured from the artifact rather than nominal quantizer labels, which vary in what they deliver (Section 3 ).
3 Measuring True Quantization A build’s name states a nominal bits-per-weight, but conventional low-bit builds retain some components, the embeddings, attention, and output head, at substantially higher precision, and the size of that hidden margin varies from build to build and vendor to vendor.
Table 1 gives the two anchor ladders; nominal labels overstate the degree of quantization by 11 to 40% (Figure A3 ).
Table 1: Measured effective bits per weight for the evaluation ladders.
Nominal labels overstate quantization by 11 to 40%; deterministic from the artifact file.
Backbone Build Nominal Measured bpw Qwen3.6-27B BF16 16 16.00 Qwen3.6-27B UD-Q4_K_XL 4-bit 5.235 Qwen3.6-27B UD-IQ2_XXS 2-bit 2.789 Qwen3.6-27B Ternary-Bonsai ternary 2.128 Qwen3.6-27B Bonsai Q1_0 1-bit 1.128 Gemma-4-31B BF16 16 16.00 Gemma-4-31B UD-Q4_K_XL 4-bit 4.901 Gemma-4-31B UD-IQ2_XXS 2-bit 2.220 Two consequences follow.
In the “2-bit” UD-IQ2_XXS build the multilayer-perceptron weights sit at 2.14 bits while the output head and attention tensors carry substantially more (4.53 and 3.55 bits), and the build mixes nine distinct quantization types; normalization tensors stay at full 32-bit precision.
This mixing is inherent to the llama.cpp k-quant schemes these builds use ( Gerganov and others, 2023 ) , which, like activation-aware and error-compensating post-training quantizers ( Frantar et al., 2023 ; Lin et al., 2024 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ) , allocate more bits to weights they judge sensitive; that allocation is tuned on capability data and has no reason to track bias.
The end-to-end ternary and one-bit builds, following the trend toward natively low-bit models ( Ma et al., 2024 ) , are the exception, holding perceptron, attention, and output head at a single flat precision, which is why their measured value tracks their label far more closely.
Multilingual bias suites exist, but each drops one of these axes: MBBQ is multiple-choice ( Neplenbroek et al., 2024 ) , the Multilingual Holistic Bias set scores template sentences rather than free generation ( Costa-jussà et al., 2023 ) , and SHADES supplies parallel stereotypes without a compression axis ( Mitchell et al., 2025 ) .
1 1 1 Code, rubrics, and baseline results: https://huggingface.co/datasets/emilioferrara/quantibias 5 Experiments Instrumentation.
Decoding is held constant across precisions (temperature 0.7, top- p p 0.95, fixed token budgets, the model’s own chat template).
We score the full-ladder generative probe with an in-family Qwen3-8B lean judge, which is fast enough to run at scale, and validate that scoring with two independent judges drawn from different vendors and from families outside the evaluation set: Claude Sonnet-5 (Anthropic) and Gemini-2.5-pro (Google), both fixed frontier models never themselves quantized, so the quantization under test cannot affect the instrument that measures them.
An independent generative benchmark (CEB ( Wang et al., 2024 ) ) provides a second instrument over disjoint items; a next-token benchmark (StereoSet ( Nadeem et al., 2021 ) ) serves as a short-form negative control; and 2 capability anchors (MMLU-Redux ( Gema et al., 2025 ) , GSM8K ( Cobbe et al., 2021 ) ) separate safety-specific degradation from general capability loss.
Every translated field is independently back-translated (Gemini 2.5 Flash) and scored against its source for gross drift, with low-agreement rows flagged rather than dropped and reserved for native-speaker validation.
Table A3 reports the safeguard, capability, and MBTP benchmarks across the full Qwen3.6-27B ladder, from 16 to 1.13 measured bpw.
The safeguards standard evaluations probe do not move: refusal of directly harmful requests stays at 0.98, over-refusal on XSTest near 0.33, and BBQ accuracy under ambiguity near 0.97, at every quantized rung.
The capability anchors hold through IQ2 and lose only a little at the one-bit extreme (MMLU-Redux 0.952 to 0.877), which separates safety-specific degradation from general capability loss: from Q4 to IQ2 capability is flat while generative bias already rises.
The stable multiple-choice scores may also be flattered by contamination: if BBQ-style items sit in the training data, a model can recognize the evaluation format and answer it well while its open-ended behavior diverges ( Balloccu et al., 2024 ) , a defeat device in the sense of Ferrara (2026) .
Safeguard, capability, and MBTP benchmarks across the Qwen3.6-27B ladder (more compression rightward).
The upper panel holds: refusal, multiple-choice bias avoidance (BBQ), and capability (GSM8K, MMLU-Redux, declining only at one bit) stay near full precision, so a standard audit reports it unchanged.
We score every one-bit instance the lean judge marked as an endorsement on a 0–4 content-severity rubric (0, no biased content; 4, a biased generalization asserted as fact) with a judge independent of the evaluation set.
Of the 48 instances, mean severity is 1.83 and the harm is real but bounded: 33 score 2 or higher (a stated group generalization) while none reach the severity-4 ceiling, so the failure mode is a confident stereotype rather than the most extreme assertion.
CEB, a generative bias benchmark sharing no items with MBTP, rises with compression across the Qwen ladder under both judges ( n ≈ 960 n\approx 960 per rung, reasoning off): the lean judge reports 3.5 % 3.5\% , 21.9 % 21.9\% , 24.9 % 24.9\% and the independent judge 13.5 % 13.5\% , 23.0 % 23.0\% , 37.6 % 37.6\% from Q4 to one bit (Figure 5 ), the same direction and judge-dependence as MBTP.
The one-bit rung is a separate end-to-end ternary backbone, so the robust comparison is the Q4-to-IQ2 rise on the shared Qwen ladder, which both judges show.
Figure 5: An independent generative-bias benchmark.
Both rise with compression, independent above lean as with MBTP; the one-bit rung is a separate ternary backbone, so the Q4-to-IQ2 rise on the shared ladder is the robust comparison.
Q8 (open circle) and Q2 (filled) stereotype rate per family with 95% intervals: lean judge (Qwen3-8B, n ≤ 320 n\leq 320 ) above, independent (Claude Sonnet-5, n = 60 n=60 ) below, on their own scales.
Baselines differ about twofold across families, with the 8B class above the 27B anchor, consistent with the size fragility reported by Chand et al. (2026) .
For round-to-nearest quantization with group scale s ∝ 2 − b s\propto 2^{-b} , each weight moves by at most s / 2 s/2 , so 𝔼 ​ ‖ Δ b ‖ 2 ∝ 2 − 2 ​ b \mathbb{E}\,\|\Delta_{b}\|^{2}\propto 2^{-2b} : the perturbation magnitude is set by the measured bit width.
This is the same tail-probability object that governs flip rates under Gaussian smoothing ( Cohen et al., 2019 ) : a behavior changes only as fast as noise of scale σ ⁡ ( b ) \sigma(b) reaches its margin.
Reasoning helps because each committed token is written to the transcript and conditioned on thereafter ( Guan et al., 2024 ) , letting later steps correct an error the drift introduced; it fails when the deliberation never revisits the stereotype decision, which nothing guarantees.
8 Conclusions Quantization leaves a model’s short-form safety behavior almost untouched while its open-ended generation grows markedly more biased.
Limitations The effect is clear; the ways it could be sharpened are specific and named, in order of how much they matter. (1) The full-sample bias results rest on a single in-family judge; on the controlled parallel probe we ran a three-family panel (Section 5 ), and the two independent judges agree closely ( α = 0.69 \alpha=0.69 ) while the in-family judge is uncorrelated with both, which shows absolute bias levels are judge-family-dependent, consistent with known LLM-as-judge pitfalls of position and self-preference bias ( Zheng et al., 2023 ; Panickssery et al., 2024 ; Wataoka et al., 2024 ) .
Label instability of this kind is not confined to model judges: human harm annotations are themselves inconsistent enough that filtering high-variance annotators flips majority harm classifications for 18.6 % 18.6\% of prompts ( Ghafouri et al., 2026 ) .

6 Conclusion References A Reproducibility License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2607.25451v1 [cs.LG] 28 Jul 2026 Bits and Memories: Measuring Verbatim Extraction Across LLM Quantization Akshay Sasi Affiliation: Independent Researcher Email: akshaysasi12.knr@gmail.com Abstract Language models are almost always quantized before they are deployed, and a growing line of work asks whether quantization also lowers their privacy risk.
This has been demonstrated repeatedly, from the early extraction attacks on GPT-2 ( Carlini et al., 2021 ) to systematic studies showing that the amount of memorized text grows with model size, with how many times a passage was duplicated in training, and with how much context the model is given ( Carlini et al., 2023 ) , and up to large-scale extraction from production systems ( Nasr et al., 2023 ) .
Several papers report that quantization reduces membership-inference success, on code models ( Haque et al., 2025 ) , on image classifiers ( Zhang et al., 2025a ) , and across compression schemes more broadly ( Li et al., 2026 ) , and the natural takeaway is that quantization helps privacy.
This is robust: it appears under bitsandbytes’ NF4 and under a plain, dependency-free round-to-nearest quantizer we wrote ourselves, and it appears whether we measure capability on WikiText or on in-distribution Pile text.
Carlini et al. (2023) turned this into a quantitative science, establishing log-linear relationships between memorization and model scale, duplication count, and context length.
More recent work refines how extraction should even be defined and measured, moving from strict greedy reproduction toward probabilistic notions of discoverable extraction ( Hayes et al., 2024 ) , and studies memorization at finer granularity ( Zhou et al., 2023 ) .
Extraction has also been scaled up to production models ( Nasr et al., 2023 ) .
Eight-bit inference with outlier handling ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ) made lossless eight-bit deployment routine.
GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) brought accurate four-bit and lower post-training quantization using second-order information; QLoRA ( Dettmers et al., 2023 ) introduced the NF4 data type we use as our main four-bit method; and activation-aware and smoothing methods ( Lin et al., 2023 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ) further reduced the accuracy cost.
Inference scaling laws show that four bits sits near the accuracy-per-bit sweet spot ( Dettmers and Zettlemoyer, 2023 ) , which is one reason it is the regime deployments favor and the regime where our effect is strongest.
Haque et al. (2025) study quantization and membership inference on code models and report reduced attack effectiveness at lower precision; Zhang et al. (2025a) report large membership-inference reductions from post-training quantization on image classifiers; Li et al. (2026) show that compression can, under some access models, increase leakage.
Theoretical and empirical treatments of membership-inference risk under quantization are beginning to appear ( Aubinais et al., 2025 ) .
Most relevant to our interpretation, Zhang et al. (2024) show that quantizing a model that has been unlearned can bring the supposedly removed knowledge back, direct evidence that memorized information can be stored in a quantization-robust way.
At every precision we compute perplexity with a sliding window over two corpora: WikiText-2 ( Merity et al., 2017 ) , which is out of the training distribution, and a sample of Pile text, which is in it.
Our main precision ladder is FP32, FP16, INT8 (LLM.int8() ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ) ), and two four-bit bitsandbytes types, NF4 and FP4 ( Dettmers et al., 2023 ) .
Table 1: Extraction and capability across precision and model size. “Extract” is the verbatim exact-match rate; perplexity is on WikiText-2 (WT) and Pile; m m , c c , s s are memorization retained, capability retained (Pile), and selectivity relative to the full-precision reference.
At 410M, dropping to NF4 costs a bit under half the capability (Pile perplexity rises by about seventy percent) but nearly three-quarters of the memorization.
The partial-match metrics tell a consistent story: the mean correct-prefix length collapses from about 28 of 32 tokens at full precision to roughly 12 at NF4, so quantization is not just flipping a few borderline sequences, it is shortening how far the model can faithfully reproduce a passage before it drifts.
4.2 But it is not a privacy defense, and gets less so with scale The second finding is what stops this from being a happy story for privacy.
At four-bit NF4, the fraction of memorized sequences that survive quantization is 0.39 at 160M, 0.27 at 410M, and 0.72 at 1B.
If you actually need to remove memorized data, quantization is not the tool; targeted unlearning or a training-time fix is, and even unlearning can be undone by a later quantization step ( Zhang et al., 2024 ) .
This lines up with the finding that quantization can restore unlearned knowledge ( Zhang et al., 2024 ) , and it suggests memorization and generalization are, at least in part, physically separable in the weights.
Our largest model is 1B, well below deployment scale, so the scale trend is an extrapolation, not a measurement at the sizes that matter most.
We study English text, weight-only post-training quantization, and strict greedy extraction; sampling-based and probabilistic extraction ( Hayes et al., 2024 ) , activation quantization, and quantization-aware training may behave differently.
6 Conclusion We measured what quantization does to the training data a language model has memorized, using real models and ground-truth memorized sequences rather than proxies.

LLM.int8(): 8-bit matrix multiplication for transformers at scale.
How quantization impacts privacy risk on LLMs for code? arXiv:2508.00128 , 2025.

D Sample Trajectories License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2607.27275v1 [cs.LG] 29 Jul 2026 Flat Score, Amplified Failures: How the Error Budget Masks Damage in Quantized LLM Agents Jiwon Jang Kisu Yang Heuiseok Lim \corresponding Hyunwoo Park \corresponding Abstract Post-training quantization to 4-bit weights is widely reported to be nearly lossless.
On τ 2 \tau^{2} -bench, across two open-weight model families in dense and MoE variants and two domains (eight cells, 456 episodes each, at 16-, 8-, and 4-bit weights), quantization indeed looks free on the standard metric.
Quantization amplifies the failure the model already exhibits at full precision (tool-name hallucination in telecom, with the same directional trend in retail entity errors) by up to 2.5 × 2.5\times in volume ( + 17.6 +17.6 points per task), while creating essentially no new failures.
The failure set is the same at every precision (rank correlation ≥ 0.94 \geq 0.94 , 0.18 % 0.18\% novel events).
Shrinking the budget to two errors re-exposes a score gap of 17 17 points, and it does so only in the one cell where quantization added error volume, exactly as the masking account predicts.
1 VAIV Company 2 Seoul National University 3 Korea University Introduction Figure 1: Flat score, amplified failures.
We run the same agent benchmark, τ 2 \tau^{2} -bench telecom with 456 episodes, using a BF16 agent and its AWQ-INT4-quantized version.
The cell shown is Gemma-4-31B. (a) On the benchmark’s headline metric, the final task score, the two agents are statistically equivalent.
Quantization looks free. (b) Underneath, the share of the agent’s tool calls that invoke a tool outside its tool list doubles, from 19.5 % 19.5\% to 38.3 % 38.3\% . (c) These are not new failures.
Every tool the BF16 agent already invented is called about 2.5 × 2.5\times more often after quantization, and only 0.18 % 0.18\% of the INT4 agent’s hallucinated calls name a tool its BF16 original never used. (d) The final score stays flat because the benchmark tolerates ten failed calls per episode and the agent recovers between failures.
Models without a pre-existing failure tendency are not amplified at all, as Table 1 shows.
Reducing weights from 16 to 4 bits cuts memory and latency several-fold, and systematic evaluations report 4-bit quantization as nearly lossless, degrading standard benchmark accuracy by only a point or two ( Frantar et al.
RQ1: Does “nearly lossless” survive the shift to multi-turn tool-calling agents, and can the benchmark’s final score tell?
2026 ) , a tool-calling benchmark with a simulated user, across two open-weight model families × \times {dense, MoE} × \times two domains (the full eight-cell grid, 456 simulations per arm), plus two earlier Qwen-3.5 checkpoints in telecom.
In telecom, where the channel is tool-name hallucination (calls outside the agent’s tool list, nearly always real tools on the user’s side of the dual-control interface), the BF16 and INT4 distributions over hallucinated names are nearly identical, and the invariance holds at 8 bits.
RQ2: Why does the final score stay flat while the agent’s per-step failures multiply?
Because the loop hides the damage. τ 2 \tau^{2} -bench, like other agentic benchmarks, allows ten failed tool calls per episode, and per-error recovery is equally reliable at both precisions, so the budget absorbs the extra failures quantization produces.
Tightening the tolerance from ten errors to two re-exposes the deficit, growing the INT4 score gap from 1.3 1.3 to 16.7 16.7 points, and it re-exposes damage only in the one cell where quantization added error volume ( 15.4 15.4 points there, at most 0.2 0.2 elsewhere).
RQ3: Why are some models damaged by quantization while others are unaffected?
End-of-episode task reward is not merely low-powered but actively masked by the environment’s error budget; stopping at task success yields a false “all-clear.” • Quantization amplifies the existing failure set (RQ1). (i) The failure set is precision-invariant across 16, 8, and 4 bits ( 0.97 0.97 correlation, 0.18 % 0.18\% novel events), ruling out the “some models are just worse” confound; (ii) the failure volume multiplier is cell-dependent, 1 × 1\times to 4.6 × 4.6\times , so damage claims must be made per cell. • The error budget masks the damage, falsifiably (RQ2).
A counterfactual budget analysis shows the recovery slack hides the damage; its advance prediction, that tightening re-exposes damage only in cells with added error volume, holds across all cells (one adds volume, nine check the converse), widening 15.4 15.4 points there versus at most 0.2 0.2 elsewhere; and a reflexive error-repair system, run for five telecom models at all three precisions, recovers score only in the damaged cell. • Full-precision propensity gates the risk (RQ3).
Both come from logs benchmarks already collect; we suggest reporting them alongside task reward, and low-slack deployments face more 4-bit risk than single-turn benchmarks show.
2022 ) has become the default mechanism for serving large language models, and systematic evaluations consistently report that 4-bit quantization leaves aggregate benchmark accuracy largely intact ( Li et al.
2026 ) , yet the benchmarks ship with generous per-episode error tolerances allowing retries.
Closest to our setting, Dong et al. (2025) evaluate quantized and pruned models on agentic capability suites and report capability-level degradation patterns, and Paramanayakam et al. (2025) document performance drops when quantized models serve as on-device function-calling agents.
We evaluate on τ 2 \tau^{2} -bench ( Barres et al.
2026 ) , a tool-calling benchmark in which an agent resolves a customer request over a multi-turn conversation with a simulated user, issuing tool calls against a stateful environment.
The domains differ in horizon and tool inventory (telecom trajectories are longer, median ∼ \sim 16 versus ∼ \sim 11 agent turns, with a larger, less standard tool set) and, as we show, expose different failure channels.
Each domain contains 114 tasks; an episode ends when the user closes the conversation, when the agent exhausts max_errors = 10 {=}10 failed tool calls, or at max_steps = 300 {=}300 .
We study two open-weight LLM families, each in a dense and a mixture-of-experts (MoE) variant, namely Gemma-4-31B (dense) and Gemma-4-26B-A4B (MoE, 4B active parameters), and Qwen-3.6-27B (dense) and Qwen-3.6-35B-A3B (MoE, 3B active parameters), plus Qwen-3.5-27B and Qwen-3.5-9B (both dense) as family-version and size extensions.
Each model runs at three precision arms: BF16 , the original 16-bit weights and full-precision reference; FP8 ; and INT4 , quantized with AWQ.
All agents are served with vLLM on NVIDIA A100 GPUs; the A100 has no native FP8 compute, so the FP8 weights run weight-only via the Marlin kernel (W8A16).
Recipes differ by family (AWQ-style with g = 32 g{=}32 for Gemma INT4, zero-point AWQ with g = 128 g{=}128 for Qwen INT4).

To isolate the effect of agent quantization, the simulated user is held fixed at full precision (Qwen-3.6-27B, BF16) across all conditions; only the agent’s precision varies.
Unless noted, comparisons are BF16 versus INT4 within a fixed model and domain, holding family, architecture, task distribution, and user simulator constant.
For each metric we report the change from BF16 to INT4 with a 95% confidence interval from 5,000 5{,}000 bootstrap resamples.
For the mechanism analysis we compute, within one model × \times domain, the BF16/INT4 correlation of hallucinated-name distributions, the fraction of INT4 events unseen at BF16, and the per-task BF16/INT4 rate relationship.
For the mitigation study, five telecom models (the grid’s four plus Qwen-3.5-27B) are additionally run at every precision with a reflexive repair loop: on a failed tool call, the agent is prompted to diagnose the error message and re-issue a corrected call, up to three attempts.
The projection is conservative (an episode exceeding K K counts as a failure even if it finished earlier) but applied identically to both precisions, so the gap S BF16 ​ ( K ) − S INT4 ​ ( K ) S_{\text{BF16}}(K)-S_{\text{INT4}}(K) is a fair comparison; too_many_errors terminations occur at 10–11 counted errors, confirming K K matches max_errors .
Results Final Task Reward Is Blind to Quantization Table 1 (left) and Figure 1 a report the change in the final score from 16- to 4-bit weights.
The Damage Is Real, in the Process, and Channel-Specific When we decompose trajectories into the two failure channels (Table 1 , right, and Figure 1 b), a large and systematic effect emerges.
In telecom , whose active channel is tool-name hallucination, 4-bit quantization raises the Gemma-4 dense hallucination rate from 19.5 % 19.5\% to 38.3 % 38.3\% of the agent’s tool calls ( + 18.8 {+}18.8 points, CI [ 14.0 , 23.6 ] [14.0,23.6] ), a 2.0 × 2.0\times increase, and drives a 9 × 9\times rise in error-budget-exhausting terminations ( → 46 5\!\to\!46 of 456 episodes).
Because INT4 trajectories also contain more calls per episode ( → 9.4 7.3\!\to\!9.4 ), the 2.0 × 2.0\times rate ratio is a 2.5 × 2.5\times growth in event volume ( → 1,646 649\!\to\!1{,}646 ), the multiplier we quote.
Whichever failure the domain makes available, quantization pushes on it in Gemma-4.
The Cliff Is (Mostly) at Four Bits Sweeping the intermediate precision shows that 8 bits, weight-only in our serving setup, is close to free.
No cell degrades significantly in score (one nominally improves by 9.7 9.7 points; with ten cells tested, one excursion is unsurprising) and no telecom channel moves at FP8; the jump to 38.3 % 38.3\% happens entirely between 8 and 4 bits.
Gemma-4 MoE retail already shows its full INT4-sized entity/argument movement at FP8 ( + 1.40 {+}1.40 , CI [ − 0.2 , 3.0 ] [-0.2,3.0] , not significant under clustered intervals).
Figure 2: Quantization amplifies the existing failure set within a single model and domain (Gemma-4-31B, telecom). (a) For each tool name the model invents at BF16, its amplification factor, INT4 count over BF16 count, versus how often it was already invented.
Only 3 of the 1,646 1{,}646 INT4 events ( 0.18 % 0.18\% ) name a tool never invented at BF16 (not shown); the BF16/INT4 frequency distributions correlate at 0.97 0.97 . (b) Per-task increase in hallucination rate versus the task’s BF16 baseline (114 tasks), a flat cloud around + 17.6 {+}17.6 points with least-squares slope − 0.06 ≈ 0 {-}0.06\approx 0 , so amplification is additive across tasks rather than proportional to baseline.
At the level of individual tool names, quantization is almost purely multiplicative (Figure 2 a).
The per-tool frequency distributions at BF16 and INT4 correlate at 0.97 0.97 , only 3 of the 1,646 1{,}646 INT4 hallucinations ( 0.18 % 0.18\% ) invoke a tool that was never invented at BF16, and the top-15 fabricated tools at BF16 are all present at INT4, invoked 2.5 × 2.5\times more often ( → 1,646 649\!\rightarrow\!1{,}646 total).
Of the 24 distinct hallucinated names, 23 are real tools on the user’s side of the dual-control interface ( toggle_airplane_mode , run_speed_test , …; the 24th is a near-miss of the user-side check_sim_status ), covering 99.8 % 99.8\% of BF16 and 100 % 100\% of INT4 events.
Regressing the per-task increase on the task’s full-precision rate gives a slope of − 0.06 {-}0.06 , indistinguishable from zero.
The propensity signal is therefore strongest as a gate , whether the model opens this failure mode at all (649 baseline events in Gemma-4 telecom, 9 in Qwen-3.6 telecom, 0 in the retail tool-name channel), rather than as fine per-task proportionality.

Quantization Stability Temperature Scaling Under Sum Scoring Template Sensitivity Per-Class Performance Failure of Single-Token Class-Code Scoring Discussion Scoring Protocol and Calibration Sources of Variation Implications for Evaluation Practice Limitations and Future Work Conclusion References License: CC BY 4.0 arXiv:2608.03854v4 [cs.LG] 20 Sep 2026 When Calibration Depends on the Scoring Rule: Quantized Biomedical LLM Classification Anton Rasmussen Hong Qin Abstract Quantized large language models can run on consumer hardware, which motivates interest in on-premises processing of sensitive data.
We evaluate three 7-billion-parameter Mistral variants (a base model, BioMistral, and an instruction-tuned checkpoint evaluated without its chat template) at FP16, INT8, and INT4 on five-class sentence classification in medical abstracts.
Accuracy changes by at most 1.4 percentage points for these two checkpoints.
Between them, prompt choice changes mean accuracy across precisions by 2.9 to 17.8 percentage points, depending on the model.
Eight-bit quantization changes accuracy by at most 1.1 percentage points for the adapted checkpoints; four-bit quantization shows mixed but non-catastrophic effects.
Automated sentence classification, which assigns functional roles such as Background , Objective , Methods , Results , and Conclusions to sentences within medical abstracts, can accelerate evidence synthesis by structuring unstructured literature at scale ( Dernoncourt and Lee 2017 ) .
2022 ) roughly halve inference-time weight memory relative to FP16, making single-GPU inference practical for many 7-billion-parameter models.
2022 ) , but recent studies find that post-training and the chat templates that accompany it both degrade probability calibration ( Huang et al.
We observe that switching from summed to mean-token log-likelihood scoring reverses which of two checkpoints, BioMistral-7B and Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, appears better calibrated on PubMed RCT before any post-hoc calibration, while accuracy remains largely unchanged.
Between the two primary templates, evaluated on the same test sample, mean accuracy over the three precisions differs by ∼ {\sim} 3pp for Instruct, ∼ {\sim} 7pp for BioMistral, and up to ∼ {\sim} 18pp for the base model, and the template determines which model achieves the highest accuracy.
For the adapted checkpoints (BioMistral, Instruct), INT8 quantization changes accuracy by at most 1.1pp relative to FP16.
INT4 (NF4) shows heterogeneous effects that vary by model and template, without catastrophic degradation in the configurations tested.
Guo et al. (2017) demonstrated that temperature scaling is a simple and effective post-hoc calibration method.
For pretrained transformers, calibration varies across tasks and distribution shifts, and temperature scaling can improve in-domain calibration ( Desai and Durrett 2020 ) .
For RLHF-tuned LLMs, Tian et al. (2023) find that verbalized confidences are typically better calibrated than conditional token probabilities.
Huang et al. (2026) show that instruction tuning can raise confidence without commensurate accuracy gains, worsening calibration across multiple languages.
Sanz-Guerrero, Mager, and von der Wense (2026) decouple post-training from chat templates, finding that templates independently aggravate miscalibration via an “ownership bias” (up to 26% higher confidence on self-generated answers).
Cho, So, and Lee (2026) demonstrate that raw and length-normalized likelihood scoring remain sensitive to superficial answer-choice content and input format, calling into question standard LLM evaluation protocols.
Zhao et al. (2021) show that few-shot prompting introduces strong recency, majority-label, and common-token biases into LLM predictions, proposing content-free calibration to mitigate them.
2024 ) continues pretraining of Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.1 on PubMed Central articles, achieving competitive results on biomedical benchmarks.
Labrak et al. (2024) include quantized variant evaluation and some calibration analysis, but do not vary the scoring rule or verbalizer.
2022 ) introduced mixed-precision INT8 decomposition that preserves outlier features in FP16, achieving near-lossless compression.
2023 ) introduced NF4 quantization with double quantization for efficient fine-tuning.
2022 ) , calibration effects have received less attention.
Zhong et al. (2025) study calibration in quantized LLMs across multiple models and datasets, finding that quantization generally worsens calibration and proposing a soft-prompt recovery framework.
2025 ) extends this paradigm to general program optimization, using LLM-proposed code changes and automated evaluation metrics.
Methods Task and Dataset We evaluate on PubMed RCT sentence classification ( Dernoncourt and Lee 2017 ) , a 5-class task assigning functional roles ( Background , Objective , Methods , Results , Conclusions ) to sentences from medical abstracts.
We use a stratified sample of n = 2,000 n=2{,}000 sentences from 1,362 1{,}362 abstracts in the test set for the main evaluation and, for temperature scaling, n = 200 n=200 validation-set examples for fitting plus a separate n = 200 n=200 test sample for evaluation.
This choice isolates the template-and-scoring variables under study, but means that Instruct is evaluated outside its intended input format, which may independently affect calibration ( Sanz-Guerrero, Mager, and von der Wense 2026 ) .
Because label names have unequal token counts ( T ℓ = 3 T_{\ell}=3 to 5 5 ), we also evaluate a length-normalized variant: score mean ( ℓ ) = 1 T ℓ ∑ t = 1 T ℓ log P ( w t ( ℓ ) | prompt , w < t ( ℓ ) ) . \text{score}_{\text{mean}}(\ell)=\frac{1}{T_{\ell}}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\ell}}\log P\!\left(w_{t}^{(\ell)}\,\middle|\,\text{prompt},w_{<t}^{(\ell)}\right).
Evolutionary Prompt Optimization A zero-shot baseline prompt collapsed to a single predicted label on a 100-example selection subset, which motivated automated prompt search.
2025 ) , an LLM-driven program-evolution framework, to run a small evolutionary search that started from this baseline prompt.
We scored each candidate with BioMistral-7B at FP16 on the 100 labeled PubMed RCT examples using our own multi-objective evaluator, and these scores guided selection.
The search evaluated 29 candidates over ∼ {\sim} 3 hours on one H100 GPU.
FP16 serves as the half-precision baseline, requiring approximately 14 GB of weight storage for a 7B model, excluding runtime overhead.
INT8 uses LLM.int8() mixed-precision decomposition ( Dettmers et al.
2022 ) via bitsandbytes ; outlier features remain in FP16, while the remaining weights are quantized to 8-bit integers, roughly halving weight storage relative to FP16.
INT4 uses NF4 (4-bit NormalFloat) quantization with double quantization ( Dettmers et al.
2017 ) , fitting a scalar temperature T T on n = 200 n=200 validation-set examples (seed 42) to minimize NLL, then applying the fitted temperature to n = 200 n=200 held-out test examples: P cal ​ ( ℓ ) = softmax ​ ( score ​ ( ℓ ) T ) P_{\text{cal}}(\ell)=\text{softmax}\!\left(\frac{\text{score}(\ell)}{T}\right) A fitted T > 1 T>1 indicates that the induced distribution is more peaked than warranted by accuracy under the selected scorer; T ≈ 1 T\approx 1 indicates the scores are already well-scaled.
Metrics We evaluate classification performance using accuracy and macro-F1, and calibration using Expected Calibration Error (ECE; 15 equal-width bins), the Brier score, and negative log-likelihood (NLL).
We computed bootstrap confidence intervals only for the paired accuracy and macro-F1 differences reported in the text.
