License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2608.29667v1 [cs.LG] 30 Aug 2026 A Target-Centric Survey of Quantization-Aware Training Jiamin Song † † thanks: Equal contribution Mengjie Zhao 1 1 footnotemark: 1 Zijing Wang Yongkang Liu † † thanks: Corresponding authors Qian Li Shi Feng Feiliang Ren Daling Wang Hinrich Schütze Affiliation: Northeastern University, China Affiliation: Shandong University, China Affiliation: CIS, LMU Munich; MCML, Germany Abstract The rapid development of LLMs incurs prohibitive memory footprints and intensive computational demands.
1 Introduction Recent years have witnessed rapid development progress of large-scale foundation models capable of communicating across different languages, understanding diverse data modalities, and generating multimedia assets.
The rapidly growing scale of these models demands intensive memory and computational resources far exceeding the capacity of most current edge devices, sometimes even consumer-grade GPUs ( Nagel et al., 2021 ; Wei et al., 2024 ; Yang et al., 2025 ; Liu et al., 2025a ) .
To mitigate these resource bottlenecks, model quantization serves as a fundamental technique, compressing the size of these massive models by lowering the bit-width of their parameters ( Jacob et al., 2018 ; Nagel et al., 2021 ; Wei et al., 2024 ) .
Model quantization methods can be broadly divided into Post-training quantization ( PTQ ) and Quantization-aware training ( QAT ; Nagel et al. (2021) ; Wei et al. (2024) ; Yang et al. (2025) ).
PTQ applies low-precision quantization to a pre-trained model without further training to reduce model size and improve inference efficiency ( Xiao et al., 2023 ; Liu et al., 2024b ; Zhang and Shrivastava, 2024 ) .
For instance, Bondarenko et al. (2021) identified that activations in residual connections contain structured outliers with high dynamic ranges, which are difficult to represent in low-bit formats.
In contrast, QAT simulates quantization during training using fake quantization modules, optimizing the model directly under quantized inference constraints, and often recovering the accuracy loss of PTQ, especially at ultra-low bit-widths such as INT4 ( Jacob et al., 2018 ; Bengio et al., 2013 ) .
Recent QAT methods further combine quantization with parameter-efficient adaptation or distillation, making them increasingly applicable to large-scale LLMs ( Xu et al., 2024 ; Chen et al., 2025 ) .
Table 1 summarizes the scope and taxonomy of representative quantization surveys and highlights how our target-centric, QAT-focused perspective differs from prior work.
Survey Scope Taxonomy Key difference from ours Nagel et al. (2021) Practical neural network quantization for efficient inference.
Wei et al. (2024) General review of neural network quantization.
Yang et al. (2025) Quantization for CNN/RNN and Transformer-based models.
Table 1: Comparison with representative surveys on neural network quantization.
In this work, we firstly establish theoretical foundations of QAT, focusing on the mathematical formulation of uniform quantization, the fake quantization mechanism, and gradient approximation techniques such as the Straight-Through Estimator (§ 2 ).
Next, we organize QAT methods by quantization target, covering weights, activations, W+A, KV cache, and gradients, and synthesize their cross-target differences and strategy transferability (§ 3 ).
We also propose a minimal reporting checklist, with detailed evaluation paradigms, metrics in Appendix D , and representative quantitative evidence provided in Appendix E (§ 4 ).
2 Preliminary Knowledge 2.1 Uniform Affine Quantization (UAQ) Quantization maps high-precision (e.g., FP32) real-valued tensors to a low-precision discrete set that is more amenable to efficient integer arithmetic and reduced memory usage.
While FP32 offers high precision and a large dynamic range, lower-precision formats such as INT8 and INT4 significantly reduce memory footprint and computation cost, at the expense of potential information loss.
UAQ is parameterized by a positive step size s > 0 s>0 , an integer zero-point z ∈ ℤ z\in\mathbb{Z} , and an integer representable range [ q min , q max ] [q_{\min},q_{\max}] .
The quantization operator first maps x x to an integer tensor x int ∈ ℤ d 1 × ⋯ × d k x_{\mathrm{int}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{k}} : x int = clamp ⁡ ( ⌊ x s ⌉ + z , q min , q max ) , x_{\mathrm{int}}=\mathrm{clamp}\!\left(\left\lfloor\frac{x}{s}\right\rceil+z,\;q_{\min},\;q_{\max}\right), (1) where ⌊ ⋅ ⌉ \lfloor\cdot\rceil denotes deterministic rounding to the nearest integer, and clamp ⁡ ( ⋅ , q min , q max ) \mathrm{clamp}(\cdot,q_{\min},q_{\max}) clips values to the integer range ( Jacob et al., 2018 ) .
During QAT, computation is usually still performed in floating point, while low-precision effects are emulated through a quantize–dequantize (i.e., fake-quantization) operator: x ^ = s ⋅ ( x int − z ) , \hat{x}=s\cdot(x_{\mathrm{int}}-z), (2) where x ^ \hat{x} is the fake-quantized proxy of x x used in the forward pass.
Symmetric quantization sets z = 0 z=0 , while asymmetric quantization allows z ≠ 0 z\neq 0 .
We detail derivations of quantization parameters in Appendix B , and Appendix B.1 shows detailed derivations of ( s , z ) (s,z) from a chosen clipping range.
E.g., per-tensor quantization uses one pair for an entire tensor while per-channel quantization is more fine-grained, targeting specific dimensions of a weight tensor ( Nagel et al., 2021 ) .
2.2 QAT and Gradient Approximation Quantization-aware training (QAT) incorporates quantization effects during training by inserting quantization operators into the training graph ( Jacob et al., 2018 ; Nagel et al., 2021 ) .
For a layer that computes y = f ⁡ ( W ​ x + b ) y=f(Wx+b) , QAT instead computes W ^ = 𝒬 ( W ) , x ^ = 𝒬 ( x ) , y = f ⁡ ( W ^ ​ x ^ + b ) , \displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{W}&=\mathcal{Q}(W),\qquad\hat{x}=\mathcal{Q}(x),\\ y&=f(\hat{W}\hat{x}+b),\end{split} (3) where 𝒬 ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathcal{Q}(\cdot) denotes the quantize–dequantize operator in Eqs.
The derivative of rounding is zero almost everywhere and undefined at integer thresholds: ∂ ⌊ u ⌉ ∂ u = 0 \frac{\partial\lfloor u\rceil}{\partial u}=0 , a.e. so naive backpropagation through the quantization path would produce vanishing or ill-defined gradients, preventing effective optimization of the underlying full-precision parameters ( Nagel et al., 2022 ) .
3 Taxonomy Existing surveys often treat QAT as a component of general neural-network quantization or organize methods primarily by architecture ( Nagel et al., 2021 ; Wei et al., 2024 ; Yang et al., 2025 ) .
Throughout this survey, we use W n A m to denote n-bit weight and m-bit activation quantization (e.g., W4A4 refers to 4-bit weights and 4-bit activations).
Figure 1: Taxonomy of QAT methods, organized by quantization target (rows) and model family (columns).
3.1 Quantization Target: Model Weights This section focuses on works applying QAT primarily to model weights.
Early weight-QAT methods established the feasibility of low-bit training but exposed trade-offs between compression and stability ( Zhou et al., 2016 ) .
Binary quantization ( Rastegari et al., 2016 ) offers maximal compression at the cost of larger accuracy loss, while ternary methods ( Li et al., 2016 ) improve representational flexibility by adding a zero state.
At very low bit-widths, the mismatch between continuous weight updates and discrete quantization levels can cause weights near thresholds to repeatedly flip between adjacent values, leading to oscillation and unstable convergence ( Nagel et al., 2022 ) .
This problem is further amplified in low-bit ViTs by learnable scaling factors and coupled Query/Key weight quantization ( Liu et al., 2023 ) .
Existing methods mitigate such instability through oscillation dampening and iterative weight freezing ( Nagel et al., 2022 ) , ViT-specific designs including statistical weight quantization, confidence-guided annealing, and Query-Key reparameterization ( Liu et al., 2023 ) , as well as stronger supervision or staged training for ternary and binary language models ( Zhang et al., 2020 ; Bai et al., 2021 ) .
In ultra-low-bit weight QAT, (e.g., below 3 bits), the central challenge is to preserve model capacity under a highly constrained parameter space.
Different low-bit regimes require different quantization functions and training strategies, rather than a uniform treatment of all weights ( Liu et al., 2025b ) .
Preserving a small subset of salient weights in higher precision ( Yuan et al., 2024 ) , using Hessian-based group-wise quantization, and adopting mixed-precision allocation ( Shen et al., 2019 ) can all reduce the degradation caused by uniform low-bit quantization.
EfficientQAT ( Chen et al., 2025 ) expands the optimization space through staged training and then updates only quantization parameters, while DL-QAT ( Ke et al., 2024 ) formulates QAT as low-rank adaptation in a decomposed quantization space.
LoRA-style methods further reduce QAT cost by coupling quantization with low-rank adaptation: L4Q ( Jeon et al., 2025 ) achieves LoRA-like training cost while producing a fully quantized final model, QA-LoRA ( Xu et al., 2024 ) improves quantization flexibility through group-wise operators and supports lossless adapter merging, and LoftQ ( Li et al., 2024 ) reduces quantization error through joint backbone quantization and low-rank initialization, including experiments with non-uniform NF2/NF4 formats.
QEFT ( Lee et al., 2024 ) bridges these two directions by using sensitivity-aware mixed precision and hardware-friendly reordering to preserve important weight structures while improving the efficiency of quantized fine-tuning.
Binary Transformer pre-training shows that discrete weight parameterization can be used from the beginning of training ( Wang et al., 2023 ) , while 1.58-bit ternary training provides a middle ground between binary compression and full-precision expressivity ( Ma et al., 2024 ) .
The timing of quantization is also important: native 1.58-bit training remains possible at smaller scales but may need architectural or capacity adjustments ( Nielsen and Schneider-Kamp, 2024 ) , and continual quantization-aware pre-training treats the switch from high precision to 1.58-bit as a key design choice ( Nielsen et al., 2025 ) .
3.2 Quantization Target: Activations Activation-centric QAT quantizes intermediates produced in the forward pass, e.g, hidden states, attention outputs, and residual streams.
Activation quantization is sensitive to distribution shift ( Jacob et al., 2018 ; Liu et al., 2024a ) , and is central to integer-only inference and memory-efficient long-sequence processing.
The Transformer architecture further challenges quantization by its structured activation outliers: a small number of tokens dominate the dynamic range, forcing most values into poorly resolved quantization intervals ( Bondarenko et al., 2021 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ) .
At very low bit-widths, gradient mismatch between the training surrogate and the inference-time discrete operator becomes an additional bottleneck ( Kim et al., 2020 ) .
Existing methods address the challenges from two directions: (1) making the quantizer adaptive.
1 ), learnable clipping thresholds, step sizes, offsets, or asymmetric ranges are optimized so that the quantization grid tracks shifting activation distributions ( Choi et al., 2018 ; Esser et al., 2020 ; Bhalgat et al., 2020 ) ; (2) reducing the mismatch between smooth training surrogates and discrete inference operators via training–inference decoupling and smoother surrogate activations ( Kim et al., 2020 ) .
3.3 Quantization Target: Weight + Activation Weights and activations (and other intermediates) can also be jointly quantized ( WA-QAT ) during training, making the simulated low-precision training graph closer to the inference graph during deployment ( Jacob et al., 2018 ; Nagel et al., 2021 ) .
WA-QAT introduces coupled distortion: quantized weights reshape downstream activation distributions, activation clipping affects backward updates, and integer deployment imposes operator- and accumulator-aware constraints ( Bondarenko et al., 2021 ; Colbert et al., 2023 ) .

On Mixtral-8x7B-Instruct, Qwen1.5-MoE-A2.7B, and DeepSeek-V2-Lite, Q-Strata consistently achieves lower WikiText2 perplexity than uniform-bitwidth GPTQ and the state-of-the-art MoE MPQ methods MxMoE and GEMQ in the low-bit regime.
Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models such as Mixtral, DeepSeek-V3, and Qwen3 have become a leading approach for scaling large language models (LLMs) across diverse tasks ( Jiang et al., 2024 ; DeepSeek-AI et al., 2025 ; Yang et al., 2025 ) .
In particular, we focus on weight-only post-training quantization (PTQ), which compresses the weights of a trained model with only a small calibration dataset and no retraining, reflecting practical deployment constraints ( Frantar et al., 2023 ; Hubara et al., 2021 ) .
Mixed-precision quantization (MPQ) assigns different bitwidths to the linear layers of a model to minimize the quality lost under a fixed budget, pushing the size-quality frontier of dense models beyond uniform quantization ( Lee and Song, 2025 ) .
One approximates this loss as a linear sum of layer-wise proxy terms, typically from a Hessian approximation, and solves the allocation with integer linear programming ( Dong et al., 2020 ; Chen et al., 2021 ) .
The other optimizes a model-level objective, evaluated on the assembled quantized model, in a black-box manner with model-based evolutionary search or reinforcement learning ( Lee et al., 2025 ; Wang et al., 2020 ; Wang et al., 2019 ) .
Each of the L L MoE blocks holds E E experts, and each expert contains three linear layers (gate, up, and down), so the MoE blocks alone hold 3 ​ L ​ E 3LE linear layers, inducing a search space of | 𝒬 | 3 ​ L ​ E |\mathcal{Q}|^{3LE} assignments over the | 𝒬 | |\mathcal{Q}| candidate quantizers, far more than in a dense model.
For instance, within the Qwen3 family, the dense Qwen3-32B has 7 × 64 = 448 7\times 64=448 linear layers, whereas the similarly sized Qwen3-30B-A3B has 3 × 48 × 128 = 18,432 3\times 48\times 128=18{,}432 linear layers in its MoE blocks alone, more than forty times as many.
1 1 1 The dense count includes all seven linear layers per block, whereas the MoE count includes only the three projections (up, gate, down) of each of the 128 128 experts across the 48 48 blocks, excluding attention and routing.
MxMoE and MC-MoE instead use proxy scores to capture the differing contributions of the experts and linear layers within each MoE block, which improves over uniform quantization, but they keep every block’s budget uniform and do not allocate across blocks ( Duanmu et al., 2025 ; Huang et al., 2025 ) .
GEMQ does allocate different budgets across blocks through a global assignment, but it still relies on an additive proxy for model quality, so it misses the dependencies that quantization creates between blocks ( Deng et al., 2026 ) .
To optimize this model-level objective directly without searching the 3 ​ L ​ E 3LE -dimensional assignment space, we propose Q-Strata , a bi-level allocator that splits the allocation along these two strata ( Figure 1 ).
Its inner stage caches a Pareto frontier of candidates per block over finely spaced budgets, and its outer stage selects one cached candidate per block to minimize the model-level objective under the global budget, operating over only L L per-block budgets rather than 3 ​ L ​ E 3LE individual bitwidths.
We solve this outer selection with a greedy descent that starts from the most expensive budgets and repeatedly lowers the budget of the block whose reduction raises the objective least, made affordable by a lazy evaluation scheme that recomputes only a few marginals per step ( Minoux, 1978 ) .
Across Mixtral-8 × \times 7B-Instruct ( Jiang et al., 2024 ) , Qwen1.5-MoE-A2.7B ( Qwen Team, 2024 ) , DeepSeek-V2-Lite ( DeepSeek-AI et al., 2024 ) , and Qwen3-30B-A3B ( Yang et al., 2025 ) , Q-Strata achieves the lowest WikiText2 perplexity among the compared methods in the low-bit regime, and its outer search also serves as a standalone allocator on dense models.
2 Preliminaries 2.1 Problem formulation We study weight-only mixed-precision quantization (MPQ) of a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with L L MoE blocks indexed by l ∈ [ L ] l\in[L] , where [ n ] = { 1 , … , n } [n]=\{1,\dots,n\} .
These expert linear layers, N = 3 ​ L ​ E N=3LE in total, are the atomic units of bit allocation.
The attention projections, the routing gate, and the embeddings hold a small fraction of the parameters and are kept at a fixed precision, so we allocate bits only over the expert linear layers ( Duanmu et al., 2025 ) .
MPQ minimizes ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}) at a target average bitwidth τ \tau , minimize 𝐱 ∈ 𝒳 \displaystyle\operatorname*{minimize}_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}} ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}) (1) subject ​ to \displaystyle~\mathrm{subject~to} b ¯ ​ ( 𝐱 ) ≤ τ . \displaystyle\bar{b}(\mathbf{x})\leq\tau. ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x}) is a model-level objective that scores the quantized model on the calibration data.
Following the protocol of Lee et al. (2025) , we use the Jensen–Shannon divergence (JSD) between the next-token distributions of the quantized and full-precision models, where a lower value indicates higher quality.
This optimization is especially hard for MoE models, since the dimension of the assignment space, N = 3 ​ L ​ E N=3LE , scales with the number of experts rather than with model size or compute.
2.2 Block reconstruction proxy For the allocation inside a single MoE block, prior MoE quantization methods rely on the block-output reconstruction error ( Duanmu et al., 2025 ; Huang et al., 2025 ) .
The reconstruction error ∥ h ^ l ​ ( 𝐱 l ) − h l ∥ 2 \lVert\hat{h}_{l}(\mathbf{x}_{l})-h_{l}\rVert^{2} depends only on the local assignment 𝐱 l \mathbf{x}_{l} , but the gating nonlinearity and routing couple the 3 ​ E 3E linear layers, so it is not additive across them.
To make it decomposable, these methods approximate it by a proxy that sums the block-output distortion each linear layer induces in isolation, 𝒟 l ​ ( 𝐱 l ) = ∑ e ∈ [ E ] ∑ i ∈ ℐ ∥ h ^ l ( e , i ) ​ ( x l , e , i ) − h l ∥ 2 , \mathcal{D}_{l}(\mathbf{x}_{l})=\sum_{e\in[E]}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\big\lVert\hat{h}_{l}^{(e,i)}(x_{l,e,i})-h_{l}\big\rVert^{2}, where h ^ l ( e , i ) ​ ( q ) \hat{h}_{l}^{(e,i)}(q) is the block output when only linear layer ( l , e , i ) (l,e,i) is quantized with quantizer q q and the rest of the block is kept at full precision.
Because the proxy decomposes over the layers, this is a multiple-choice knapsack problem (MCKP), in which the 3 ​ E 3E linear layers are the choice groups and the quantizers in 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} are the items, and choosing a quantizer for a layer contributes its bitwidth to the cost and the block-output distortion it induces to the objective.
The distortion terms ∥ h ^ l ( e , i ) ​ ( q ) − h l ∥ 2 \lVert\hat{h}_{l}^{(e,i)}(q)-h_{l}\rVert^{2} summed in 𝒟 l \mathcal{D}_{l} are precomputed once for every layer and quantizer, reusing the block inputs X l X_{l} from a single full-precision forward over the calibration set.
We solve over a shared budget grid ℬ = { β ( 1 ) < ⋯ < β ( K ) } \mathcal{B}=\{\beta^{(1)}<\dots<\beta^{(K)}\} of K K equally spaced levels with step Δ = β ( k + 1 ) − β ( k ) \Delta=\beta^{(k+1)}-\beta^{(k)} , and cache the candidates 𝒮 l = { 𝐱 l ⋆ ​ ( β ) : β ∈ ℬ } \mathcal{S}_{l}=\{\mathbf{x}_{l}^{\star}(\beta):\beta\in\mathcal{B}\} .
Caching one candidate per budget level collapses the global search from 𝒬 3 ​ L ​ E \mathcal{Q}^{3LE} to a per-block budget choice 𝜷 ∈ ℬ L \boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}^{L} .
Choosing a budget β l ∈ ℬ \beta_{l}\in\mathcal{B} for each block yields 𝐱 ⁡ ( 𝜷 ) = ( 𝐱 1 ⋆ ​ ( β 1 ) , … , 𝐱 L ⋆ ​ ( β L ) ) \mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\beta})=(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{\star}(\beta_{1}),\dots,\mathbf{x}_{L}^{\star}(\beta_{L})) , and we solve 𝜷 ⋆ \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}~~ = argmin 𝜷 ∈ ℬ L ℒ ( 𝐱 ( 𝜷 ) ) \displaystyle=~~\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}^{L}}~~~~~\mathcal{L}\big(\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\big) (3) subject ​ to 1 L ​ ∑ l = 1 L β l ≤ τ , \displaystyle~~~\mathrm{subject~to~}~~~~~\tfrac{1}{L}\textstyle\sum_{l=1}^{L}\beta_{l}\leq\tau, with 𝐱 ⋆ = 𝐱 ⁡ ( 𝜷 ⋆ ) \mathbf{x}^{\star}=\mathbf{x}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\star}) .
3.3 Solving the outer problem The outer problem is a black-box combinatorial optimization over the lattice ℬ L \mathcal{B}^{L} under a budget constraint on the sum of per-block budgets, where each evaluation of ℒ \mathcal{L} is a forward pass of the assembled model.
A budgeted greedy would weigh each move’s δ l \delta_{l} against the budget it saves ( Khuller et al., 1999 ) , but here every one-level move saves the same budget, so we lower the block with the smallest δ l \delta_{l} .
Each step evaluates ℒ \mathcal{L} on an assembled assignment, so the descent captures the coupling between blocks, but a direct implementation recomputes every δ l \delta_{l} at each of the 𝒪 ⁡ ( L ​ K ) \mathcal{O}(LK) steps, 𝒪 ⁡ ( L 2 ​ K ) \mathcal{O}(L^{2}K) evaluations of ℒ \mathcal{L} .
Our implementation runs this descent with lazy evaluation ( Minoux, 1978 ) .
Our ℒ \mathcal{L} carries no guarantee of this condition, so we use lazy evaluation as a heuristic, justified empirically by the near-monotone growth of the marginals along the descent ( Figure 2 ).
Definition 3.1 (DR-submodularity on the budget grid) .
A function G : ℬ L → ℝ G:\mathcal{B}^{L}\to\mathbb{R} is DR-submodular if, for all 𝜷 ≤ 𝜷 ′ \boldsymbol{\beta}\leq\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime} taken coordinatewise and every such block l l , G ⁡ ( 𝜷 + l ) − G ⁡ ( 𝜷 ) ≥ G ⁡ ( ( 𝜷 ′ ) + l ) − G ⁡ ( 𝜷 ′ ) , G(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{+l})-G(\boldsymbol{\beta})\ \geq\ G\big((\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime})^{+l}\big)-G(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}), so the gain from raising a block by one level does not increase as the other budgets grow ( Soma and Yoshida, 2018 ) .
When G = − ℒ G=-\mathcal{L} satisfies Definition 3.1 , the descent only lowers budgets, so a δ l \delta_{l} cached at a higher budget can only be exceeded later, exactly the underestimate condition above.
All methods share the same weight-only GPTQ quantizer set (1,2,3,4 bit, group size 128, asymmetric).
GPTQ, MxMoE, and Q-Strata use the rotated setting of Section 4.1 , while GEMQ (shared) deploys GEMQ’s allocation in its original rotation-free setting without router fine-tuning.
Section 4.4 compares against GEMQ with the router fine-tuning of its official recipe restored, with and without rotation.
† The 2.0 2.0 -bit uniform GPTQ rows use the per-channel 2-bit format with 2.0 2.0 effective bits, since no uniform group-128 configuration meets that budget.
If G = − ℒ G=-\mathcal{L} is DR-submodular ( Definition 3.1 ), this lazy descent commits the same moves as the eager descent that recomputes all L L marginals each step, while evaluating ℒ \mathcal{L} fewer times ( Minoux, 1978 ; Soma and Yoshida, 2018 ) .
Writing R R for the average number of evaluations per committed move, the lazy descent issues 𝒪 ⁡ ( R ​ L ​ K ) \mathcal{O}(RLK) , smaller than the eager 𝒪 ⁡ ( L 2 ​ K ) \mathcal{O}(L^{2}K) by a factor L / R L/R .
4 Experiments 4.1 Setup We mainly evaluate three MoE LLMs of different scales, Mixtral-8 × \times 7B-Instruct (46.7B - A12.9B), Qwen1.5-MoE (14.3B - A2.7B), and DeepSeek-V2-Lite (15.7B - A2.4B) ( Jiang et al., 2024 ; Qwen Team, 2024 ; DeepSeek-AI et al., 2024 ) , and scale the comparison up to Qwen3-30B-A3B ( Yang et al., 2025 ) in Section D.6 .
All compared methods quantize weights with GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) and share one quantizer set, the group-128 asymmetric formats at 1 1 , 2 2 , 3 3 , and 4 4 bits.
The budget grid spans 1.25 1.25 to 4.25 4.25 bits in steps of 0.125 0.125 , giving K = 25 K=25 levels.
We report WikiText2 perplexity and the average accuracy over six zero-shot tasks, PIQA, BoolQ, WinoGrande, ARC-easy, ARC-challenge, and HellaSwag ( Bisk et al., 2020 ; Clark et al., 2019 ; Sakaguchi et al., 2019 ; Clark et al., 2018 ; Zellers et al., 2019 ) .
Uniform GPTQ gives every linear layer the same bitwidth ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) .
MxMoE shares our inner stage but assigns every MoE block the same budget τ \tau rather than optimizing per-block budgets, making it the counterpart that omits our outer stage ( Duanmu et al., 2025 ) .
GEMQ allocates bitwidth globally at expert granularity, solving an ILP that minimizes a gradient-based additive proxy for model quality ( Deng et al., 2026 ) .
Uniform GPTQ, MxMoE, and Q-Strata apply the quantizers after a random Hadamard rotation without online rotation, following the protocol of Duanmu et al. (2025) ( Ashkboos et al., 2024 ) , so differences among these three reflect bit allocation alone.
GEMQ is reproduced under a shared protocol, labeled GEMQ (shared) in Table 1 , by taking its allocation from its own gradient-proxy ILP and deploying it with the shared quantizer set, keeping its original rotation-free setting and omitting its router fine-tuning (RFT) step, an additional training stage.
2 2 2 GEMQ’s original pipeline further differs from the shared protocol in its GPTQ variant (MSE range search vs. min-max), its 1-bit format (symmetric, 1.125 effective bits, vs. asymmetric, 1.25), and its evaluation context length (2048 vs. our 4096).
Section 4.4 additionally compares Q-Strata against GEMQ with the RFT of its official recipe restored, with and without rotation.
Q-Strata attains the lowest WikiText2 perplexity for every model at every bitwidth, and the best zero-shot accuracy in all cases but one , DeepSeek-V2-Lite at 2.25 2.25 bits, where MxMoE leads by 0.3 0.3 points.
At 1.75 1.75 bits on Mixtral, Q-Strata more than halves the perplexity of MxMoE, 12.14 12.14 against 25.20 25.20 , and improves average accuracy by almost eight points.
The gains also persist on the larger Qwen3-30B-A3B with 18,432 18{,}432 expert linear layers, at a one-time search cost of 23 to 89 GPU-hours per model ( Section D.6 ).

We evaluate Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct under INT8 and INT4 quantization on RGB and HotpotQA, measuring both accuracy and faithfulness with a hallucination detector, NLI entailment, and an LLM judge.
INT4 reduces accuracy and, more critically, even among answers that remain factually correct, over 90% of faithfulness changes are negative, i.e., accuracy metrics are blind to this regression.
Prior work shows KV caches can be quantized to INT8 and INT4 with little accuracy degradation ( Liu et al., 2024 ; Hooper et al., 2024 ) , though aggressive compression can harm reasoning on complex tasks ( Liu et al., 2025 ) .
Exact Match and F1 measure agreement with a reference answer; they do not verify whether the answer is grounded in the provided context.
A model can preserve the correct answer string while becoming less dependent on retrieved evidence, creating a hidden failure mode in compressed RAG systems ( Yen et al., 2025 ; Tamber et al., 2025 ) .
Figure 1 shows our controlled protocol, which isolates cache precision as the sole variable and evaluates faithfulness with three independent signals.
Second, we show that INT8 is near-lossless, while INT4 silently degrades grounding even on accuracy-preserved examples ( > > 90% of faithfulness flips are negative; McNemar p < 10 − 20 p<10^{-20} ).
Evaluate : three complementary faithfulness signals (HHEM hallucination rate ↓ \downarrow , DeBERTa-v3 NLI entailment ↑ \uparrow , LLM-as-judge ↑ \uparrow ) are measured alongside standard accuracy metrics.
KIVI ( Liu et al., 2024 ) achieves near-lossless 2-bit asymmetric quantization; KVQuant ( Hooper et al., 2024 ) reaches sub-4-bit via per-channel key quantization; ZipCache ( He et al., 2024 ) identifies token-level precision sensitivity; KVTuner ( Li et al., 2025 ) sets 4-bit as the safe floor for Qwen2.5-7B on reasoning tasks.
Sub-4-bit can cause up to 59% accuracy loss on long-context tasks ( Mekala et al., 2025 ) , prompting variance-normalized quantization ( Müller et al., 2026 ) and query-aware mixed-precision allocation ( Zhang et al., 2025 ) .
RAGChecker ( Ru et al., 2024 ) and RAGTruth ( Niu et al., 2024 ) show that EM and F1 miss systematic faithfulness failures.
HELMET ( Yen et al., 2025 ) finds standard accuracy proxies correlate poorly with downstream RAG quality.
LLM-as-judge outperforms detector-only methods for faithfulness evaluation ( Tamber et al., 2025 ; Song et al., 2025 ) .
Distractor passages cause systematic grounding failures even without compression ( Wu et al., 2025 ; Amiraz et al., 2025 ) .
3 Method Our goal is to isolate the effect of KV-cache quantization in RAG.
The method builds one position-consistent cache for the full retrieved context, quantizes it before storage, dequantizes it before decoding, and compares BF16, INT8, and INT4 under identical inference conditions.
After prefill, we quantize the KV cache before storage and dequantize it back to bfloat16 before decoding.
INT8 uses per-token asymmetric quantization ( Liu et al., 2024 ) .
INT4 uses group-wise quantization with groups of 64 channels, where each group stores its own scale and zero-point.
This reduces the risk that large-magnitude channels dominate the quantization range and collapse smaller values under INT4’s 16 levels ( Liu et al., 2024 ; Hooper et al., 2024 ) .
The on-disk footprint of one KV cache is M ⁡ ( b , g , o ) = 2 ​ L ​ H ​ d h ​ N ⋅ b 8 + 2 ​ L ​ H ​ d h ​ N g ⋅ o 8 , M(b,g,o)=2LHd_{h}N\cdot\tfrac{b}{8}+\tfrac{2LHd_{h}N}{g}\cdot\tfrac{o}{8}, (1) where L L , H H , d h d_{h} , and N N denote the number of layers, KV heads, head dimension, and cached tokens.
INT8 uses b = 8 b{=}8 and g = d h g{=}d_{h} , while INT4 uses b = 4 b{=}4 and g = 64 g{=}64 .
Because INT8 and INT4 store metadata for each group, their realized compression ratios are lower than the nominal 2 × 2{\times} and 4 × 4{\times} limits.
C1 (BF16) stores and reloads the uncompressed cache, serving as the cache baseline.
C2 (INT8) and C3 (INT4) store quantized caches and dequantize them before decoding.
Thus, C1–C2 isolates the effect of INT8 compression, while C1–C3 isolates the effect of aggressive INT4 compression.
4 Experimental Setup We evaluate Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ( Yang et al., 2024 ) , a bfloat16-native 7B instruction-tuned decoder with a 32K-token context window.
Each example is evaluated at K ∈ { 1 , 3 , 5 } K\in\{1,3,5\} retrieved chunks, giving 300 × 3 × 4 = 3,600 300\times 3\times 4=3{,}600 generations per dataset.
Accuracy is measured by containment exact match (EM), which marks a prediction correct if any normalized gold alias appears in the generated answer, and by token-level F1 as a secondary overlap metric.
The LLM judge achieves 92% agreement on 50 hand-checked labels and serves as the primary faithfulness signal.
We test three hypotheses using C1 BF16 as the uncompressed reference and C3 INT4 as the aggressive compression condition.
For H1 , we test whether faithfulness degrades even when accuracy is preserved.
For H3 , we test whether retrieval noise amplifies INT4 degradation by regressing the per-example INT4 faithfulness gap on distractor fraction within each K K .
H3 is evaluated only on RGB, where per-example noise ratios are available. License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2609.01587v1 [cs.LG] 01 Sep 2026 The Structure of Quantization Damage in LLMs: Why the Next Bit Should Be Spent Globally Jundong Hu † † thanks: Corresponding author ( jundongh@alumni.upenn.edu ).
Using causal mixed-precision intervention as ground truth (raise each layer to 8-bit in turn and measure the accuracy it recovers) across 9 open-weight models in 4 architecture families, we test 3 intuitive hypotheses: that quantization damage lives in task circuits, where the model computes, or in weight statistics.
Recovery is instead diffuse: for 8 of 9 models, recovering 75 % 75\% of the gap takes roughly half the layers; the lone exception, Qwen3-8B, is sharply concentrated.
At a matched precision budget, spending it globally on finer quantization granularity beats locally repairing the most recoverable layers for all 8 group-128-compatible models (all but OpenLLaMA, whose width rules out group-128), by 21 21 – 52 52 points, including the concentrated Qwen3-8B.
We report 2 secondary findings: the residual is budget-limited (8-bit is near-lossless in our evaluation across RTN, GPTQ, and AWQ), and the location of peak recovery correlates with architecture within a family, though not across families.
1 Introduction Quantization is now standard for cheap LLM serving, but its accuracy cost is uneven: under the same 4-bit scheme some models and tasks lose far more than others, and practitioners mitigate this by trial and error.
This leads to 2 practical questions: where does the damage occur, and how should a limited precision budget be allocated to repair it?
We test all 3 against a causal ground truth, mixed-precision intervention : raise each candidate location to 8-bit in turn, leave the rest at 4-bit, and measure the accuracy recovered (its marginal value of precision ).
The mixed-precision intervention instead shows recovery is generally diffuse: at a matched budget, precision is best allocated globally, to finer granularity, not to a critical few layers (Figure 1 ; claims below).
At matched effective bits/weight, global granularity beats oracle-selected local layer repair for all 8 group-128-compatible models (by 21 21 – 52 52 points), including the most concentrated one (Qwen3-8B): unconditional within the tested budget setting (§ 5 ). • No cheap localizer predicts the marginal value of precision.
Task circuits, the causal computation site, and weight/reconstruction statistics all fail to locate where restoring precision recovers accuracy; only the causal intervention identifies where restoring precision improves accuracy (§ 4 ). • The structure of recovery.
Damage is diffuse ( ∼ {\sim} half the layers); the residual is budget-limited (8-bit near-lossless across RTN, GPTQ, AWQ); and where recovery concentrates, the site is architecture-correlated within a family (leave-one-out predicts the held-out LLaMA-3.x size, 3/3), not across (§ 4 –§ 5 ).
H1 damage follows task circuits H2 damage lives where the model computes H3 the weights reveal the vulnerable layer × \times × \times × \times causal mixed-precision intervention Structure ✓ \checkmark diffuse damage architecture-correlated repair budget-allocated Figure 1: Three hypotheses, one causal test.
Three hypotheses for where quantization damage lives (H1–H3, left) are each refuted by mixed-precision intervention (center, × \times ); the resulting pattern is shown at right ( ✓ \checkmark ).
2 Related Work Standard 4-bit PTQ (round-to-nearest (RTN), group-wise scaling, GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) and AWQ ( Lin et al., 2024 ) ) and near-lossless mixed-precision 8-bit inference ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ) define our operating range.
Sensitivity-based allocation (Hessian importance ( Dong et al., 2019 ) , weight magnitude) and salience-based weight protection ( Xiao et al., 2025 ) both assume damage is localizable.
We find the recoverable layer is dissociated from causal importance, weight statistics, and reconstruction error (we do not test Hessian sensitivity itself; see § 6 ), so such heuristics transfer only within some families, and our “no few-layer fix” concerns whole layers, leaving weight-level protection a separate axis.
Task-aware quantization ties damage to calibration data ( Williams and Aletras, 2024 ) and task ( LeVi et al., 2025 ) ; we examine why it is task-specific.
3 Experimental Setup Models and evaluation.
Each is evaluated on 22 tasks at 200 samples/task with a fixed continuation-scoring harness (CORE ( Li et al., 2024 ) , a nanochat-style loop ( Karpathy, 2025 ) , seed 1337).
The tasks span reading comprehension, commonsense completion, factual retrieval, and formal/symbolic reasoning, among others (e.g. squad , hellaswag , arc_challenge , dyck_languages ), and cluster into roughly 5 reproducible groups stable across all 9 models, so “quantization disrupts task circuits” is a natural hypothesis (Appendix A ).
Quantization damage is a model’s fp16 → \to 4-bit CORE gap.
The 3 hypotheses locate the damage, each with a cheap localizer computed on a common calibration set: H1 (circuits) , each task’s head-deviation profile (every head’s activation relative to the cross-task mean), from which we measure circuit drift ; H2 (computation site) , causal activation patching ( Meng et al., 2022 ) , i.e. which layers the prediction causally depends on; and H3 (weights) , per-layer weight statistics (standard deviation and reconstruction error).
We compare these localizers with one causal instrument, mixed-precision intervention : raise a chosen layer (or set) to 8-bit with the rest at 4-bit and read the fraction of the 4-bit → \to 8-bit gap it recovers, the marginal value of precision at that location.
The controlled damage probe is per-row RTN at 4-bit (localization, § 4 ); the budget analysis (§ 5 ) adds package GPTQ/AWQ ( llm-compressor ( Red Hat AI and vLLM Project, 2024 ) , W4A16, group-128) and an 8-bit tier.
Our group-128 configuration needs a width divisible by 128, which excludes OpenLLaMA (intermediate dim 8640 8640 ); the budget analysis (§ 5 ) therefore covers the 8 remaining models.
H1 (circuits): if quantization hurt a task by reorganizing its circuit, tasks whose circuits drift more should lose more accuracy.
This prediction fails: the raw drift–damage correlation ( r = + 0.377 r{=}{+}0.377 , 198 198 task–model pairs) is a task-type confound that falls to + 0.05 {+}0.05 (n.s.) after jointly controlling for model and category, TOST (two one-sided tests)-equivalent to zero within ± 0.2 \pm 0.2 ( Lakens, 2017 ; Schuirmann, 1987 ) and not underpowered (magnitude drift does predict damage under the same test; Appendix B ).
H2 (computation site): causal activation patching places computation at the boundary layers (first and last MLP), which suggests protecting them.
H3 (weights): weight statistics (standard deviation or reconstruction error) should identify the recoverable layer, but neither does so outside LLaMA: a weight-std “fragility” heuristic finds it only within LLaMA (there, L1), missing Mistral’s endpoint and Qwen’s L4, while reconstruction error is negatively related to recovery in the one concentrated model (Qwen3-8B), whose most-recoverable layers are among the lowest-error ones (protecting its top 3 cuts reconstruction error by only ∼ 7 % {\sim}7\% yet recovers nearly all the accuracy; Appendix D ).
We do not test Hessian sensitivity (§ 6 ).
Ranking layers by their own protect-one recovery (raising that layer alone to 8-bit; an oracle order) and restoring the top- k k to 8-bit, recovery accrues slowly: reaching 50 / 75 / 90 % 50/75/90\% of the gap takes ∼ 20 / 49 / 73 % {\sim}20/49/73\% of layers on average (9/9 models; curves in Figure 3 , Appendix E ).
No single layer accounts for more than ∼ 44 % {\sim}44\% of the damage in any model.
Qwen3-8B is an exception: its 3 highest-recovery layers recover essentially the whole gap (its single best only ∼ 40 % {\sim}40\% ), so concentration is family-contingent.
Where it sits is architecture-correlated: LLaMA-3.x peaks at the same layer (L1) at every size, so leave-one-out predicts the held-out size (3/3); Mistral (n=1) peaks at its last, Qwen only at 8B.
But this is within-family only: Qwen3’s peak moves with scale, and no signal forecasts a new family’s site before a sweep (Appendix E ).
Global granularity yields higher recovery than the best matched local repair for all 8 group-128-compatible models, by 21 21 – 52 52 points (Table 1 ).
Finer granularity applied globally is therefore a better default than selective layer protection, unconditional within the tested matched-budget setting (scope caveats in § 6 ).
Recovery = % of the per-row RTN4 → \to RTN8 CORE gap; “top-1 conc.” = single-best-layer recovery.
Concentration does not imply that local repair is better at this budget: the local arm funds only ∼ 1.3 {\sim}1.3 layers, and Qwen3-8B’s single best recovers just ∼ 40 % {\sim}40\% , so even a model whose top 3 layers would close the gap cannot assemble enough of them locally.
The equal-budget match is first-order, but for the 7 diffuse models local repair would need 5.16 5.16 – 6.33 6.33 effective bits/weight to match global ( 7 7 – 15 × 15\times the disputed + 0.146 {+}0.146 increment), so the accounting would have to be wrong by 7 7 – 15 × 15\times to flip any of them.
The gain from the global arm is primarily attributable to granularity rather than calibration: per-row → \to g128 RTN recovers + 0.095 {+}0.095 CORE on average, whereas GPTQ and AWQ add only + 0.020 {+}0.020 / + 0.017 {+}0.017 over the same granularity.
At 8-bit, the methods perform similarly within the evaluation noise: per-row RTN matches fp16 within harness noise (residual CI contains zero for 6 / 8 6/8 ; full ladder, Table 8 ), and every 4-bit lever collapses to zero (Appendix F ).
6 Limitations Untested alternatives: our “no few-layer fix” is scoped to greedy, recovery-ranked interventions at layer granularity; non-greedy layer sets and weight-level salience protection ( Xiao et al., 2025 ) are untested, and H3 covers weight standard deviation and reconstruction error, not Hessian sensitivity.
Scale ceiling: all runs used a single 40 GB A100 (MIG) partition, which capped evaluation at ≤ 8 {\leq}8 B parameters, so larger scales are untested.
First-order bit-accounting: the equal-budget comparison is per-layer and weight-only; an exact-byte, per-weight local allocation and a multi-bit rate–distortion sweep ( Xu et al., 2024 ) are left to future work.

The cluster count is chosen by silhouette score, an internal criterion using no task labels: sweeping k = 2 ​ … ​ 8 k=2\ldots 8 , the silhouette is sharply maximal at k = 2 k{=}2 (the formal-vs-rest cut) and has a broad secondary plateau around k = 5 k{=}5 , the granularity we report as it is the finest split that stays stable across all 9 models.
The groupings are more consistent with task demands than with surface labels: arc_easy and arc_challenge co-cluster in 9/9 models (a single multiple-choice mechanism); language_identification clusters with formal tasks in 7/9 (processed as pattern-matching); and commonsense_qa is a persistent orphan (7 different partner sets). “Same tasks cluster” means similar relative activation patterns, not that identical physical heads fire; each model finds its own circuits under similar computational demands.

Appendix D H3: Weight Statistics Do Not Localize the Layer Weight-std vs. ground truth.

===== 2609.04748 (128 条) =====
On llama.cpp with GGUF weights this path divergence rises with quantization: for Qwen2.5-7B over ten independent server histories, it reached 37.4 percent of episodes at 16-bit and 82.5 percent at four-bit.
Serving four-bit weights through 16-bit kernels left divergence not detectably different from 16-bit, placing the amplification mainly in the quantized execution path.
At Q4_K_M, run-to-run divergence came mainly from llama.cpp’s server-level prompt cache: under the canonical episode order it moved divergence by 37.5 points, and at its default, divergence spanned 20.0 to 28.7 percent under five random orders.
Index Terms: Large language models, inference serving, reproducibility, prompt caching, key-value cache, quantization, software testing, empirical software engineering † † history: Date of publication xxxx 00, 0000, date of current version xxxx 00, 0000.
The savings are large and well documented: a recent evaluation of long-horizon agent pipelines reports 41 to 80 percent lower cost and 13 to 31 percent faster time to first token when caching is on [ 4 ] .
One describes an accuracy drop from roughly 78 percent to 60 percent when the prefix cache interacts with the recomputation path [ 5 ] .
In the literature, one recent study established that cached and recomputed decoding are not numerically equivalent under FP16 on single-turn arithmetic [ 7 ] , and a study of inference backends found that backend choice alone can shift benchmark scores by as much as 16.6 points, naming prefix caching among the causes, although its ablation of that feature measured aggregate accuracy alone [ 8 ] .
The workload is multi-turn agentic tool use from the Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard [ 9 ] , with single-turn grade school mathematics [ 10 ] as a bridge to the prior single-turn result.
SGLang now ships a deterministic mode [ 12 ] that reports consistent output across cached and uncached prefill on two of its three attention backends [ 13 ] .
Under the canonical episode order, a single server-level prompt-cache setting is shown to move run-to-run divergence by 37.5 percentage points, and at its default the divergence ranges from 20.0 to 28.7 percent under five random orders; once the setting is disabled, the order in which passes run has no effect in the configuration tested.
Under the configurations tested, restoring cache state removed all observed variation within each path, and that state is something deployments neither report nor control. • A replicated measurement of how weight quantization interacts with path divergence for Qwen2.5-7B on llama.cpp with GGUF weights.
Over ten independent server histories, divergence rises from 37.4 percent of episodes at F16 to 82.5 percent at Q4_K_M, an odds ratio of 2.30 per format step (95% CI 1.76 to 3.15).
Direct top-two margin measurements, and a control that serves the four-bit weights through the 16-bit kernels, point to the quantized execution path rather than the rounded weight values as the main source of the amplification. • An outcome-level analysis separating instability from degradation, independently replicating a concurrent single-turn result in a serving-engine setting: individual items change correctness in both directions with no detectable net change in accuracy. • A released harness, raw per-request logs including server-reported cache exposure, and an analysis pipeline that regenerates every number in this paper from the released logs and telemetry summary.
PagedAttention introduced the memory management that makes such sharing practical by storing the cache in non-contiguous blocks that different sequences can reference [ 1 ] .
Cache eviction and windowing schemes add another layer of state that varies between runs [ 15 , 16 ] .
The closest evaluation to ours in workload terms measured caching across more than five hundred agent sessions but recorded only cost and time to first token [ 4 ] .
SGLang implemented such a deterministic mode as an opt-in flag [ 12 ] ; it supports the radix cache on two of its three attention backends [ 13 ] , and its reported performance benchmarks were run with that cache disabled, so the reported cost of determinism excludes the cache.
Another quantified how much benchmark scores move when only the inference backend changes, reporting shifts up to 16.6 points and naming prefix caching among the mechanisms [ 8 ] .
II-C Quantization and behavioral change Quantization studies usually report aggregate accuracy, and by that measure modern low-bit formats are close to lossless [ 19 ] .
A more careful reading of compressed model behavior shows that this framing hides churn: models that match a baseline on aggregate accuracy can disagree with it on a substantial share of individual items [ 20 ] .
Damage from quantization is also unevenly distributed across populations that aggregate metrics average over [ 21 ] .
III Study Design III-A Research questions RQ1 (baseline determinism).
The key-value cache is stored in 16-bit floating point in every arm, including the arms that serve quantized weights, so that cache precision never varies with the weight format.
All measurements ran on a rented NVIDIA RTX 4090 with 24 GB of memory, compute capability 8.9, under Ubuntu 24.04 with the CUDA 12.6 toolkit.
The replicated sweep and the controls of Section III-F ran on a third machine of the same type under driver 595.91.07 with the same llama.cpp build.
The secondary workload is single-turn grade-school mathematics from GSM8K.
Three further experiments address both, all on Qwen2.5-7B at Q4_K_M under llama.cpp, the configuration with the highest run-to-run divergence in the grid; the third is also repeated for all four formats alongside the replicated sweep (Section III-F ).
Finally, to separate the quantized weight values from the arithmetic that serves them, the Q4_K_M weights are also dequantized to a 16-bit file, which keeps their values to within 16-bit rounding but runs them through the F16 kernels, and the sweep is repeated for that file under four histories.
III-G Measures Divergence is measured on token identifiers rather than on decoded text wherever the engine returns them, so that a difference is registered even when detokenization hides it. llama.cpp returns token identifiers on every response. vLLM 0.11.0 returns token strings on the endpoint we use, so its two configurations are compared on decoded tokens, which is a slightly weaker criterion; each configuration records which criterion applied.
These single-pass intervals are approximate: with 80 ordered episodes their coverage depends on the strength and form of the dependence, which is one reason the replicated sweep rather than any single pass carries the quantization result.
2 ) and the differences reported for its dequantization control use a bootstrap that resamples episodes and histories independently, the two-way design for crossed factors [ 27 ] , because each of those quantities pools passes over several histories.
The quantization trend is estimated with methods that keep each episode paired across formats, with the formats entered as an ordinal score from 0 (F16) to 3 (Q3_K_M): a logistic model with a random intercept per episode, fitted by maximum likelihood with adaptive quadrature; on the original grid, a conditional logistic regression stratified by episode, fitted by conditional maximum likelihood; and, as a secondary check, an exact test that permutes divergence across formats within each episode, which assumes that the format labels are exchangeable within an episode [ 28 ] .
Paired outcome changes on GSM8K are tested with an exact McNemar test [ 29 ] on the discordant pairs, which is the appropriate test when the same item is observed under both conditions and we care whether changes favor one condition [ 30 ] .
In every configuration, across both serving engines, across weight formats from 16-bit floating point down to three-bit k-quantization, the second run reproduced the first exactly: identical prompts at every step, identical emitted tokens, identical numbers of requests per episode.
IV-B RQ2: cache-enabled execution diverges, and why We then repeated the identical procedure with prefix reuse enabled, changing nothing else about the workload or the decoding parameters.
IV-B 1 Cache-enabled and cache-disabled execution differ The path-divergence column of Table V reports the first comparison.
This measurement is stable: on the canonical history of the replicated sweep (Section IV-D ), run with the server-level prompt cache disabled, path divergence for the four Qwen2.5-7B formats is 40.0, 60.0, 81.2 and 77.5 percent, against 36.2, 61.3, 75.0 and 77.5 percent in the grid.
Divergence also begins early: across configurations the first differing request has a median index between 1 and 4 of roughly ten, and within that request the first differing token has a median index between 4 and 17.
IV-B 2 On llama.cpp, a second caching layer drives run-to-run divergence Re-running the cache-enabled arm against itself (run-to-run divergence) changed the trajectory on 8.8 to 77.5 percent of episodes across the ten configurations of Table V .
Once the llama.cpp layer is disabled, repeated cache-enabled runs of Qwen2.5-7B at Q4_K_M agree on 79 of 80 episodes.
The first two use Qwen2.5-7B at Q4_K_M, the configuration with the highest run-to-run divergence in the grid, and hold the model, episode set, decoding parameters, and server freshness constant.
The vLLM comparison at the end of the section uses the same model at BF16, the checkpoint’s native 16-bit format, since vLLM serves the unquantized weights.
Beyond the per-request prefix reuse under study, llama.cpp maintains a server-level host-memory prompt cache that stores whole conversation states, selects among them by longest-common-prefix similarity rather than by identity, and evicts oldest-first [ 31 ] .
It is enabled by default at 8192 MiB and controlled by a documented flag [ 32 ] , and it was introduced in October 2025, so evaluations on llama.cpp builds from that date with default settings could have been affected without notice.
Holding everything else fixed, including a fresh server for each arm and the canonical episode order, and changing only that flag, repeated cache-enabled runs diverged on 1 of 80 episodes with the layer disabled (1.2 percent, dependence-aware 95% CI 0.0 to 6.7) and 31 of 80 with it at its default (38.8 percent, 22.5 to 57.5).
Under that order, changing this one setting produced a paired difference of 37.5 percentage points in run-to-run divergence (dependence-aware 95% CI 21.2 to 57.5).
With the server-level prompt cache active, interposing a complete cache-disabled pass between the two cache-enabled passes raises divergence from 38.8 to 77.5 percent, a difference of 38.8 points (dependence-aware interval 22.5 to 55.0), consistent with the intervening traffic rewriting the state that the second pass inherits.
With the layer disabled the same manipulation changes nothing, 1.2 percent either way, and the two orderings produced identical output, token for token, in every cell.
The two rows do not share a common starting point: at the default setting the first pass issues 854 requests and with the layer disabled it issues 827, so the setting changes behavior within a pass as well as between runs.
Split into quartiles of execution order, the counts of divergent episodes are 16, 6, 6 and 3, a trend that a permutation test puts at p = 0.0002 p=0.0002 .
Run-to-run divergence averaged 24.5 percent, with a between-order standard deviation of 3.1 points and a range from 20.0 percent (dependence-aware 95% CI 11.2 to 30.0) to 28.7 percent (17.5 to 40.0).
One episode in that order grew beyond the 16,384-token context window at the same request in both passes, and it was compared on the requests both passes completed.

Isolation changes the state each episode starts from, so these rates need not match the sweep’s: Q8_0 and Q3_K_M fall outside the range of its histories, while every quantized format remains above F16.

As a result, accuracy can exhibit unstable and non-monotonic behavior under progressive quantization, masking substantial fidelity loss relative to the BFloat16 ( BF16 ) uncompressed base model and potentially providing misleading signals for deployment decisions.
These measures capture changes in the predictive distribution that may not be reflected in top-1 task accuracy.
We conduct a 120-run experimental matrix across five foundation model architectures and four reasoning benchmarks under progressive quantization regimes, from uncompressed BF16 to Q2_K , providing a systematic fidelity analysis.
Our results show that divergence metrics generally increase under stronger quantization in the evaluated settings, complementing task accuracy with a fidelity signal relative to the BF16 reference.
Across the tested llama.cpp quantization schemes, mixed-precision Q4_K generally yields lower divergence than uniform Q4_0 at similar memory footprints.
1 : VRAM requirement versus average TVD in the evaluated settings.
The plot illustrates that more aggressive compression is associated with increased distributional divergence from the BF16 reference, with Q4_K providing a favorable empirical trade-off for the tested models and quantization schemes.
I Introduction The rapid scaling of Large Language Models (LLMs) is fundamentally bottlenecked by the strict memory constraints of edge devices [ 1 , 2 , 3 ] .
To bridge this gap, Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) serves as the standard for model compression [ 4 , 5 , 6 , 7 ] , with frameworks like llama.cpp [ 8 ] popularizing mixed-precision schemes (e.g., K-quants) [ 9 ] .
Despite these advancements, standard evaluation methodologies remain flawed [ 10 ] .
Industry benchmarks heavily rely on zero-shot task accuracy (i.e., evaluation without task-specific demonstrations) ( e.g., MMLU [ 11 ] , BoolQ [ 12 ] , PIQA [ 13 ] , HELM [ 14 ] ).
Building on Dutta et al. [ 16 ] , who showed that stable aggregate accuracy can hide severe internal prediction “flips” [ 17 ] , we argue that quantized LLM evaluation must move from discrete argmax outcomes to continuous, full-vocabulary probability distributions.
Standard benchmarks often treat LLMs as deterministic classifiers, yet the argmax operation discards most of the model’s uncertainty information and can score a confident BF16 model and a degraded quantized model as equivalent [ 18 , 19 ] .
This concern is consistent with evidence from computer vision [ 20 ] , adversarial robustness [ 21 ] , and medical AI [ 22 ] , where accuracy alone has proven insufficient for reliability assessment.
Foundational LLM work has likewise shown that confidence and calibration are necessary for reliable deployment [ 19 ] .
Quantization Focus Primary Metric(s) Gong et al. (2023) [ 10 ] General PTQ Benchmarking No (Top-1 Argmax) Uniform Zero-Shot Accuracy Huang et al. (2024) [ 15 ] Low-bit Degradation Trends No (Top-1 Argmax) Uniform Accuracy & Perplexity Dutta et al. (2024) [ 16 ] Prediction Instability Partial (KL-Divergence) Uniform Flip Rate & KL-Div.
Kübler et al. (2026) [ 23 ] Statistical Significance No (Binary Sample-Level) Uniform & Mixed McNemar’s Test Proposed Framework Structural Distributional Drift Yes (Full Vocabulary) Uniform & Mixed TVD & JSD Drawing on these cross-disciplinary principles, we investigate whether aggressive quantization induces distributional drift relative to the BF16 reference model even when task accuracy remains stable.
To quantify this drift, we use Total Variation Distance (TVD) [ 24 ] and Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) [ 25 ] , computed over the full vocabulary.
Moving beyond binary accuracy, our approach provides distribution-level fidelity signals under stronger compression (Figure 1 ).
Our four main contributions are: (1) we show that task accuracy can mask substantial distributional differences relative to a BF16 reference; (2) we introduce a full-vocabulary fidelity analysis using bounded TVD and JSD, supported by a coarse-graining result; (3) we evaluate 120 120 configurations across five foundation models, four benchmarks, and six quantization regimes; and (4) for the tested llama.cpp schemes, we find that mixed-precision Q4_K generally yields lower divergence than uniform Q4_0 at similar memory footprints.
II Related Work II-A LLM Compression and the Precision Bottleneck To mitigate the latency and privacy bottlenecks of cloud-centric LLMs [ 26 , 27 , 28 , 29 ] , Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) has become a standard approach for deploying large models on VRAM-constrained edge devices [ 30 , 9 ] .
Unlike Quantization-Aware Training, PTQ avoids costly retraining while reducing model memory through lower-precision weights and activations [ 31 , 32 ] .
This massive parameter reduction facilitates viable on-device local inference for latency-sensitive and privacy-critical applications, such as real-time critical error detection in machine translation [ 33 ] .
While PTQ methods reduce memory cost enough to make edge deployment feasible [ 4 , 5 ] , they do not guarantee that the quantized model preserves the full predictive distribution of its BF16 reference.
Unlike legacy uniform quantization ( e.g., Q4_0 ), which applies the same low-bit format across layers, mixed-precision quantization preserves higher precision ( e.g., 6-bit) in sensitive components while compressing more robust feed-forward layers more aggressively [ 9 ] .
II-B The Illusion of Accuracy and Distributional Divergence The prevailing paradigm for evaluating quantized LLMs relies on discrete, zero-shot accuracy [ 10 ] .
However, top-1 accuracy can create statistical mirages that obfuscate continuous model behaviors [ 18 ] .
Huang et al. [ 15 ] observed that low-bit models exhibit erratic, non-monotonic behavior on reasoning tasks.
Critically, Dutta et al. [ 16 ] highlighted that aggregate accuracy frequently masks massive internal prediction instability, leading to “flips” in previously correct answers.
While Dutta et al. [ 16 ] exposed this instability through discrete prediction flips and KL divergence, their distributional analysis inherits KL divergence’s sensitivity to near-zero probabilities, which are common in extreme low-bit quantization.
Similarly, Kübler et al. [ 23 ] rigorously quantified model degradation using McNemar’s test, and Rababah et al. [ 34 ] characterized behavioral divergences through sample-level correctness agreement.
For instance, Guo et al. [ 20 ] demonstrated that modern neural networks can maintain high task accuracy while becoming severely uncalibrated.
Similarly, adversarial robustness literature reveals that model vulnerabilities are often detected not by immediate accuracy drops, but by measuring divergence in the underlying softmax distributions [ 21 ] .
Furthermore, Kadavath et al. [ 19 ] recently emphasized that evaluating discrete accuracy without assessing an LLM’s internal confidence calibration leads to brittle, unreliable deployments.
This vulnerability extends directly to model compression; for instance, Xu et al. [ 35 ] recently demonstrated that quantization can silently destroy a model’s safety alignment while leaving traditional accuracy and perplexity metrics effectively unchanged.
Recognizing this, recent efforts have actively begun exploring uncertainty-aware and calibration-focused evaluations tailored specifically for low-bit quantized LLMs [ 36 ] .
To address the blind spots of discrete task-level evaluation, we introduce a continuous framework to quantify probability mass displacement relative to the BF16 reference.
Traditional legacy approaches ( e.g., Q4_0 ) utilize uniform quantization , wherein every weight tensor across the entire network is rigidly compressed to the same bit-width ( e.g., 4-bit).
While computationally simple, uniform quantization often destroys highly sensitive outlier weights and degrades critical attention mechanisms [ 5 , 6 ] .
2 : Evaluation framework for quantized LLMs.
The pipeline compares BF16 and quantized models through aligned inference, contrasting standard task accuracy with distribution-level fidelity metrics such as TVD and JSD.
Conversely, modern llama.cpp implementations utilize K-quantization ( e.g., Q4_K ), a sophisticated mixed-precision heuristic [ 9 ] .
Legacy methods rely on basic block quantization, assigning a full-precision ( BF16 ) scale and offset to every small block of weights ( e.g., 32 parameters), which introduces significant memory bandwidth overhead.
They group multiple standard blocks together into a larger “super-block.” The scaling factors for the individual constituent blocks are themselves quantized down to 8-bit integers, and a single set of FP16 super-scales is stored for the entire super-block to dequantize them.
They assign higher precision ( e.g., 6-bit) to mathematically sensitive structures, such as attention layers and final output tensors, while aggressively compressing the robust, over-parameterized feed-forward networks down to 3-bit or 4-bit.
We use these metrics to quantify distributional drift relative to the BF16 reference that may not be visible through zero-shot accuracy alone.
Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) [ 25 ] : To capture information-theoretic divergence stably, JSD evaluates the divergence of both distributions from their smoothed average mixture M = 1 2 ​ ( P + Q ) M=\frac{1}{2}(P+Q) : J S D ( P ∥ Q ) = 1 2 D K ​ L ( P ∥ M ) + 1 2 D K ​ L ( Q ∥ M ) JSD(P\parallel Q)=\frac{1}{2}D_{KL}(P\parallel M)+\frac{1}{2}D_{KL}(Q\parallel M) where D K ​ L ( ⋅ ∥ ⋅ ) D_{KL}(\cdot\parallel\cdot) denotes the standard Kullback-Leibler divergence.
In the context of our evaluation, a JSD of 0 0 indicates that the quantized model’s predictive distribution perfectly matches the uncompressed baseline (zero fidelity loss).
Conversely, a JSD approaching 1 1 indicates nearly disjoint predictive distributions and therefore severe divergence from the BF16 reference.
Together, these metrics quantify fidelity loss relative to the BF16 reference caused by quantization.
We compute statistical divergence between full-vocabulary softmax distributions to quantify fidelity to the BF16 reference that zero-shot accuracy may conceal.
Stream 1 computes zero-shot task accuracy by leveraging native Key-Value caching and selecting the candidate option that minimizes the standard length-normalized negative log-likelihood (NLL) utilized by established benchmarks [ 14 ] : NLL ( Y ) = − 1 | Y | ∑ i = 1 | Y | log P ( y i ∣ X , y < i ) \text{NLL}(Y)=-\frac{1}{|Y|}\sum_{i=1}^{|Y|}\log P(y_{i}\mid X,y_{<i}) .
For each prompt, Stream 2 evaluates the prompt, extracts the backend-provided full-vocabulary output vector, and applies a temperature- 1.0 1.0 softmax without distribution truncation.
We then compare the BF16 and quantized distributions using TVD and JSD.
Thus, Stream 2 is a prompt-conditioned full-vocabulary fidelity diagnostic.
VII ∎ IV-B Quantization, Prompting, and Flip Analysis To guarantee that measured divergence stems strictly from precision truncation, all quantized models are generated directly from their BF16 Hugging Face baselines using llama.cpp .
To objectively map the degradation curve, we evaluate five distinct compression states: 8-bit uniform ( Q8_0 ), 6-bit mixed ( Q6_K ), 4-bit uniform ( Q4_0 ), 4-bit mixed ( Q4_K ), and 2-bit mixed ( Q2_K ).
Furthermore, adapting the methodology of Dutta et al. [ 16 ] , we track discrete prediction “total flips” (the aggregate sum of both Flip-to-Wrong and Flip-to-Correct transitions) between the BF16 and quantized states to expose the overall instability of task-level accuracy.
IV-C Metrics We use TVD and JSD as fidelity diagnostics relative to the BF16 reference and compare their trends with task accuracy and prediction flips.
