This bound varies from 10 1 10^{1} to 10 13 10^{13} for typical LLM modules, making inter-module sensitivity ranking unreliable.
In Stage 1, the scalar proxy is replaced by an activation-aware metric derived from the Kronecker-factored Hessian, reducing the MCKP to a form whose continuous relaxation admits a closed-form solution.
In Stage 2, a cross-layer-aware local search evaluates bit-width updates using the end-to-end model loss.
Experiments on multiple LLMs across different bit budgets show that CASA achieves lower perplexity than the latest scalar-proxy baselines, especially at ultra-low bit-widths ( < 3 <3 bits per weight).
1 Introduction Weight-only post-training quantization (PTQ) ( Lin et al., 2024 ; Frantar et al., 2022 ) has become the standard technique for deploying large language models under memory constraints.
Among the various PTQ strategies, mixed-precision quantization—where different weight matrices are quantized to different bit widths—offers a flexible trade-off between model size and accuracy ( Guo et al., 2025 ; Dong et al., 2019 ; Dong et al., 2020 ) .
The bit allocation problem is naturally formulated as a Multiple-Choice Knapsack Problem (MCKP) Lee and Song (2025) ; Chen et al. (2021) : given a total bit budget, assign a bit width to each weight matrix so as to minimize the total quantization-induced loss.
Recent mixed-precision allocators, including HIGGS Malinovskii et al. (2025) and Q-Palette Lee and Song (2025) , acknowledge the role of activations but ultimately reduce this matrix-form proxy to a single scalar coefficient α l \alpha_{l} , yielding α l ​ ( ‖ Δ ​ 𝑾 ( l ) ‖ 2 / ‖ 𝑾 ( l ) ‖ 2 ) \alpha_{l}(\nicefrac{{\|\Delta\bm{W}^{(l)}\|^{2}}}{{\|\bm{W}^{(l)}\|^{2}}}) .
The deep residual architecture of modern LLMs violates this assumption: quantization error injected at layer l l propagates through the residual stream and interacts with the error at layer l + 1 l{+}1 , producing cross-layer terms—the off-diagonal blocks of the full network Hessian—that the additive proxy cannot represent.
Guided by this separation of concerns, we propose Cross-layer Activation-aware Sensitivity Allocation (CASA) (see Figure 1 ), a two-stage allocator in which Stage 1 handles the former problem inside the MCKP, and Stage 2 handles the latter problem outside it.
This advantage is not merely empirical: any scalar surrogate suffers a worst-case multiplicative distortion relative to the true quadratic ( Theorem 3.1 ), which can flip the sensitivity ranking of two modules and thereby induce unbounded MCKP regret ( Proposition 3.2 ); in the high-rate limit our proxy further yields a water-filling allocation that no scalar surrogate can recover ( Theorem 4.1 ).
Treating the Stage-1 assignment as initialization, we perform a greedy bit-swap search whose acceptance criterion is the calibration cross-entropy loss measured after re-quantization, so that the non-additive cross-layer interactions are evaluated end-to-end rather than through any quadratic surrogate.
Figure 1: Overview of Cross-layer Activation-aware Sensitivity Allocation (CASA), which determines the bit allocation by accounting for activation-aware loss and cross-layer interactions.
We derive an activation-aware quantization-error proxy that retains the full Kronecker-factored Hessian from the second-order Taylor expansion of the task loss, and prove a worst-case distortion bound for any scalar proxy ( Section 3 ).
Building on this proxy, we propose CASA, a two-stage allocator that solves an activation-aware MCKP for self-term costs (Stage 1) and then performs a cross-entropy-driven cross-layer local search (Stage 2) that provably never worsens the Stage-1 solution ( Section 4 ).
We further observe that CASA’s improvement grows with the distortion bound, consistent with Theorem 3.1 ( Section 5 ).
2 Preliminaries 2.1 Bit-allocation as constrained optimization problem The mixed-precision quantization problem reduces to choosing a quantizer for each layer so as to minimize the total weighted error subject to a resource constraint ( Chen et al., 2021 ) .
In addition to the MCKP formulation, bit-allocation has been addressed via reinforcement learning ( Wang et al., 2019 ) or differentiable search ( Yang and Jin, 2021 ) .
2.2 Sensitivity Measurement Many methods construct a surrogate objective to determine the bit allocation.
The HAWQ series ( Dong et al., 2019 ; Dong et al., 2020 ; Yao et al., 2021 ) uses spectral information of the Hessian as a sensitivity metric.
OMPQ ( Ma et al., 2023 ) prioritizes the bit allocation so that layer outputs tend to be mutually orthogonal.
These approaches fail to account for activation outliers, which are explicitly handled by recent quantization methods ( Lin et al., 2024 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ) .
2.3 Cross-layer Awareness for Quantization Cross-layer awareness has been exploited for quantization itself, but rarely for bit allocation.
BRECQ ( Li et al., 2021 ) iteratively refines the bit allocation by monitoring the validation loss.
QEP ( Arai and Ichikawa, 2025 ) propagates the previous layer’s quantization loss to the next layer, thereby suppressing error accumulation.
InfoQ ( Akbulut et al., 2026 ) studies how quantization perturbations propagate to affect the output information.
For KV-cache quantization, KVTuner ( Li et al., 2025 ) solves a multi-objective optimization problem informed by inter-layer correlations.
3 Activation-Aware Quantization Error Proxy 3.1 Derivation Consider a pre-trained model with L L layers.
We approximate the change in task loss Δ ​ L \Delta L through the following chain of standard assumptions: (i) Second-order Taylor expansion. Δ ​ L ≈ 1 2 ​ Δ ​ 𝜽 ⊤ ​ 𝑯 ​ ( 𝜽 ) ​ Δ ​ 𝜽 \Delta L\approx\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta\bm{\theta}^{\top}\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})\,\Delta\bm{\theta} , where 𝑯 \bm{H} is the Hessian of the loss with respect to all parameters 𝜽 \bm{\theta} . (ii) Local optimality.
Given calibration samples { 𝒙 ( n ) } n = 1 N \{\bm{x}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N} , the Hessian is estimated via the empirical Fisher: 𝒙 ( n ) ​ ( 𝒙 ( n ) ) ⊤ \bm{x}^{(n)}(\bm{x}^{(n)})^{\top} for the input side and 𝑯 y ( n ) \bm{H}_{y}^{(n)} for the output side.
Combining (i)–(iv), the per-layer loss is given by Δ ​ L ( l ) ≈ 1 2 ​ tr ⁡ ( 𝑩 ( l ) ​ Δ ​ 𝑾 ( l ) ​ 𝑨 ( l ) ​ ( Δ ​ 𝑾 ( l ) ) ⊤ ) , \Delta L^{(l)}\;\approx\;\frac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{tr}(\bm{B}^{(l)}\,\Delta\bm{W}^{(l)}\,\bm{A}^{(l)}\,(\Delta\bm{W}^{(l)})^{\top}), where 𝑨 ( l ) = 1 N ​ 𝑿 ​ 𝑿 ⊤ ∈ ℝ d in × d in , 𝑩 ( l ) = 1 N ​ ∑ n = 1 N 𝑯 y ( n ) ∈ ℝ d out × d out . \bm{A}^{(l)}=\frac{1}{N}\,\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{in}}\times d_{\text{in}}},~~\bm{B}^{(l)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bm{H}_{y}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{out}}\times d_{\text{out}}}.
3.2 Existing Proxies as Degenerate Cases Section 3.1 provides a unified view of existing quantization error proxies as successively coarser approximations (see Table 1 ).
Table 1: Hierarchy of quantization error proxies.
Consider two modules indexed by i ∈ { 1 , 2 } i\in\{1,2\} and two bit-width choices, L L and H H , where L L is of lower precision and H H is of higher precision.
Suppose that the true sensitivities and proxy sensitivities have opposite rankings: γ 1 > γ 2 , γ ^ 2 > γ ^ 1 \gamma_{1}>\gamma_{2},~~\widehat{\gamma}_{2}>\widehat{\gamma}_{1} .
Then the true optimal allocation assigns H H to module 1 1 , whereas the proxy-optimal allocation assigns H H to module 2 2 .
The true regret of the proxy-optimal allocation is Regret = ( γ 1 − γ 2 ) ​ ( d L − d H ) . \operatorname{Regret}=(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})(d_{L}-d_{H}). (3) In particular, taking γ 1 = R \gamma_{1}=R and γ 2 = 1 \gamma_{2}=1 makes the regret equal to ( R − 1 ) ​ ( d L − d H ) (R-1)(d_{L}-d_{H}) , which diverges as R → ∞ R\to\infty .
4 Cross-layer Activation-aware Sensitivity Allocation (CASA) To address the issues derived from scalar-based sensitivity and layer-wise independent treatment, we propose Cross-layer Activation-aware Sensitivity Allocation (CASA) .
Stage 1 solves an MCKP using the activation-aware proxy of Section 3.1 on the self-term alone ( Section 4.1 ); Stage 2 then refines this allocation via a cross-layer local search that evaluates bit-swaps using the end-to-end loss ( Section 4.2 ); this refinement is guaranteed to produce a solution no worse than the Stage-1 one.
4.1 Stage 1: MCKP Formulation Following Section 3 , we formulate the bit allocation as an MCKP over all weight matrices in the model.
Each module can be quantized at one of the candidates in 𝒬 = { q 1 , … , q | 𝒬 | } \mathcal{Q}=\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{|\mathcal{Q}|}\} (e.g., { 2.0 , 3.0 , … , 6.0 } \{2.0,3.0,\ldots,6.0\} ).
The MCKP is: min P ∈ { 0 , 1 } \displaystyle\min_{P\in\{0,1\}}~ ∑ l , m , q P l , m , q ​ ℓ l , m , q \displaystyle\sum_{l,m,q}P_{l,m,q}\,\ell_{l,m,q} (5) subject to ∑ q P l , m , q = 1 ​ ( ∀ l , m ) , ∑ l , m , q P l , m , q ⋅ bits q ⋅ size l , m ≤ B avg ⋅ ∑ l , m size l , m \displaystyle\sum_{q}P_{l,m,q}=1~(\forall\,l,m),\quad\sum_{l,m,q}P_{l,m,q}\cdot\text{bits}_{q}\cdot\text{size}_{l,m}\leq B_{\rm avg}\cdot\sum_{l,m}\text{size}_{l,m} (6) where P l , m , q = 1 P_{l,m,q}=1 indicates that module ( l , m ) (l,m) is quantized at bit width q q , bits q \text{bits}_{q} includes the overhead from zero points and scales, and B avg B_{\rm avg} is the target average bits per weight (BPW).
In the continuous relaxation of this MCKP, where bit-widths are allowed to take real values and per-module errors follow the high-rate distortion law Γ i ​ 2 − 2 ​ β i \Gamma_{i}2^{-2\beta_{i}} with Γ i \Gamma_{i} proportional to ℓ l , m , q \ell_{l,m,q} at high q q and n i = size l , m n_{i}=\text{size}_{l,m} , the optimal allocation admits a closed-form solution: Theorem 4.1 (Optimal continuous bit allocation under high-rate distortion) .
4.2 Stage 2: Local Search for considering Cross-Layer Interactions This section presents a local search that refines the Stage-1 allocation by explicitly accounting for cross-layer interactions.
We relax Assumption (iii) in Section 3.1 (layer-wise block-diagonal Hessian) to a block-tridiagonal approximation.
Specifically, we retain off-diagonal Fisher blocks only between adjacent layers ( l , l + 1 ) (l,l{+}1) and, within each such block, only the same-module-type entries ( l , m ) (l,m) – ( l + 1 , m ) (l{+}1,m) . Δ ​ L ≈ ∑ l , m err self ⁡ ( l , m ) + 2 ​ ∑ l = 1 L − 1 ∑ m err cross ⁡ ( l , l + 1 , m ) . \Delta L\;\approx\;\sum_{l,m}\operatorname{err}_{\text{self}}(l,m)+2\sum_{l=1}^{L-1}\sum_{m}\operatorname{err}_{\text{cross}}(l,l\!+\!1,m). (10) Consideration of the cross-layer interactions is not harmful for obtaining better assignment, which is ensured by following proposition.
For the cross-terms, we apply a Kronecker factorization to the off-diagonal Fisher block: 𝑭 ( l , m ) , ( l + 1 , m ) ≈ 𝔼 n ​ [ 𝒙 n ( l , m ) ​ ( 𝒙 n ( l + 1 , m ) ) ⊤ ] ⊗ 𝔼 n ​ [ 𝒈 n ( l , m ) ​ ( 𝒈 n ( l + 1 , m ) ) ⊤ ] , \bm{F}^{(l,m),(l+1,m)}\approx\mathbb{E}_{n}[\bm{x}_{n}^{(l,m)}(\bm{x}_{n}^{(l+1,m)})^{\top}]\otimes\mathbb{E}_{n}[\bm{g}_{n}^{(l,m)}(\bm{g}_{n}^{(l+1,m)})^{\top}], For computational tractability, we approximate the Kronecker factors of Section 4.2 by their diagonals (consistent with the diagonal approximation already used for the self-term).
The MCKP has already minimized the self-term proxy; the local search further reduces the total objective ( 4.2 ) by exploiting cross-layer interactions while maintaining the budget constraint.
Each round consists of three phases: (1) candidate generation, (2) evaluation, and (3) acceptance.
We write 𝒒 ⊕ s \bm{q}\oplus s for the resulting allocation, in which unaffected modules retain their original bit-widths and every module satisfies ( 𝒒 ⊕ s ) l , m ∈ { 1 , … , | 𝒬 | } (\bm{q}\oplus s)_{l,m}\in\{1,\dots,|\mathcal{Q}|\} .
The cross-term delta is then: Δ cross ​ ( s ) = 2 ​ ∑ l , m ( C ¯ q l , m new , q l + 1 , m new ( l , m ) − C ¯ q l , m , q l + 1 , m ( l , m ) ) . \Delta_{\text{cross}}(s)=2\sum_{l,m}\Bigl(\bar{C}^{(l,m)}_{q_{l,m}^{\text{new}},\,q_{l+1,m}^{\text{new}}}-\bar{C}^{(l,m)}_{q_{l,m},\,q_{l+1,m}}\Bigr). (16) In Phase 2 (evaluation), we form 𝒮 \mathcal{S} as the top- K K swaps with the smallest (most negative) Δ cross ​ ( s ) \Delta_{\text{cross}}(s) , restricted to Δ cross ​ ( s ) < 0 \Delta_{\text{cross}}(s)<0 .
In Phase 3 (acceptance), we accept the candidate with the largest loss reduction: 𝒒 ( r + 1 ) = 𝒒 ( r ) ⊕ argmin s ∈ 𝒮 ​ ℒ cal ​ ( 𝒒 ( r ) ⊕ s ) \bm{q}^{(r+1)}=\bm{q}^{(r)}\oplus\underset{s\in\mathcal{S}}{\operatorname{argmin}}\;\mathcal{L}_{\rm cal}(\bm{q}^{(r)}\oplus s) (17) where ℒ cal \mathcal{L}_{\rm cal} is the calibration loss.
We evaluate CASA on five models: Llama-2-7B, Llama-3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-8B, and Qwen3-14B.
We compare four methods: • Uniform : all modules are assigned the same bit-width. • Q-Palette ( Lee and Song, 2025 ) : one of the latest mixed-precision methods, in which a scalar sensitivity parameter is computed via the HIGGS metric.
While the original Q-Palette uses condensed quantizers, we replace them with widely-used GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2022 ) . • CASA self : an ablation that solves only the activation-aware MCKP (Stage 1 of Algorithm 1 ), without the cross-term local search. • CASA (ours): the full proposed method ( Algorithm 1 ).
We use GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2022 ) as the main quantizer with a group size of 128 and C4 as calibration data.
The candidate bit-widths 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} are integers from 2 to 8.
For the local search (Stage 2), we use K = 100 K=100 candidate evaluations per round with a maximum of 20 rounds.
We report perplexity on WikiText-2 and average accuracy on a suite of common-sense reasoning benchmarks.

Four-bit per-channel weights reduce UTMOS, a predicted mean opinion score, by 2.8 on Supertonic and 0.07 on Kokoro, and per-tensor scaling can cause severe degradation even at 8 bits.
A staged ablation procedure identifies it, and per-layer GPTQ can restore it to within 0.1 UTMOS.
Real int8 and int4 kernels reproduce the simulated ordering at hardware-dependent cost.
On a Mac mini, a 4-bit weight kernel runs Supertonic at 0.60 × \times the fp32 latency while int8 is slower, so each configuration requires validation on the target runtime.
1 Introduction On-device text-to-speech (TTS) enables private and offline speech synthesis under memory and compute constraints [ 1 ] .
Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses a trained model without retraining [ 6 , 14 ] , but the bit width is only one of several design choices.
Prior quantization studies cover a single system, or several systems under a single method [ 10 , 28 , 11 ] , and no study compares heterogeneous pretrained TTS pipelines, quantized-component scopes, activation granularity, and measured deployment paths under one protocol.
Real int8 and int4 execution shows that peak memory, latency, and energy depend on the runtime and the hardware (Sec.
2 Related work TTS compression includes architecture search in LightSpeech [ 16 ] , acoustic-model int8 PTQ combined with architectural compression [ 9 ] , and BitTTS [ 10 ] , which applies extreme ternary quantization (1.58-bit training) and weight indexing to build compact TTS models for on-device use and identifies the vocoder as the sensitive component of its model.
PTQ for diffusion models is established for image generation [ 25 , 12 , 5 ] and has been extended to audio generation by PTQ4ADM and timestep-aware quantization [ 28 , 11 ] .
HAWQ ranks layers by Hessian curvature for mixed precision in vision [ 4 ] , and Diet-KIT selects per-layer precision by sensitivity for speech translation [ 15 ] .
Their English floating-point (fp) baselines reach UTMOS 4.473, 4.398, and 4.524 at WER 0.028, 0.032, and 0.030, respectively, on ONNX CPU fp32, torch Metal fp16, and torch x86 fp32, with a default number of function evaluations (NFE) of 8 for Supertonic and 32 for OmniVoice.
1 ) are the flow-matching F5-TTS [ 3 ] , the feedforward StyleTTS 2 [ 13 ] and MMS-TTS [ 20 ] , and the autoregressive codec-token LMs Zonos [ 26 ] , Dia [ 18 ] , Orpheus [ 2 ] , Kyutai TTS [ 30 ] , and CSM-1B [ 8 ] .
Each evaluated language uses the same 200 frozen FLORES-200 devtest sentences [ 19 ] with fixed per-utterance seeds, in English and, where supported, in Korean.
Each condition is paired with the unquantized model at its native floating-point precision (fp) 1 1 1 fp32 for Supertonic, Kokoro, F5-TTS, StyleTTS 2, MMS-TTS, and Chatterbox, fp16 for OmniVoice and Dia, and bf16 for Orpheus, Kyutai, CSM, VoxCPM, and the Zonos backbone, as shipped. on the same platform and at the same number of sampling steps.

OmniVoice, which is evaluated in both languages, uses 64 English and 64 Korean sentences, and the English-only models of Secs.
The weight-only sweep applies symmetric round-to-nearest (RTN) quantization at 8, 6, and 4 bits with s = max ⁡ | w | / ( 2 b − 1 − 1 ) s=\max|w|/(2^{b-1}-1) and 16-bit scale factors shared per tensor, per output channel (the default), or per group of 128 consecutive weights within a channel (group:128).
We quantize the whole model, individual components, and mixed-precision combinations, and we sweep the NFE over { 4 , 8 , 12 } \{4,8,12\} for Supertonic and { 4 , 8 , 16 , 32 } \{4,8,16,32\} for OmniVoice.
Calibrated PTQ applies per-layer GPTQ [ 6 ] or activation-aware weight scaling [ 14 , 29 ] to one component at a time, with input statistics collected from 64 English calibration sentences synthesized by the fp model (Sec.
For Supertonic the vocoder is excluded from weights and activations, for Kokoro every module is quantized, and for OmniVoice the W4 configuration (LM at W4, token head at W8, codec at fp) adds A8 to every module, on x86 fp32 baselines for Supertonic and Kokoro and on a CUDA fp16 baseline for OmniVoice.
The replication models receive whole-model A8 at per-channel and per-tensor scales and A8 on their W4 configurations.
Real 4-bit weight execution uses the block-wise 4-bit operator of ONNX Runtime ( MatMulNBits , block size 128) for Supertonic on x86, after the dense 1 × 1 1\times 1 convolutions of its flow estimator are rewritten as matrix products so that the kernel covers 99.5% of the weights (7.6% as shipped), and torchao int4 weight-only quantization (group 128, bf16 operands) for the LMs of OmniVoice, Orpheus, Kyutai, CSM, Chatterbox, and VoxCPM on an RTX PRO 6000.
Paired 95% bootstrap intervals use 10,000 resamples over the 200 sentences, conditional on the evaluated settings.
A quantized configuration is defined to be quality-preserving on the two English proxies when its paired Δ \Delta UTMOS interval lies within [ − 0.05 , 0.05 ] [-0.05,0.05] and the upper endpoint of its paired Δ \Delta WER interval is at most 0.01 0.01 relative to fp, evaluated at unrounded endpoints.
The LM of OmniVoice runs at 8-bit weights and activations (W8A8) and the LM of Orpheus at weight-only int8, with the codecs in floating point, bf16 operands, and compiled fp16 (OmniVoice) or bf16 (Orpheus) latency baselines.

License: CC BY-NC-ND 4.0 arXiv:2609.33923v2 [cs.CL] 29 Sep 2026 Quantization Error Is Spectrally Flat: A Single Random Probe Is a Calibrated, Data-Free Sensitivity Estimator, with Application to Budget-Targeted Mixed-Precision Quantization at 400B Scale I.
Kennedy Affiliation: (Auckland, New Zealand) Abstract A single random Gaussian probe gives an unbiased estimate of the squared Frobenius norm of a layer’s quantization error (Theorem 1 ).
Flatness makes the probe estimator calibrated : its coefficient of variation is 2 / d eff \sqrt{2/d_{\mathrm{eff}}} , predictable from tensor shape before touching the weights, and it matches measurement to 0.1 % 0.1\% at the median.
One probe measures per-tensor sensitivity to within 4 4 to 7 % 7\% , twenty probes to within 1.3 1.3 to 1.4 % 1.4\% , and averaging converges at the i.i.d. rate against exact ground truth.
Gaussian probes carrying the network’s own input statistics score every tensor at six candidate bit-widths, which by a one-line extension of the theorem estimates the layer-wise objective that GPTQ minimizes with calibration data; a multiple-choice knapsack solver allocates bit-widths under an exact byte budget, and divergence guardrails block catastrophic 2-bit assignments.
The isolated and propagated signals are different quantities: they rank tensors independently on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (Spearman − 0.01 -0.01 ), and calibration transfers only partly (per-sequence CV 0.16 to 2.4 against 0.04 to 0.07), yet rank agreement with an independent reference at the allocator’s 50 sequences is 0.97 to 1.00 because the propagated signal spans ten times the range across tensors.
Propagation supplies the missing input statistics: on Qwen3-8B, Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.8-27B the propagated probe’s estimate of the layer-wise calibration objective rank-correlates 0.83, 0.81 and 0.83 with the objective computed from real activations (domain ceiling 0.99), while the isolated estimator is uncorrelated with it ( − 0.07 -0.07 to − 0.15 -0.15 ).
That objective is nevertheless the wrong quantity to allocate from: at matched bytes on Qwen3.8-27B, allocating llama.cpp k-quant types from the block-output probe signal beats the vendor IQ3_M mix by 5.5% perplexity and beats an oracle that allocates from the real-activation objective by 1.6%, because the block signal carries the downstream weighting the per-tensor objective lacks.
Fed through one allocator at matched bytes on Qwen3-8B, the propagated probe ties calibration-based HAWQ-V2 Hessian-trace sensitivity on WikiText-2 (paired p = 0.96 p=0.96 ), both beat the isolated estimator by 0.6 % 0.6\% ( p < 10 − 15 p<10^{-15} ), and all three tie on MMLU; on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B the isolated and propagated allocations tie on perplexity and the isolated one leads MMLU by 1.1 1.1 points.
On the four MoE models where uniform 4-bit builds of comparable size exist, RAM builds reach 3.5 to 13.6% lower median WikiText-2 perplexity.
On Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, RAM beats a GPTQ-style “protect attention” allocation by 1.7% perplexity at 4.8% smaller size, and at 33.6 GB it scores 73.1% on MMLU against 72.1% for the BF16 model.
1 Introduction Post-training quantization (PTQ) is the main route to running large language models (LLMs) on consumer hardware.
GPTQ [ 13 ] , AWQ [ 25 ] , and SqueezeLLM [ 20 ] compress well, and all three need a representative calibration dataset.
Calibration data may be unavailable for proprietary models, the chosen distribution may not match the deployment domain, and calibration itself costs compute: on a 400B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model, GPTQ-style calibration loads the full model and runs forward passes over hundreds of sequences, which takes hours on high-end hardware.
Data-free approaches [ 32 , 37 , 4 ] avoid calibration but either apply uniform bit-widths or rank tensors by a single weight statistic (kurtosis [ 1 ] , the Frobenius norm of the rounding error [ 37 ] ) with hand-tuned thresholds.
This observation, formalized in Theorem 1 , says that a single random probe estimates in expectation the quantity that calibration-based methods estimate with data.
Because the single-probe coefficient of variation is 2 / d eff \sqrt{2/d_{\mathrm{eff}}} (Lemma 4 ), flatness places the estimator in its best-case concentration regime: one probe yields a 4 4 to 7 % 7\% measurement of per-tensor sensitivity and twenty probes yield 1.3 1.3 to 1.4 % 1.4\% .
Theoretical guarantee : We prove that 𝔼 ⁡ [ 𝐱 ⊤ ​ Δ ⊤ ​ Δ ​ 𝐱 ] = ‖ Δ ‖ F 2 \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}^{\top}\Delta^{\top}\Delta\,\mathbf{x}]=\|\Delta\|_{F}^{2} for Gaussian 𝐱 \mathbf{x} , where Δ = 𝐖 − 𝐖 ^ \Delta=\mathbf{W}-\hat{\mathbf{W}} is the quantization error matrix (Theorem 1 , § 3.1 ).
Measured spectral flatness : We compute the effective dimensionality of quantization error in closed form on 1,683 tensors across two architectures and four bit-widths: d eff = 0.93 d_{\mathrm{eff}}=0.93 to 0.96 × m ​ n / ( m + n ) 0.96\times mn/(m{+}n) , the i.i.d.-noise value.
The resulting per-probe accuracy 2 / d eff \sqrt{2/d_{\mathrm{eff}}} is predictable from tensor shape alone and verified to 0.1 % 0.1\% median accuracy (§ 3.2 ).
Propagated functional probes : Random Gaussian probes propagated through the network score every tensor at six candidate bit-widths by output-space divergence, in 36 seconds to 9 minutes per model with no calibration data (§ 3.3 ).
The propagated probe is the same estimator applied to the input- and downstream-weighted error operator (Proposition 3 ), which with the network’s own input statistics is the calibration objective of GPTQ and OBQ without data.
Isolated and propagated sensitivities are different quantities : the two signals rank tensors independently on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (Spearman − 0.01 -0.01 over 390 tensors) and calibration transfers only partly: per-sequence CV 0.16 to 2.4 against 0.04 to 0.07, yet rank agreement at the allocator’s 50 sequences is 0.97 to 1.00 because the propagated signal spans ten times the range across tensors (§ 4.4 , § 5.1 ).
Data-free estimate of the calibration objective : the propagated probe’s quadratic form rank-correlates 0.83 (Qwen3-8B), 0.81 (Qwen3.5-9B) and 0.83 (Qwen3.8-27B, with the pipeline’s adaptation rule switched off) with the GPTQ layer objective under real activations, against − 0.07 -0.07 to − 0.15 -0.15 for the isolated estimator and 0.25 for HAWQ-V2; the block-output cosine the pipeline uses reaches only 0.50 and 0.11.
Allocating from the objective itself, with real data or without, nevertheless loses to the block-output signal at matched bytes on Qwen3.8-27B (§ 4.5 ).
Matched-budget ablations : through one allocator at one budget, the isolated and propagated allocations tie on Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on perplexity, with the isolated one 1.1 1.1 MMLU points ahead; on Qwen3-8B the propagated probe ties calibration-based HAWQ-V2 (paired p = 0.96 p=0.96 ), both beat the isolated estimator by 0.6 % 0.6\% perplexity ( p < 10 − 15 p<10^{-15} ), and all three tie on MMLU (§ 4.7 , § 4.8 ).
Budget-targeted allocation : Per-tensor scores feed a multiple-choice knapsack solver under an explicit byte budget, with divergence guardrails against catastrophic 2-bit assignments; one probe pass serves any budget (§ 3.5 ).
Seven-architecture evaluation : Quality results on models from 8B to 122B parameters (15 to 250 GB in BF16), and probe timing up to 400B parameters (803 GB), covering dense, MoE with up to 256 experts, FP8-native, and MLA architectures (§ 4 ).
Blanket “protect attention” rules lose to measured sensitivity : A GPTQ-style allocation that leaves all attention and shared-expert tensors unquantized is both larger and worse than the probe allocation; the probes rank attention output/value projections and MoE routers most sensitive and routed experts least, so bytes go to specific tensors rather than to a tensor class (§ 4.2 , § 5.3 ).
MLA probe coverage : We identify and resolve a limitation of functional probes on Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) [ 8 ] architectures, where compressed KV projections create sensitivity blind spots (§ 3.7 ).
GPTQ [ 13 ] applies layerwise Optimal Brain Quantization using Hessian information from calibration data, processing one column at a time with error compensation.
SqueezeLLM [ 20 ] combines sensitivity-based non-uniform quantization with dense-and-sparse decomposition.
SpQR [ 9 ] isolates outlier weights for full-precision storage.
MXQ [ 37 ] uses the Frobenius norm of quantization error as a single sensitivity metric for mixed-precision allocation.
HQQ [ 4 ] uses half-quadratic splitting for fast uniform quantization.
HIGGS [ 26 ] applies Hadamard rotation via the linearity theorem.
Lee et al. [ 23 ] proposed a data-free mixed-precision sensitivity metric for vision networks with synthetic labelled data.
Concurrent calibration-free allocators for LLMs rank layers or experts by weight-spectrum statistics: NSDS [ 38 ] combines numerical and structural layer sensitivities, AlphaQ [ 33 ] scores experts with a heavy-tailed self-regularization exponent, and BitsMoE [ 39 ] allocates by spectral energy of an SVD factorization.
HAWQ [ 11 ] and HAWQ-V2 [ 10 ] allocate bit-widths by per-tensor Hessian-trace sensitivity, with the trace estimated by Hutchinson’s method on calibration data; § 4.8 compares RAM against HAWQ-V2 at matched bytes.
LLM-MQ [ 24 ] formulates mixed-precision allocation as an integer program with calibration-based sensitivities.
SliM-LLM [ 16 ] achieves group-wise mixed precision via salience-driven allocation, and CherryQ [ 7 ] identifies high-impact parameters; both use calibration data.
MC-MoE [ 15 ] combines expert quantization with dynamic pruning for MoE models, and Q-Strata [ 22 ] allocates across MoE blocks with a model-level objective evaluated on the assembled quantized model.
GAMMA [ 34 ] and MixQuant [ 28 ] share RAM’s goal of one offline analysis that serves any budget; GAMMA learns module preferences with a hidden-state reconstruction objective, and MixQuant marginalizes each layer’s distortion over random upstream quantization configurations.
CASA [ 35 ] shows that scalar sensitivity proxies in the multiple-choice knapsack formulation can distort inter-module rankings when the input and output Hessian factors are ill-conditioned; RAM’s propagated probes address the input side by using the network’s own upstream statistics.
LQER [ 36 ] applies an activation-induced scale matrix to drive the singular-value distribution of the quantization error toward a distribution that a low-rank correction can absorb.
Theorem 1 applies this machinery with A = Δ ⊤ ​ Δ A=\Delta^{\top}\Delta , where Δ \Delta is the quantization residual.
The Johnson–Lindenstrauss lemma [ 18 ] gives the same conclusion from the projection side: random projections preserve norm structure, so probe-based rankings track the true sensitivity ordering.

4 Experiments and Findings 4.1 Experimental Setup Quantization-aware distillation (QAD).
QATFactory simulates deployment-time quantization while performing matrix multiplications in BF16, allowing models to adapt to quantization noise without requiring training hardware that natively supports the target format; for example, it supports NVFP4 training on H100 GPUs, which lack FP4 Tensor Cores.
The framework supports NVFP4, MXFP4, and llama.cpp’s Q4_K format; dense and mixture-of-experts models; and both full-parameter and LoRA-based training.
With QATFactory, we conduct extensive experiments on models ranging from 8B to 230B parameters and evaluate exported checkpoints in production inference engines.
On Qwen3.5-9B, QAD achieves average benchmark accuracies of 68.9% under NVFP4 and 66.0% under MXFP4, outperforming the best PTQ results of 65.4% and 56.4%, respectively.
Through our experiments, we found that although both FP4 formats quantize weights and activations at deployment, the best training strategy is format-dependent: NVFP4 generally performs better when only weights are quantized during training, whereas MXFP4 benefits from quantizing both weights and activations.
For QAD of Qwen3.5-9B, rank-16 LoRA reduces GPU memory usage by 2.9 × 2.9\times , but increasing the rank does not close the quality gap with full-parameter QAD.
At a fixed training token budget, training on fewer 32K sequences improves average accuracy by 1.9 points over training on more 4K sequences.
Beyond distillation, NVFP4 QARL improves end-to-end training throughput by 1.23 × 1.23\times and average accuracy by 2.7 points over BF16 reinforcement learning followed by PTQ across five math benchmarks.
Figure 1: Comparison of post-training quantization (PTQ) and quantization-aware training (QAT) in QATFactory .
On Qwen3.5-9B, QATFactory produces higher-quality NVFP4 models than RTN and GPTQ on both LiveCodeBench and BigCodeBench.
On NVIDIA’s Blackwell generation, a single B200 GPU delivers peak Tensor Core throughput of 4.5 PFLOPS in BF16, 9 PFLOPS in FP8, and 18 PFLOPS in FP4 [ NVIDIA Corporation, 2024 ] .
1 1 1 Per-GPU datasheet figures with structured sparsity; dense throughput is half of each figure (2.25, 4.5, and 9 PFLOPS, respectively).
Each Blackwell package comprises two reticle-limited dies, but the 1:2:4 scaling across BF16, FP8, and FP4 holds under either convention.
Exploiting low-precision formats, particularly 4-bit formats such as NVFP4 and MXFP4 [ Rouhani et al., 2023 ; NVIDIA, 2025 ] , is therefore becoming a prerequisite for realizing the computational and economic value of modern accelerators.
However, whether such emerging 3-bit formats can preserve usable model quality will depend not only on codebook design and calibration data, but increasingly on whether models are explicitly trained to withstand their deployment numerics [ Ibarra et al., 2026 ] .
This separation works well at 8 bits and often remains adequate for weight-only 4-bit quantization.
It breaks down, however, at W4A4—the regime that actually unlocks FP4 tensor-core throughput.
Unlike W4A16, where only the weights incur a static perturbation while activations remain expressive, W4A4 injects error into both operands of every matrix multiplication.
FP4 provides little room to absorb this error: an E2M1 value occupies one of only 15 representable points over a coarse range before scaling, making substantial rounding and clipping unavoidable.
Such models generate thousands of autoregressive tokens, repeatedly encounter high-entropy branching decisions, and condition every subsequent prediction on their earlier outputs [ Liu et al., 2025 ; Lotfi et al., 2026 ; Marcuzzi et al., 2025 ] .
Quantization-aware distillation (QAD) further stabilizes this process by using the original high-precision model to supervise its quantized counterpart [ Jacob et al., 2018 ; Hinton et al., 2015 ; Liu et al., 2024 ; Kim et al., 2019 ; Xin et al., 2026 ] .
Existing open-source QAT pipelines [ PyTorch Team, 2024 ; Liu et al., 2024 ] largely target generic integer quantization schemes rather than the floating-point and engine-native formats used in practice.
Support for emerging formats such as NVFP4 and MXFP4, as well as engine-native formats such as Q4_K, remains underexplored.
We introduce QATFactory , a fully open framework for engine-aligned QAT and QAD across dense and mixture-of-experts (MoE) models [ Shazeer et al., 2017 ] .
QATFactory supports NVFP4, MXFP4, and llama.cpp’s Q4_K format [ Gerganov and contributors, 2023 ] , together with both full-parameter training and parameter-efficient QAT through low-rank adaptation (LoRA) [ Hu et al., 2022 ] .
Moreover, because QATFactory emulates the target arithmetic rather than executing it natively, it enables NVFP4 QAT on widely available H100 GPUs, despite their lack of NVFP4 Tensor Cores [ NVIDIA Corporation, 2025 ] .
We introduce an open framework spanning NVFP4, MXFP4, and Q4_K; dense and MoE architectures; and full-parameter and LoRA-based training.
By emulating target-format numerics while retaining high-precision computation, QATFactory enables NVFP4 QAT on widely available H100 GPUs and removes the requirement that training hardware natively support the deployment format. • New insights into low-precision optimization.
Across model families, architectural classes, and scales, QAD-trained checkpoints consistently outperform strong PTQ baselines and approach BF16 quality when evaluated directly in production inference engines.
2 Related Work Post-training quantization for LLMs.
GPTQ [ Frantar et al., 2022 ] uses approximate second-order information to minimize weight reconstruction error during quantization, while SmoothQuant [ Xiao et al., 2023 ] redistributes quantization difficulty between weights and activations to enable accurate low-precision inference.
AWQ [ Lin et al., 2024 ] further exploits activation statistics to preserve salient weight channels under low-bit weight quantization.
Recent studies show that quantization can disproportionately affect reasoning behavior, generation trajectories, and other model properties [ Liu et al., 2025 ; Lotfi et al., 2026 ; Marcuzzi et al., 2025 ] .
Classical QAT methods use fake quantization together with straight-through estimators to optimize continuous latent parameters under discretized forward computations [ Jacob et al., 2018 ] .
Subsequent work improves the optimization of quantized networks by learning activation clipping thresholds, as in PACT [ Choi et al., 2018 ] , and quantization step sizes, as in LSQ [ Esser et al., 2019 ] .
Quantization Noise [ Fan et al., 2020 ] further studies training under simulated quantization perturbations for extreme model compression.
Q-BERT [ Shen et al., 2020 ] studies ultra-low-precision quantization of BERT using Hessian information, while TernaryBERT [ Zhang et al., 2020 ] combines ultra-low-bit quantization with knowledge distillation to recover model quality.
I-BERT [ Kim et al., 2021 ] further demonstrates integer-only inference for Transformer models by quantizing both linear and nonlinear operations.
For LLMs, LLM-QAT [ Liu et al., 2024 ] extends this paradigm through data-free distillation and studies joint quantization of weights, activations, and KV caches.
BitDistiller [ Du et al., 2024 ] further demonstrates that self-distillation can substantially improve the robustness of extremely low-bit LLMs.
More recently, Xin et al. [2026] study quantization-aware distillation (QAD) specifically for NVFP4 and show that a high-precision teacher can recover substantial accuracy lost during FP4 deployment.
EfficientQAT [ Chen et al., 2025a ] develops efficient training procedures for low-bit LLM adaptation, while QA-LoRA [ Xu et al., 2024b ] combines quantization awareness with low-rank adaptation to reduce the trainable parameter and memory footprint.
LR-QAT [ Bondarenko et al., 2024 ] introduces a memory-efficient low-rank QAT formulation for LLMs that produces fully quantized models without additional inference overhead, while L4Q [ Jeon et al., 2025 ] integrates low-rank adaptation with QAT for parameter-efficient quantization-aware fine-tuning.
ParetoQ [ Liu et al., 2026 ] investigates optimization regimes across extremely low-bit quantization settings, and Chen et al. [2025b] study how QAT performance varies with model scale, training data, and quantization configuration.
The emergence of native FP4 hardware has motivated increasing interest in training models directly under FP4 numerical constraints.
NVFP4 combines E2M1 values with fine-grained block scaling and a higher-level tensor scale to improve the accuracy of FP4 computation [ NVIDIA, 2025 ] , while microscaling formats such as MXFP4 use shared block-level scales to enable efficient low-precision arithmetic [ Rouhani et al., 2023 ] .
Recent work has demonstrated large-scale pretraining with NVFP4 [ Abecassis et al., 2025 ] and MXFP4 [ Cim et al., 2026 ] , while Full-Stack FP4 [ Ding et al., 2026 ] extends FP4 computation across multiple components of the training stack.
QuRL [ Li et al., 2026 ] studies low-precision reinforcement learning for efficient reasoning, while QeRL [ Huang et al., 2026 ] combines quantization with reinforcement learning to improve training efficiency and investigates quantization as more than a purely deployment-time compression mechanism.
QaRL [ Gu et al., 2026 ] studies rollout-aligned quantization-aware reinforcement learning and addresses the numerical mismatch between low-precision rollout generation and policy optimization.
More recently, Miles [ Chen et al., 2026b ] presents a production-level post-training system that includes low-precision training and rollout workflows, with explicit attention to consistency between training-time quantization, weight synchronization, and low-precision inference.
3 QATFactory QATFactory is an open-source framework for adapting pretrained large language models (LLMs) to deployment-native quantization formats through quantization-aware training (QAT) or distillation.
It simulates quantization and dequantization during training while performing all matrix multiplications in BF16.
This design allows models to be trained for low-bit formats, such as NVFP4, MXFP4, and Q4_K, without requiring specialized hardware.
3.1 Framework overview and design goals Figure 2: Overview of QATFactory. (a) Quantization-aware distillation (QAD): a frozen high-precision teacher supervises a quantization-aware student initialized from the same pretrained checkpoint. (b) Quantization-aware reinforcement learning (QARL): a low-precision inference engine generates rollouts, reward functions or verifiers evaluate them, and updated weights are synchronized back to the rollout engine.

We audit 27 quantized artifacts across 13 families and four architecture backbones, 0.35B–14B parameters, evaluated down their published ladder to Q2_K ( ∼ 2.6 {\sim}2.6 bits per weight), on 429 frequency-validated rare English words under two probes: surface inclusion of a prompt-supplied word and its one-sentence definition, scored by a tiered multi-synonym matcher, its error measured by a blind LLM-judge census of every definition, with human verification.
In every sub-2B artifact, definitions fall 20 20 – 67 % 67\% below the artifact’s baseline, typically several times the inclusion loss.
The damage is frequency-graded, provider-dependent, and not calibrated by WikiText-2 perplexity: across nine artifact-matched ladders, near-identical Q2 penalties ( 44.7 % 44.7\% / 47.6 % 47.6\% ) separate an artifact keeping its definitions ( 3.6 % 3.6\% ) from one losing them ( 43.6 % 43.6\% ).
1 Introduction In self-hosted use, publicly available open-weight large language models (LLMs) are routinely deployed on consumer hardware after post-training quantization (PTQ), often as a default deployment format ( Ollama, 2026b ) .
GGUF is a binary file format from the llama.cpp ecosystem ( Gerganov et al., 2023–2026 ) for storing quantized model weights and metadata, with K-quants as its family of mixed-precision quantization schemes.
The default “pull” command used by Ollama, ollama pull model_name , fetches a q4_K_M quantized variant of the Llama-3.2-3B, Mistral-7B, and Qwen2.5-7B models ( Ollama, 2026b ) , which are amongst the 27 artifacts used in this study.
Model cards ( Mitchell et al., 2019 ) are the standard route by which vendors provide performance and evaluation metrics about a model.
In this study we fill that gap in model evaluation, and present a failure mode that is invisible to the aggregate benchmarks vendors release, and that the WikiText-2 perplexity scores an AI practitioner deploying those models currently trusts can register without identifying.
This regularity in the frequency distribution of words is Zipf’s law ( Zipf, 1949 ) , and we use its frequency scale to select and stratify the rare-word set in Sec.
Vocabulary size has separately been argued to be an under-allocated dimension of model scaling, with compute-optimal vocabularies considerably larger than the ones most current models use ( Tao et al., 2024 ) .
Our data show no support for this hypothesis: tokenizer vocabulary size does not predict the model’s definitional degradation (Spearman ρ = 0.12 \rho{=}0.12 across non-collapsed artifacts) while parameter count is the dominant predictor of definitional robustness ( ρ = 0.72 \rho{=}0.72 ).
Ali et al. trained 24 models at a 2.6B parameter scale, one for each of 24 tokenizers, and tested whether the standard intrinsic measures of tokenizer quality predicted how the resulting models performed on downstream tasks.
They found such measures not consistently predictive, and described them as a questionable proxy for downstream performance ( Ali et al., 2024 ) .
Specifically, we show that 1. this second failure mode is the one that actually occurs in small models under aggressive GGUF quantization, 2. it concentrates on low-frequency rare-word vocabulary, 3. the standard perplexity curve tracks it but cannot calibrate it, and 4. it varies between quantized builds of the same model.

Evidence from nine artifact-matched perplexity ladders (0.5B–7B, including two robust controls, one unusable build, and a dual-build pair) shows that WikiText-2 perplexity tracks the failure but cannot calibrate it.
Perplexity co-moves with overall damage severity, but supplies neither a threshold nor a calibration: near-identical Q2 penalties of + 47.6 % +47.6\% and + 44.7 % +44.7\% separate an artifact that loses 43.6 % 43.6\% of its definitions from one that loses just 3.6 % 3.6\% .

In total the sweep produced ≈ \approx 98,400 word-level definition judgements and ≈ \approx 295,200 inclusion generations ( ≈ \approx 393,600 scored outputs), all on a single GPU within a 12–16 GB VRAM budget (RTX 3060, 12 GB; Kaggle T4, 16 GB) 2 Related Work Quantization methods and their evaluation.
Post-training quantization of large transformers became practical with LLM.int8() ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ) .
It holds a small number of outlier feature dimensions in 16-bit and quantizes the rest of the multiplication to 8-bit.
The GGUF K-quant family ( Gerganov et al., 2023–2026 ) uses block-wise mixed-precision compression for consumer inference and deployment.
Systematic evaluations establish that 4-bit ( Kurt, 2026 ) weights generally can preserve aggregate performance with little degradation on aggregate metrics while 2-bit ( Li et al., 2024 ) causes substantially larger losses.
Zhou et al. (2026b) fitted a task-stratified scaling law to 293 quantized configurations of Qwen3 and Llama-3, with separate exponents for model size, bit-width, group size and calibration-set size.
Overall degradation was modest, and 4-bit models did not consistently perform worse than 8-bit models.
In the model-parameter range comparable to our study, a pruning-versus-quantization comparison across six models from 0.5B to 3.8B ( Zhou et al., 2025 ) recommends quantization as the safer choice: at 4-bit it costs at most 5.6 % 5.6\% accuracy, while 75 % 75\% sparsity pruning shows losses up to 48.07 % 48.07\% .
Both studies went down to 4-bit quantization and used closed-form answers.
Hooker et al. (2019) ; Hooker et al. (2020) found that pruned and quantized vision models showed comparable accuracy to full-precision counterparts, while diverging considerably on a narrow, underrepresented subset of examples.
Wu et al. (2026) report the opposite direction for factual knowledge: in Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-8B and Gemma-2-9B under eleven compression methods, questions about popular entities lost more of their baseline accuracy than questions about rare ones.
In Japanese, automatic benchmarks recorded a 1.7 % 1.7\% average drop, while human evaluators scoring realistic prompts reported 16.0 % 16.0\% , an order of magnitude larger.
The same gap has been measured for quantization alone, at scale, in social-bias evaluation: 50 quantized models across 13 bias datasets ( Hua et al., 2026 ) .
For compressed LLMs specifically, the LLM-KICK benchmark ( Jaiswal et al., 2024 ) shows that models compressed with negligible perplexity change can lose significant knowledge-intensive ability.
Duan (2026) is closest to our method, where perplexity missed underlying feature damage which they measured by degradation in active SAE features in weight-quantized models; and Hooker’s is closest to our thesis: a behavioural, aggregate-metric-invisible subset effect.
A systematic on-device evaluation of GGUF quantization ( Song et al., 2025 ) finds that quantization tolerance depends sharply on scale: from Q5_K to Q3_K a 14B model holds GSM8K accuracy at 89 % 89\% while a 1.5B model falls from 59.1 % 59.1\% to 46.5 % 46.5\% , and code generation is particularly sensitive to compression.
Recent work documents disproportionate loss of low-resource-language ability under aggressive quantization in machine translation ( Marie & Fujita, 2025 ) .
Patodiya (2026) audited 305 quantized artifacts in Ollama’s official library and found five that fail every functional test: four Qwen2.5-Coder-3B conversions and one Phi-3.5-mini conversion.
More recent work ( Ide et al., 2026 ) has formalized the approach of definitional generation and its evaluation with lexicographic criteria and LLM-as-judge scoring.
Finally, subword tokenization ( Sennrich et al., 2016 ; Kudo & Richardson, 2018 ) is the motivation behind the fragmentation hypothesis we test: rare words that are split into more subword tokens might be more susceptible to definitional degradation as the model is quantized.
Importantly, perplexity can improve while a significant fraction of features actually degrade: on Gemma-2-2B, INT7 improves perplexity while 18.7% of active features degrade.
Probe B thus produces one deterministic response per word ( n = 429 n{=}429 ), which allowed us to do exact word-level paired comparisons across quantization levels.
3.3 Scoring and scorer validation Probe A scores verbatim (case-insensitive word-boundary) inclusion of the word supplied through the prompt.
It is blind to morphology ( exhausted counts, exhaustion does not), and it visibly undercounted correct definitions, producing full-precision baselines of only 0.19–0.46.
We flagged this scorer because some artifacts (Llama-3.2-3B, Gemma-2-9B) moved in the opposite direction on common-word control (definitional accuracy increased with increasing quantization), compared to the rare-word experiment.
This was flagged because of the difference between Probe A inclusion success vs Probe B definitional success, where Probe A was above 0.90 for twenty of the twenty-four artifacts with a full-precision baseline, but Probe B started below 0.57 for all twenty-four, and below 50 percent for nineteen of them.
It raised the baselines at full precision to 0.62–0.94 (from 0.27–0.56 at Tier 1), consistent with models genuinely knowing most of the words at full precision.
Using an LLM as an evaluator of generated text is established practice ( Zheng et al., 2023 ) , including specifically for judging definition quality ( Ide et al., 2026 ) .
We ran a census in which LLM judges graded every definition in the study (98,409 definitions from the 257 file-level Probe B evaluations of Sec.
3.4 , each one artifact at one quantization level).

Each tile stores one selected representation, while phase-specialized kernels reuse the packed model and expand lower-bit weights for INT8 computation with 8-bit activations.
Across four LLMs spanning 3B to 14B parameters, AlignQuant achieves up to 2.50 × 2.50\times generation speedup over BF16 while preserving model quality.
Evaluations further cover three GPUs and contexts up to 64K tokens.
1 Introduction Large language model (LLM) inference is increasingly constrained by parameter storage, memory traffic, and computation.
Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces these costs through lower-precision representations, while the resulting quantization error makes accuracy preservation a central challenge ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ; Frantar et al., 2022 ; Lin et al., 2024b ) .
Uniform-bit quantization addresses this challenge by improving the quantized representation at a fixed weight precision ( Frantar et al., 2022 ; Lin et al., 2024b ) .
Mixed-precision quantization additionally exploits differences in sensitivity by assigning higher precision to sensitive weight regions under a weight-storage budget ( Chen et al., 2024 ; Huang et al., 2024 ; Zheng et al., 2026 ; Li et al., 2026 ; Nie et al., 2026 ; Hooper et al., 2025 ) .
Figure 1 illustrates how precision allocation, weight representation, and GPU execution jointly shape model quality and inference efficiency.
Channel- and output-feature-level methods maintain regular partitions by sharing one bit-width across each channel or output feature, coupling weights with different sensitivities to the same precision choice ( Chen et al., 2024 ; Zheng et al., 2026 ) .
Finer-grained approaches expose more local choices through quantization groups and hardware-aligned blocks, together with reordering, mixed formats, or specialized execution support ( Huang et al., 2024 ; Li et al., 2026 ; Nie et al., 2026 ; Hooper et al., 2025 ) .
System-oriented quantizers co-design low-bit formats with weight layouts, dequantization, and GPU data paths to control these deployment costs ( Zhao et al., 2024 ; Lin et al., 2025 ) .
Figure 1: Comparison of precision allocation, representation, and GPU execution.
Colors denote 8-bit/4-bit weight precision, and purple outlines track logical tiles.
The quality panel reports WikiText-2 PPL increase over BF16, and the right panel reports generation throughput across prompt lengths on Llama-3.2-3B.
At a matched 4-bit tile fraction, two-dimensional tile allocation improves WikiText-2 perplexity over the output-row control.
Second, the selected mixed-bit weights must retain their compact format in GPU memory and during loading while supporting integer computation tailored to each phase’s workload ( Lin et al., 2025 ) .
Joint loss-aware calibration assigns 4-bit or 8-bit precision under a model-wide budget, giving higher-precision priority to tiles important to either phase.
Packing stores one selected representation per tile, preserving 4-bit weights in compact form through storage and loading.
Across Llama-3.2-3B, Qwen3-4B, Ministral-3-8B, and Qwen3-14B, AlignQuant achieves maximum 2.50 × 2.50\times inference speedups over BF16 on an NVIDIA A100, NVIDIA H100 NVL, and RTX 4090 GPUs while preserving model quality.
Joint Prefill/Decode Allocation: We develop a loss-aware rule that scores local projection-output changes from 8-to-4-bit substitutions under 8-bit activations.
Joint prefill/decode calibration produces one precision map under a model-wide storage budget, prioritizing 8-bit retention for tiles important to either phase.
2 Related Work Mixed-precision LLM quantization couples three design dimensions: the granularity at which precision varies, the representation used to realize heterogeneous precision, and the numerical transformations used to reduce quantization error.
Layer-wise methods reduce the number of precision decisions and preserve regular operators, while coupling regions of different sensitivity to one bit-width across each layer ( Lee et al., 2025 ; Zhao et al., 2025 ) .
Channel-, output-feature-, and group-wise methods capture finer heterogeneity through structured units, coupling the weights within each unit to one precision and, under layer-local budgets, constraining high-precision placement ( Chen et al., 2024 ; Huang et al., 2024 ; Zheng et al., 2026 ; Harma et al., 2026 ) .
Block-level schemes expose more local choices through hardware-aligned partitions, using global budgets, Fisher-weighted perturbation, or outlier-aware clustering for bit-width selection ( Li et al., 2026 ; Nie et al., 2026 ; Hooper et al., 2025 ; Xie et al., 2025 ) .
Phase-adaptive methods vary precision across inference phases and generation steps, adding runtime selection among quantized weight representations ( Chen et al., 2025 ) .
Low-bit systems co-design numerical formats, packing, data movement, and kernels for efficient GPU computation ( Dettmers et al., 2022 ; Zhao et al., 2024 ; Lin et al., 2025 ; Hu et al., 2025a ) .
Within this category, uniform weight formats couple sensitive and less sensitive regions to the same bit-width ( Lin et al., 2025 ; Hu et al., 2025a ) .
Executable mixed-precision systems organize heterogeneous weights through output-feature partitions, reordered blocks, expert groups, or hardware-supported formats, tying their precision choices to corresponding layouts, indexing, or arithmetic paths ( Zheng et al., 2026 ; Nie et al., 2026 ; Duanmu et al., 2025 ; Liu et al., 2026 ; Jang and Tambe, 2025 ) .
Representation-centric methods use sparse outliers, residual subspaces, reusable binary codes, or fractional-bit quantizers to improve fidelity or precision flexibility, with corresponding indexed accesses, auxiliary products, or specialized reconstruction ( Dettmers et al., 2023 ; Saxena et al., 2024 ; Park et al., 2026 ; Lee and Song, 2026 ) .
Scaling methods protect salient weights or redistribute quantization difficulty between weights and activations, with flexibility limited to feature-wise rescaling ( Xiao et al., 2023 ; Lin et al., 2024b ) .
Rotation- and permutation-based methods redistribute outliers across feature dimensions, while transforms spanning precision partitions change the weights assigned to each quantized region ( Ashkboos et al., 2024 ; Liu et al., 2025 ; Lin et al., 2024a ) .
Affine and jointly optimized scaling and orthogonal transformations offer richer distribution shaping, while spatial bit-width placement remains a separate decision ( Ma et al., 2024 ; Hu et al., 2025b ; Sun et al., 2024 ) .
Runtime smoothing adapts channel scales during inference to control dynamic ranges, while loss-sensitive weight placement requires a separate allocation criterion ( Yi et al., 2025 ) .
Purple outlines track one logical weight tile. (1) Candidates.
Tile-aligned U / V U/V transforms prepare 4-bit and 8-bit weight candidates. (2) Joint scoring.
The budget assigns 4-bit precision to the lowest-scoring tiles, storing one selected representation and scale per tile. (4) Direct execution.
Each tile uses 4-bit or 8-bit weights, while uniform 8-bit activations provide a common input format for hardware-accelerated INT8 computation.

We evaluate each replacement of 8-bit weights with 4-bit weights through its local projection-output change, weighted by gradients of the full model loss.
3.1 Tile-Level Precision Assignment We introduce offline precision allocation to select 8-to-4-bit replacements with the smallest estimated impact under a storage budget .
The allocation leverages per-tile 4-bit and 8-bit candidates, multi-phase activation and gradient evidence, and a target 4-bit fraction.
Let ρ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \rho\in[0,1] denote the target fraction of 4-bit tiles.
Since all tiles contain the same number of weights, each 8-to-4-bit replacement saves the same storage.
Thus, the budget determines how many tiles use 4-bit weights, while the scores determine which ones .
As shown in Figure 2 (1), candidate construction applies orthogonal input and output transformations ( V V along K K , U U along N N ) to redistribute outliers and minimize quantization error ( Ashkboos et al., 2024 ; Liu et al., 2025 ) , yielding symmetric 4-bit and 8-bit candidates per tile.
Let W ^ 4 , t \widehat{W}_{4,t} and W ^ 8 , t \widehat{W}_{8,t} denote the reconstructed 4-bit and 8-bit candidates of the same transformed tile.
Calibration and inference use the same 8-bit activation quantizer, so candidate comparisons reflect deployed inputs (Appendices B.2 and F.1 ).
Joint scoring in panel (2) of Figure 2 begins by measuring the local projection-output change caused by replacing tile t t ’s 8-bit candidate with its 4-bit candidate.
We write X ^ t p , c \widehat{X}_{t}^{p,c} for the transformed 8-bit activations reconstructed with their scales.
Holding these activations and the other weight-tile candidates fixed, the substitution produces Δ ​ Y ~ t p , c = X ^ t p , c ​ ( W ^ 4 , t − W ^ 8 , t ) ⊤ . \Delta\widetilde{Y}_{t}^{p,c}=\widehat{X}_{t}^{p,c}\left(\widehat{W}_{4,t}-\widehat{W}_{8,t}\right)^{\top}. (1) The matrix product combines candidate errors across the tile’s input dimensions, capturing how they reinforce or cancel in the projection output .
Figure 3: Tile-level sensitivity of query-projection weights in Llama-3.2-3B at three decoder depths.
We weight squared output perturbations by squared loss gradients using the diagonal empirical Fisher ( Hooper et al., 2025 ) .
A larger F t p F_{t}^{p} gives tile t t higher priority for retaining 8-bit weights in phase p p .
Figure 3 shows local sensitivity variation within query-projection weights at three decoder depths, illustrating the value of assigning precision to individual tiles.
Calibration forward and gradient-capture details are given in Appendix C.1 , and phase-specific observation construction in Appendix C.2 .
F_{t}=\max_{p\in\{\mathrm{pre},\mathrm{dec}\}}\frac{F_{t}^{p}}{\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}}F_{t^{\prime}}^{p}}. (3) Each ratio expresses tile t t ’s share of one phase’s total sensitivity score.
A tile with high relative sensitivity in either phase therefore receives higher priority for retaining 8-bit weights.

H Additional sensitivity analysis I Budget trade-off, sensitivity, and allocations I.1 Downstream task scores License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2610.09183v1 [cs.LG] 06 Oct 2026 Q-PACE: Dynamic Precision Allocation for Quantization-Aware Training Alexandra Volkova † † thanks: Equal contribution.
Pretraining and supervised fine-tuning experiments on LLMs of up to 4B parameters show that Q-PACE consistently improves over existing mixed-precision training recipes, and achieves comparable loss at substantially lower total memory budgets.
1 Introduction Quantization-aware training (QAT) methods ( Courbariaux et al., 2016 ; Jacob et al., 2018 ) use low-precision arithmetic to lower the cost of both deploying LLMs, as they result in lower-bitwidth accurate models, and training them, as the matrix multiplications during training can be mapped to lower precision.
Recent recipes make end-to-end NVFP4 and MXFP4 pretraining viable by leveraging techniques such as block (micro) scaling, stochastic rounding, rotations, and unbiased gradient estimators ( Castro et al., 2026 ; Chen et al., 2026b ; NVIDIA et al., 2026 ) .
However, there is still a clear loss gap between lower-precision approaches and BF16 and MXFP8 training, and uniform four-bit training can be lossy or even become unstable over long horizons ( NVIDIA et al., 2026 ) .
Moreover, modern microscaling formats allow matrix products across various precisions, or even mixed-precision operands ( Rouhani et al., 2023 ) , so the bitwidth of each linear layer can be chosen separately, assuming a global budget constraint.
Current approaches either keep a fixed set of layers, typically the last few, in higher precision ( NVIDIA et al., 2026 ) , or rank layers by a local proxy, such as the product of gradient norm and quantization error ( Pan et al., 2026 ) .
This gives a training-time analogue of the “linearity theorem” model of Malinovskii et al. (2025) , previously used for one-shot, post-training quantization.
We evaluate Q-PACE on pretraining experiments for models between 350M and 4B parameters, and on supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3 models, against state-of-the-art baselines ( NVIDIA et al., 2026 ; Pan et al., 2026 ) , at budgets between 5.0 and 6.5 bits per weight.
In pretraining, it achieves the lowest final loss in every setting we evaluate, recovering up to 81 % 81\% of the loss gap between uniform NVFP4 and MXFP8 training; the same trend holds for supervised fine-tuning. • Q-PACE provides a substantially better quality–precision trade-off.
At a fixed precision budget, Q-PACE produces better final loss than both SNIP and the NVIDIA heuristic, while at matched loss it requires fewer bits per model parameter (Figure 1 ). • A detailed analysis of the produced allocations shows that quantization sensitivity is highly structured across the network: it depends on both layer type and depth, and its profile evolves over the course of training.
As a result, only a small number of reallocations (on the order of 10 over a full training run) is sufficient, keeping the overhead of Q-PACE low in practice.
Figure 1: Final loss improvement for a 500M model trained with mixed precision across methods and budgets.
We improve over last layers in BF16 ( NVIDIA et al., 2026 ) and SNIP ( Pan et al., 2026 ) .
2 The Problem: QAT with Adaptive Mixed-Precision Our goal is to choose the quantization levels of n n layers in a network during quantization-aware training.
We distinguish between formats even if they nominally have the same number of bits but use a different value representation, such as FP8 E8M0 and FP8 E4M3.
For a sequence B = ( b 1 , … , b n ) ∈ β n B=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\in\beta^{n} , called bitwidth allocation , we denote its loss by L ( W , B ) := L ( { Q ( W i , b i ) for b i ∈ B } ) L(W,B):=L(\{Q(W_{i},b_{i})\text{ for }b_{i}\in B\}) , and its resource cost by C ⁡ ( W , B ) C(W,B) .
With the goal to constraint memory at inference time, we take d ⁡ ( W i ) = : d i d(W_{i})=:d_{i} as the number of elements in a tensor W i W_{i} , and κ ⁡ ( b i ) \kappa(b_{i}) as the number of bits required to store an element, including the format’s block scales (4.5 bits for NVFP4; 6.25 and 8.25 bits for MXFP6 and MXFP8; 16 bits for BF16), which we write as b i b_{i} again, meaning that C ⁡ ( W , B ) = ∑ i = 1 n d i ​ b i C(W,B)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}b_{i} measures the total model size in bits.
Ultimately, for a model with set of weights W W , the problem of optimal bitwidth allocation is minimization of the loss under a given maximum model size constraint, C 0 C_{0} .
B^{\ast}=\argmin_{B=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})}\,L(W,B)\text{ subject to }\sum_{i}b_{i}d_{i}\leq C_{0}. (1) For post-training quantization, the bitwidth allocation only has to be chosen once.
In this work, however, we focus on QAT, where the choice of quantization has a direct influence on the quality of learned weights, and one cannot assume that the best quantization strategy is constant over the course of training ( Liu et al., 2025b ) .
Consequently, we are interested in finding a sequence of allocations B 1 , … , B T B^{1},\dots,B^{T} , where T T is the number of training steps, that ultimately achieves the lowest final loss while adhering to the cost budget at each iteration.
Then the optimal sequence of allocations during training should be ( B 1 , … , B T ) ∗ ≔ argmin all trajectories ​ ( B t ) L ​ ( W T ​ ( B 1 , … , B T − 1 ) , B T ) ​ subject to ​ max t = 1 , … , T ⁡ C ⁡ ( B t ) ≤ C 0 . (B^{1},\dots,B^{T})^{\ast}\coloneqq\!\!\!\argmin_{\text{all trajectories }(B^{t})}\!\!\!L(W^{T}(B^{1},\dots,B^{T-1}),B^{T})\text{ subject to }\max_{t=1,\dots,T}\!\!C(B_{t})\leq C_{0}. (2) Due to the complex structure with interactions between layers as well as time steps, a direct optimization of Eq. ( 2 ) is intractable.
Moreover, we assume the errors to be uncorrelated between layers, 𝔼 ⁡ [ δ ​ W i ​ δ ​ W j ] = 0 \mathbb{E}[\delta W_{i}\delta W_{j}]=0 for 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n 1\leq i<j\leq n .
Assuming this, we can take the expectation over δ ​ W i \delta W_{i} in Eq. ( 3 ) to obtain: 𝔼 ⁡ [ L ⁡ ( W + δ ​ W ) − L ⁡ ( W ) ] \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[L(W+\delta W)-L(W)\Bigr] ≈ 1 2 ∑ i Tr ( H ii ) σ i 2 = 1 2 ∑ i Tr ( H ii ) 1 d i ​ 𝔼 ​ ‖ δ ​ W i ‖ F 2 ⏟ = MSE ⁡ ( Q ⁡ ( W i ) , W i ) , \displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\;\Tr(H_{ii})\,\sigma_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\;\Tr(H_{ii})\underbrace{\frac{1}{d_{i}}\mathbb{E}||\delta W_{i}||^{2}_{F}}_{=\operatorname{MSE}(Q(W_{i}),W_{i})}, (4) where d i d_{i} denotes the number of elements in the i i -th weight matrix, and H i ​ i H_{ii} denotes the Hessian matrix of L L with respect to these weights.
The derivation presented above can be done for any precision low ; thus, we obtain, for any bitwidth allocation: L ⁡ ( W , B ) ≈ L high + ∑ i α i ​ MSE ^ ​ ( Q ⁡ ( W i , B i ) , W i ) , L(W,B)\approx L_{\texttt{high}}+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\,\widehat{\operatorname{MSE}}(Q(W_{i},B_{i}),W_{i}), (5) where L high = L ⁡ ( W ) L_{\texttt{high}}=L(W) is the loss over full-precision weights, and α i \alpha_{i} is an estimate of 1 2 Tr ( H ii ) \frac{1}{2}\Tr(H_{ii}) that can be interpreted as the aggregated curvature of a layer.
To estimate α i \alpha_{i} , we use that Q ⁡ ( W i , b i ) = W i Q(W_{i},b_{i})=W_{i} and MSE ⁡ ( Q ⁡ ( W i , b i ) , W i ) = 0 \operatorname{MSE}(Q(W_{i},b_{i}),W_{i})=0 for the layers that are kept in full precision ( b i = high b_{i}=\texttt{high} ), so effectively Eq. ( 5 ) sums only over those layers that are quantized.

In principle, Eq. ( 6 ) yields α 1 , … , α n \alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n} from n + 1 n+1 forward passes over 𝒟 \mathcal{D} , but we note that using several noise perturbations per layer with different variances improves our loss estimation.
Once we have determined sensitivities α i \alpha_{i} for all i = 1 , … , n i=1,\dots,n , we can solve Eq. ( 1 ) with Eq. ( 5 ) as a proxy for the intractable L ⁡ ( W , B ) L(W,B) .
For our implementation we use pseudo-polynomial dynamic programming ( Pisinger, 1995 ) : the budget C 0 C_{0} is discretized into M M bins, and each layer cost d i ​ b i d_{i}b_{i} is rounded up to the whole number of bins.
In our experiments, re-allocation periods lower than 5B training tokens did not yield measurable improvement.
In contrast, gradient-based methods ( Pan et al., 2026 ) require storing the activations and gradients needed for the backward pass, resulting in a substantially larger memory overhead.
One recalibration costs m ​ n + 1 mn+1 forward passes over 𝒟 \mathcal{D} : one unperturbed pass for L high L_{\texttt{high}} and one per (layer, noise level) pair.
4 Experimental Results 4.1 Experimental Setup We train a decoder-only Transformer ( Vaswani et al., 2017 ) , with each block following an OLMo-2-style ( OLMo: et al., 2025 ) : multi-head self-attention and a two-layer MLP, with RMSNorm ( Zhang and Sennrich, 2019 ) applied to each sub-layer output before the residual.
We start our experiments with a 350M model on 18B tokens and evaluate all the strategies, then select the best-performing ones for larger up to 4B model runs.
We follow the e2e QAT setup by Panferov et al. (2026) , which performs all of the weights-activations matmul and gradients-weight/activations matmuls in FP4.
In our experiments, the candidate low precision formats for matmix multiplications are NVFP4, MXFP6, MXFP8.
We compare Q-PACE against uniform NVFP4, MXFP8, the last-layers strategy of NVIDIA et al. (2026) , and SNIP ( Pan et al., 2026 ) .
4.2 Forward Error Is Sufficient for Loss Estimation Before we report our method’s performance, we provide a justification for our choice of modeling the quantization error purely as a function of the MSE \operatorname{MSE} , i.e. weight quantization error, Eq. ( 4 ).
Other mixed-precision training methods model loss degradation using the error introduced during the backward pass ( Pan et al., 2026 ) .
For this, we compare two training settings for a 500M model trained on 74B tokens, with various precision allocation methods.
In the second, the precision assignment is applied only to the forward matmuls, while all the backward matmuls are computed in FP4 (FP4 bwd).

When forward and backward precisions are the same per layer, the correlations are r = 0.98 r=0.98 for our model.
Keeping the backward pass uniformly in FP4 does not change this, and the corresponding Pearson correlation remains r = 0.98 r=0.98 .
Budget equals target average bits per parameter. “Rec.” (recovery) is the share of the FP4-to-FP8 gap recovered, ( L FP4 − L ) / ( L FP4 − L FP8 ) (L_{\text{FP4}}-L)/(L_{\text{FP4}}-L_{\text{FP8}}) .
The column “gain” reports the ratio between the gain of Q-PACE over FP4 and the gain of the NVIDIA allocation over FP4, ( L FP4 − L Q-PACE ) / ( L FP4 − L NVIDIA ) (L_{\text{FP4}}-L_{\text{Q-PACE }})/(L_{\text{FP4}}-L_{\text{NVIDIA}}) .

Across all model scales, precision budgets, and candidate precision sets, Q-PACE achieves lower final test loss than both the NVIDIA heuristic and SNIP ( Pan et al., 2026 ) .
Q-PACE recovers substantially more of the FP4-to-FP8 loss gap than other methods, up to 81 % 81\% of the gap.
The benefit of sensitivity-aware allocation is particularly clear when more than 2 precision levels are available.
At the same 6.5 6.5 -bit budget, replacing the bitwidth set { 4 , 16 } \{4,16\} with { 4 , 8 , 16 } \{4,8,16\} increases the recovered gap of Q-PACE from 43 % 43\% to 75 % 75\% for the 350M model and from 48 % 48\% to 81 % 81\% for the 500M model.
