A.3 Task-Specific Calibration Protocol Why task-specific calibration?
1 Introduction Figure 1: Motivation for budget-aware precision allocation under a fixed KV cache budget.
As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly integral to long-context reasoning, complex question answering, and multi-step planning, their growing inference-time memory footprint has emerged as a critical bottleneck to practical deployment Xiao et al. (2024) ; Kwon et al. (2023) ; Liu et al. (2026) .
In long-sequence generation, the Key–Value (KV) cache constitutes a dominant source of GPU memory consumption, making efficient KV cache compression a central challenge for scalable and cost-effective LLM inference Zhang et al. (2026) ; Wang et al. (2026) .
KIVI Liu et al. (2024) introduces asymmetric low-bit quantization by applying channel-level quantization to keys and token-level quantization to values, substantially reducing KV cache memory consumption.
Subsequent token-oriented methods further improve this direction, with ZipCache He et al. (2024) identifying salient tokens and OTT Su et al. (2025) tracing and preserving outlier tokens at higher precision to reduce token quantization errors.
In contrast, KVTuner Li et al. (2025) and PM-KVQ Liu et al. (2026) allocate mixed precision according to layer or block sensitivity, thereby reducing quantization errors caused by layer heterogeneity.
Recent multi-granularity methods, such as KVmix Li et al. (2026) , further explore the joint adaptation of layer-wise precision and token-level preservation by incorporating layer importance and pivotal context selection.
We conduct extensive experiments on reasoning and long-context benchmarks, including GSM8K, GPQA, and LongBench-v2, using models ranging from 7B to 32B parameters, including Llama-3-8B-Instruct Grattafiori et al. (2024) , Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3 Jiang et al. (2023) , DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B/14B DeepSeek-AI (2025) , and QwQ-32B Qwen Team (2025) .
2 Related Work KV Cache Quantization and Precision Allocation.
Token-aware approaches, such as ZipCache He et al. (2024) and OTT Su et al. (2025) , preserve salient tokens at higher precision to mitigate token quantization errors.
Layer-wise methods, including KVTuner Li et al. (2025) and PM-KVQ Liu et al. (2026) , allocate mixed precision according to layer sensitivity.
KVmix Li et al. (2026) further explores layer- and token-level adaptation, while KV-COBRA Ha et al. (2026) studies budget-aware allocation of compression configurations under fixed memory constraints.
MoE-nD Sun et al. (2026) extends KV compression to multiple dimensions by jointly considering quantization and token reduction.
PagedAttention Kwon et al. (2023) improves memory efficiency through virtual memory paging, while subsequent approaches such as H2O Zhang et al. (2023) , StreamingLLM Xiao et al. (2024) , SqueezeAttention Wang et al. (2024) , XKV Li et al. (2024) , and KVPR Jiang et al. (2025) reduce memory pressure through token eviction, retention, or offloading strategies.
3 Preliminary 3.1 Generative Inference and KV Cache Transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) generate text autoregressively in two phases: prefilling and decoding .
3.2 KV Cache Quantization To alleviate this bottleneck, Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) compresses the KV cache into low-bit representations.
For a tensor 𝐗 \mathbf{X} (representing either 𝐊 \mathbf{K} or 𝐕 \mathbf{V} ), uniform affine quantization maps floating-point values to b b -bit integers: 𝐗 ¯ = clamp ​ ( ⌊ 𝐗 s + z ⌉ , 0 , 2 b − 1 ) , \bar{\mathbf{X}}=\text{clamp}\left(\left\lfloor\frac{\mathbf{X}}{s}+z\right\rceil,0,2^{b}-1\right), (4) where s s and z z denote the scale factor and zero-point, respectively.
The quantization error ℰ = ‖ 𝐗 − 𝐗 ^ ‖ F 2 \mathcal{E}=\|\mathbf{X}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}\|_{F}^{2} stems primarily from rounding and outlier clamping.
However, this overlooks the heterogeneous sensitivity of KV cache representations: (1) certain layers are more robust to quantization noise and can tolerate lower bit-widths, while (2) specific tokens contribute disproportionately to quantization errors due to their distinct value distributions.
As illustrated in Figure 2 , MultiAlloc consists of three stages: decoupled precision demand profiling, budget-coupled precision allocation, and static policy execution.
4.1 Problem Formulation and Overview We formulate KV cache quantization as a precision allocation problem under a fixed KV cache memory budget.
Specifically, let 𝐪 = { q l } l = 1 L \mathbf{q}=\{q_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L} denote the layer-wise bit-width configuration, where q l q_{l} represents the quantization precision of the KV cache at layer l l .
Meanwhile, let 𝐤 = { k l } l = 1 L \mathbf{k}=\{k_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L} denote the token preservation configuration, where k l k_{l} represents the number of high-precision tokens preserved per group at layer l l .
Given a fixed KV cache memory budget B B , the objective is to minimize the quantization-induced degradation while satisfying the memory budget: min 𝐪 , 𝐤 ⁡ ℒ ⁡ ( 𝐪 , 𝐤 ) s . t . ℳ ⁡ ( 𝐪 , 𝐤 ) ≤ B . \min_{\mathbf{q},\mathbf{k}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{k})\quad\mathrm{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{k})\leq B. (5) where ℒ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathcal{L}(\cdot) denotes the estimated degradation caused by KV cache compression and ℳ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \mathcal{M}(\cdot) denotes the corresponding memory consumption.
To address this challenge, MultiAlloc adopts an offline profiling and allocation pipeline, as illustrated in Figure 2 .
4.2 Layer-wise Precision Demand Estimation Different Transformer layers exhibit different sensitivity to KV cache quantization, resulting in heterogeneous requirements for quantization precision.
The resulting profile { S l ​ ( b ) } l = 1 L \{S_{l}(b)\}_{l=1}^{L} captures the precision requirements of different layers under candidate bit-widths.
4.3 Token-wise Preservation Demand Estimation Besides layer-wise precision differences, KV cache quantization also exhibits token-level heterogeneity, where a small subset of tokens can contribute disproportionately to inference quality.
For a token representation 𝐊 l , t \mathbf{K}_{l,t} at layer l l , we measure its importance using the activation magnitude: s l , t = ‖ 𝐊 l , t ‖ 1 , s_{l,t}=\|\mathbf{K}_{l,t}\|_{1}, (9) which provides a lightweight proxy for the contribution of token t t to attention computation.
To estimate the preservation demand, we measure the importance mass lost after preserving k l k_{l} tokens in each group: ℒ tok ​ ( k l ) = ∑ t ∈ 𝒢 l s l , t − ∑ t ∈ TopK ⁡ ( 𝒢 l , k l ) s l , t , \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tok}}(k_{l})=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{G}_{l}}s_{l,t}-\sum_{t\in\mathrm{TopK}(\mathcal{G}_{l},k_{l})}s_{l,t}, (10) where 𝒢 l \mathcal{G}_{l} denotes a token group at layer l l .
By evaluating different preservation capacities, we obtain a token-level preservation profile: { ℒ tok ​ ( k l ) } l = 1 L , \{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tok}}(k_{l})\}_{l=1}^{L}, which characterizes the precision demand of preserving additional tokens.
4.4 Budget-Coupled Precision Allocation The layer-wise and token-wise profiling stages provide complementary precision demand signals for allocation.
Given a KV cache memory budget B B , we first construct a set of token preservation candidates: 𝒪 = { o i } i = 1 N , \mathcal{O}=\{o_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}, (11) where each candidate o i o_{i} specifies a layer-wise token preservation capacity configuration: o i = { k i , l } l = 1 L . o_{i}=\{k_{i,l}\}_{l=1}^{L}. (12) For each candidate o i o_{i} , its token preservation memory cost M tok ​ ( o i ) M_{\mathrm{tok}}(o_{i}) and degradation ℒ tok ​ ( o i ) \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tok}}(o_{i}) are estimated according to the token preservation profiles.
B_{\mathrm{layer}}(o_{i})=B-M_{\mathrm{tok}}(o_{i}). (13) Under the remaining layer budget, the optimal layer-wise bit-width configuration is obtained by solving: q i ∗ = arg ⁡ min 𝐪 \displaystyle q_{i}^{*}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{q}} ℒ layer ​ ( 𝐪 ) \displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{layer}}(\mathbf{q}) (14) s . t . \displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.} M layer ​ ( 𝐪 ) ≤ B layer ​ ( o i ) , \displaystyle M_{\mathrm{layer}}(\mathbf{q})\leq B_{\mathrm{layer}}(o_{i}), where 𝐪 = { q l } l = 1 L \mathbf{q}=\{q_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L} denotes the layer-wise precision configuration.
J_{i}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{layer}}(q_{i}^{*})+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{tok}}(o_{i}). (15) The final precision allocation policy is selected as: i ∗ = arg ⁡ min i ⁡ J i , i^{*}=\arg\min_{i}J_{i}, (16) yielding the final policy π ∗ = ( q ∗ , k ∗ ) . \pi^{*}=(q^{*},k^{*}).
4.5 Static Policy Execution After the offline allocation stage, MultiAlloc obtains a static precision policy π ∗ \pi^{*} , which specifies the layer-wise precision configuration and token preservation configuration.
5 Experiments 5.1 Experimental Setup We evaluate reasoning and long-context performance on five instruction-tuned models spanning architectures and scales: Llama-3-8B-Instruct Grattafiori et al. The overall score averages the GPQA, GSM8K, and LongBench-v2 macro-average scores.

(2026) , an importance-guided hybrid KV cache quantization method, and our profiling-guided precision allocation framework MultiAlloc .
5.2 Main Results under Fixed KV Budgets Table 1 reports the main results under fixed, model-specific KV cache budgets.

On DeepSeek-Qwen-14B, MultiAlloc matches the best compressed GPQA result and achieves the strongest performance on GSM8K and LongBench-v2.
On QwQ-32B, it achieves the best GPQA and LongBench-v2 performance under the same budget.

===== 2610.32417 (129 条) =====
We develop 29 agent tasks with automatic pass/fail checks in six families, ranging from single-answer SQL analytics to multi-step repository bug fixing, and measure CPU and GPU energy per output token and per completed task for 4-bit and 8-bit versions of two open-weight models on bare-metal NVIDIA A100 nodes with llama.cpp.
Our findings reveal that 4-bit precision generally saves energy per token, though whether it saves energy per completed task is task dependent and changes with the model and the execution budget (i.e., limits on turns, tokens, and time).
A 4-bit file needs less memory than an 8-bit one, usually generates each token with less energy, and often keeps accuracy close to the unquantized model ( Jin et al., 2024 ; Giagnorio et al., 2026 ) .
Benchmarks such as TokenPowerBench report exactly this energy per output token ( Niu et al., 2026 ; Samsi et al., 2023 ; Wilhelm et al., 2025 ) .
Jang et al. (2026) show that quantized agents make more failed tool calls and need more model responses and tool calls before finishing, and Pham et al. (2026) find that failed attempts carry a substantial energy cost of their own.
Choosing a 4-bit instead of an 8-bit quantized model needs no new hardware and no change to the agent, and it lowers both memory and per-token energy.
RQ4:: How do the execution budget, the model, and the task set change which precision is cheaper per completed task, and is an unquantized 16-bit model (which needs twice the GPU memory) ever cheaper than the quantized files?
We run the 4-bit Q4_K_M and 8-bit Q8_0 files 1 1 1 Q4_K_M and Q8_0 are llama.cpp quantization formats that store most weights in 4 and 8 bits ( Section 2.1 ).
Where the two measures favor different precisions, which we call a disagreement, we measure the extra energy per completed task to answer RQ2.
To address RQ4, we repeat the comparison with an expanded budget, a 16-bit reference, Devstral Small 2 ( Mistral AI, 2025 ) , and held-out tasks from the BIRD text-to-SQL and EvalPerf code-efficiency benchmarks ( Li et al., 2023 ; Liu et al., 2024 ) .
Summary of findings 4-bit uses 6–16% less energy per output token than 8-bit on average over the 29 tasks for both models, so per-token benchmarks favor it almost everywhere ( Section 5.1 ).
These tokens arise from malformed tool calls and unnecessary reruns of tests that had already passed, which prolong attempts, as well as wrong answers that cause attempts to fail ( Section 5.3 ).
As tasks scale in difficulty, the precision choice becomes more concerned with completion rate, and which precision completes more depends on how much context and how many turns the agent is given ( Sections 5.1 and 5.4 ).
These findings hold through a second model, whose costly tasks differ from the first, and the patterns of our task families carry over to held-out benchmark problems, correctly predicting where 4-bit is cheaper per completed task.
An unquantized 16-bit model is never clearly cheaper than the quantized files ( Section 5.4 ).
Quantized models for local inference Local deployments commonly serve models with llama.cpp ( Gerganov and llama.cpp contributors, 2023 ) from GGUF files 2 2 2 GGUF is the single-file model format of llama.cpp.
A precision label such as Q4_K_M names a quantization scheme, not only a bit width.
Q4_K_M stores most tensors at 4 bits in blocks that share a scale and an offset, and it keeps some attention and feed-forward tensors at 6 bits ( Kawrakow, 2023 ) .
Our 4-bit files were also calibrated on sample text, so weights whose inputs are largest on that text are rounded most carefully ( Bartowski, 2025b ; Bartowski, 2025a ) .
Q8_0 rounds weights to 8 bits in blocks of 32 with one scale per block and needs no calibration ( llama.cpp contributors, 2026 ) .
A BF16 file holds the original, unquantized weights in 16-bit bfloat16.
Because of the shared scales and the 6-bit tensors, each quantized file stores more bits per weight than its label suggests (8.5 for Q8_0 and 4.88 for our 4-bit Qwen file).
Yellow cells are the values that each quantized block shares to rescale its weights (one 16-bit scale in Q8_0 , and a scale, an offset, and a share of a larger group’s scale and offset in Q4_K_M ), so they add storage beyond the bit width.
Each shows the same grid of 32 weights, with cells half as wide at 8 bits and a quarter as wide at 4 bits, plus small yellow cells for shared scales next to the quantized grids.
Bars below give file sizes of 57, 30, and 17 GiB on one scale.
Quantized models, including 4-bit ones, often stay close to their unquantized versions on general and code-generation benchmarks ( Jin et al., 2024 ; Li et al., 2024 ; Giagnorio et al., 2026 ; Wei et al., 2023 ) .
The loss is larger on long-context inputs ( Mekala et al., 2025 ) and on realistic agent applications such as simulated science experiments and text games, where 4-bit quantization lowered accuracy by 10 to 15% ( Dong et al., 2025 ) .
Whether quantization lengthens outputs depends on its severity and on the model ( Liu et al., 2025 ) .
The speed and energy saved by a lower precision also depend on the hardware, kernels, and batching, including whether dequantization is fused into the matrix multiplication ( Benazir and Lin, 2025 ; Chung et al., 2026b ; Delavande et al., 2026 ; Rajput and Sharma, 2024 ) .
From energy per token to energy per completed task Units beyond the token Energy per output token is the common unit of inference benchmarks ( Niu et al., 2026 ; Samsi et al., 2023 ; Wilhelm et al., 2025 ) , and studies that compare quantization methods report it beside accuracy without combining the two ( Husom et al., 2025 ; Shi and Ding, 2025 ) .
The energy per successful goal of Panigrahy and Tyagi (2026) divides the energy of all attempts, including failed ones, by the number of successes, and our energy per completed task ( Section 3 ) applies this goal-level accounting to the CPU packages and GPU board of the machine that runs the model and its tools.
Intelligence per Watt relates accuracy on single-turn queries to measured energy across 16-, 8-, and 4-bit models ( Saad-Falcon et al., 2026 ) , and TokenArena ranks endpoints by estimated joules per correct answer ( Gao et al., 2026 ) .
Energy accumulates over the model calls, tools, and waiting of the task window, and in the business query of our study (i.e., an SQL query that summarizes sales by customer segment), 4-bit is cheaper per token but costlier per completed task. (a) An example attempt, not to scale.
Pham et al. (2026) quantify the energy of failed runs of a local 4-bit agent, and Jang et al. (2026) vary precision without measuring energy, finding that a quantized agent can succeed as often while making more failed tool calls.
The attempt starts from a newly loaded model and ends when the agent submits a result or reaches a limit of its execution budget ( Section 4.3 ).
The model-call energy per output token, the unit that per-token benchmarks report ( Niu et al., 2026 ; Samsi et al., 2023 ) . • Model-call energy per completed task, E inf E_{\mathrm{inf}} .
It charges every attempt, failed or not, to the completions, following the energy per successful goal of Panigrahy and Tyagi (2026) .
Each completion carries the energy of one successful attempt plus a share of the failed ones, and the weight ( 1 − p ) / p (1-p)/p grows quickly as the completion rate falls.
At p = 1 / 2 p=1/2 each completion also pays for one failed attempt, and at p = 1 / 4 p=1/4 it pays for three.

The two precisions of a comparison differ in the model file and share the machine, engine build, tasks, tools, budget, and sampling seeds ( Sections 4.1 and 4.3 ). • Verify.
Attempts differ in length and outcome, so we attribute energy to each model call and tool call and charge failed attempts to the completions ( Section 4.4 ).

Each has two AMD EPYC 7763 processors and one NVIDIA A100 GPU with 40 GB of memory (80 GB on one machine).
Models We chose two models that differ in architecture and vendor and that fit entirely in the memory of one 40 GB GPU at 4-bit and at 8-bit, so all computation stays on the GPU at either precision.
The main model, Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507 ( Qwen Team, 2025 ; Yang et al., 2025 ) , is an instruction-tuned mixture-of-experts release without a separate thinking mode.
It has 30.5 billion parameters, of which 3.3 billion are active per token (i.e., each token passes through a small subset of expert sub-networks, while the file stores all 30.5 billion).
Precisions We compare the Q4_K_M and Q8_0 files, which differ in bit width, quantization scheme, calibration ( Section 2.1 ), and kernels.
The Qwen 16-bit file needs 57 GiB ( Figure 1 ), so we run the 16-bit reference on the 80 GB machine, where every precision fits entirely in GPU memory at the largest context we use.
Tasks and verification Energy per completed task needs a completion criterion that is objective, reproducible, and identical for every precision, so we use verifiable tasks , whose result an automatic check accepts or rejects, as repository-level coding benchmarks do ( Jimenez et al., 2024 ) .

Setting the budgets Limits on context, turns, tool calls, output tokens, and time keep every attempt finite, and together they form its execution budget ( Table 3 ).
We fixed the primary budget after pilot runs, setting each limit above what pilot attempts outside repository repair needed (e.g., a 600 s deadline well above the longest pilot attempt, and 2,048 tokens per turn for answers of about 1,100 tokens).
The expanded budget’s turn limit equals the step limit of mini-SWE-agent ( SWE-agent contributors, 2025 ; Yang et al., 2024b ) for SWE-bench ( Jimenez et al., 2024 ) , where a step is also one model response.
With the primary budget, 389 of 399 attempts on the other families end by submitting, and with the expanded budget, all such attempts end by submitting.

===== 2611.02368 (48 条) =====
However, final accuracy can conceal substantial behavioral changes: AWQ reduces Qwen-7B one-shot solves from 17 to 0 while increasing loop-based solves from 10 to 25.
Failure profiles also differ: Qwen-32B produces far more compilation errors than the smaller Qwen models, rising slightly under quantization, while Devstral’s failures shift significantly from runtime errors toward wrong answers and timeouts.
Recent benchmarks such as OJBench ( Wang et al., 2025 ) provide challenging settings for studying this behavior.
Quantization is a widely used approach for reducing these costs ( Frantar et al., 2023 ; Xiao et al., 2023 ; Liu et al., 2024 ) .
However, reasoning performance can be sensitive to low-bit quantization, especially on difficult tasks where small perturbations can affect multi-step generation ( Liu et al., 2025 ) .
In this work, we present, to the best of our knowledge, the first empirical study of 4-bit weight-only quantization in execution-feedback coding agents.
We evaluate BF16, AWQ, and GPTQ versions of Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct ( Hui et al., 2024 ) at 7B, 14B, and 32B scales, together with Devstral-Small-2507 ( Mistral AI, 2025 ) .
We investigate three research questions: RQ1: How robust is end-to-end coding-agent performance to 4-bit quantization?
RQ2: Does end-to-end accuracy conceal changes in coding-agent solution trajectories?
RQ3: Does quantization change how coding agents fail, not just how often?
Overall accuracy is over the Easy and Medium splits (115 problems); the Hard split is excluded because all models scored 0%.
Existing evaluations cover real-world data-science tasks through DSBC ( Kadiyala et al., 2025 ) , automated program repair ( Puvvadi et al., 2025 ) , interactive application environments such as AppWorld ( Trivedi et al., 2024 ) , multilingual repository-level tasks through SWE-PolyBench ( Rashid et al., 2025 ) , and competition-level programming through OJBench ( Wang et al., 2025 ) .
AWQ ( Lin et al., 2024 ) and GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) are widely used methods for 4-bit weight-only quantization.
Prior single-pass studies indicate that quantization generally preserves reasoning performance, although smaller models and more difficult tasks experience greater degradation ( Liu et al., 2025 ) .
3 Experimental Setup This section describes the setup used to evaluate BF16 and AWQ/GPTQ 4-bit quantized models on competitive coding tasks within an execution-based agentic workflow.
3.1 Dataset: OJBench We evaluate on OJBench ( Wang et al., 2025 ) , a benchmark of 232 competition-level problems from China’s National Olympiad in Informatics (NOI) and the International Collegiate Programming Contest (ICPC).
Evaluation across three seeds yields 345 problem–seed observations per configuration.
3.2 Evaluation Setup Models and Quantization We evaluate Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct ( Hui et al., 2024 ) at 7B, 14B, and 32B, spanning scales at which size may interact with quantization, plus the agent-oriented 24B Devstral-Small-2507 ( Mistral AI, 2025 ) as an out-of-family comparison.
Each model is run at BF16 and as W4A16 variants produced with AWQ ( Lin et al., 2024 ) and GPTQ ( Frantar et al., 2023 ) via llmcompressor ( AI and vLLM Project, 2024 ) (256 calibration samples, max sequence length 1,024).
Because every precision is evaluated on the same problems and seeds, outcomes are paired; we apply an exact two-sided McNemar test to the 345 paired observations, with b b and c c the discordant pairs solved by only the first or only the second precision, and p < 0.05 p<0.05 as the threshold.
Because each analysis involves eight BF16-versus-quantized comparisons, we also report Holm-adjusted p p -values.
4 Empirical Findings We investigate how weight-only quantization affects Python coding performance on OJBench for execution-feedback agents across BF16, AWQ, and GPTQ 4-bit precisions.
Table 1 reports accuracy, Figure 1 the failure-mode distribution, and Appendix A.2 the paired tests and tool-use statistics.
RQ1: How robust is end-to-end coding-agent performance to 4-bit quantization?
We evaluate whether replacing BF16 weights with W4A16 AWQ or GPTQ changes final coding accuracy in execution-feedback agents.
We compare accuracy across Qwen-2.5-Coder scales and Devstral-Small-2507, together with paired statistical tests in Table 3 .
Because we run eight BF16-versus-quantized comparisons, we apply a Holm correction, and because the three seeds of a problem are correlated, we also repeat the analysis with each problem as one unit.
Qwen-32B shows the largest drop, about 3 points under both quantizers, but it does not reach significance after correction ( p Holm = 0.21 p_{\mathrm{Holm}}=0.21 for AWQ) or at the problem level ( p = 0.051 p=0.051 ).
Qwen-2.5-Coder-32B shows the largest drop, but it does not reach statistical significance.
Malformed tool calls are rare for the Qwen models, but most of Devstral’s tool calls do not follow the tool schema, which keeps its recorded tool use low; quantization still reduces how often it tries the tool (AWQ: 42.0% → \rightarrow 27.0%; GPTQ: 42.0% → \rightarrow 21.4%).
We compare terminal failure categories (IR /WA /TLE /CE /MLE) across precision settings, and test whether each failure distribution differs from BF16 using a chi-square test with Holm correction (Table 2 ).
For Qwen-7B, AWQ shifts failures slightly away from Wrong Answer and toward Invalid Return (WA: 65.0 → 59.7 65.0\rightarrow 59.7 ; IR: 21.7 → 29.0 21.7\rightarrow 29.0 per seed).
Qwen-32B exhibits substantially more Compilation Errors than the smaller Qwen models, with CE rising slightly under quantization.
5 Conclusion We evaluated 4-bit weight-only quantization in execution-feedback coding agents on OJBench, comparing BF16, AWQ, and GPTQ across Qwen2.5-Coder (7B, 14B, 32B) and Devstral-Small-2507.
Quantization reduces checkpoint storage by about 3.4 × \times and causes no statistically significant accuracy loss for any agent after correcting for multiple comparisons, although Qwen-32B shows a consistent nominal drop.
More importantly, similar final accuracy can hide large behavioral shifts: Qwen-7B moves strongly from one-shot to loop-based solving after quantization, while Qwen-32B remains fully loop-dependent.
Our study focuses on one competition-level benchmark, one execution-feedback scaffold, and W4A16 quantization, with Python as the solution language.
Within these limits, 4-bit quantization appears practical for execution-feedback coding agents, but its reliability should be evaluated within the complete agentic workflow.

1 Introduction Figure 1 : Agent-driven compression outcomes. (a) Mean GSM8K, HumanEval, and TriviaQA accuracy relative to BF16 versus package bits per original parameter; values below 80% lie on the axis. (b) API cost versus within-task PPL rank; diamonds average six tasks per agent.
Large language model (LLM) inference is increasingly bounded by memory traffic rather than arithmetic: in autoregressive decoding, every generated token reads the model weights from GPU memory, and an off-chip memory access costs far more energy than the arithmetic it feeds ( Horowitz, 2014 ) .
Compression that preserves a model’s behavior within a smaller storage budget therefore reduces both the memory a deployment occupies and the energy spent on every token it generates, making models cheaper to serve and runnable on smaller hardware ( Frantar et al., 2023 ; Lin et al., 2024 ; Lin et al., 2025 ) .
Even at the same nominal precision, clipping, rounding, calibration, and metadata change the quality of a representation ( Frantar et al., 2023 ; Shao et al., 2024 ) .
The agent must implement an inference path suited to the target hardware, as in quantized inference systems ( Lin et al., 2024 ; Lin et al., 2025 ) , and verify that it runs correctly.
To evaluate these abilities without prescribing how agents compress, we formulate LLM compression as a terminal task ( Figure 2 ), following the outcome-based evaluation of Terminal-Bench and Harbor ( Merrill et al., 2026 ; Harbor Framework Team, 2026 ) .
Given source weights, a storage limit, a GPU, and a pinned runtime, the agent may combine quantization, mixed precision, pruning, low-rank representations, and distillation, and must implement the inference operations its representation requires ( Section 2.1 ).
A supplied evaluator returns the source-to-compressed perplexity (PPL) ratio on continuations sampled from the source model; the expected excess cross-entropy equals the next-token KL divergence from the source ( Section 2.3 ).
Both the saved package and the resident GPU weights must fit the limit; GSM8K, HumanEval, and TriviaQA accuracy must retain 80% of BF16; and review checks integrity.
In Section 3 , we evaluate the models that six coding agents deliver for three source models with two storage limits each.
Selecting the lowest-PPL agent design per task yields an average 3.4% PPL increase over BF16 and 77.33% downstream accuracy, compared with 79.56% for BF16 ( Section 3.2 ).
In 21 of 24 revisions that change package size by at most 0.01 GiB, agents lower PPL by improving the quantizer or redistributing precision ( Section 4.1 ).
Intermediate models gain and lose correct answers at nearly unchanged PPL, and consistency tests expose inference bugs that likelihood misses ( Section 4.3 ).

2.2 Experiments and Feedback The agent allocates T T among implementation, calibration, recovery, local tests, and evaluation of complete candidates.
This interaction follows the budgeted experimentation setting of MLAgentBench and RE-Bench ( Huang et al., 2024 ; Wijk et al., 2025 ) .

===== 2611.07217 (270 条) =====
We therefore introduce TileQuant , a training-free framework that assigns precision to hardware-aligned 2D weight tiles.
Within each projection’s retention budget, the highest-scoring tiles remain at 16-bit and the rest use 8-bit.
The resulting map guides reconstruction into compact weight buffers, which the kernel reuses for native integer and 16-bit computation with local accumulation to reduce memory traffic.
Experiments show that TileQuant reaches 2.66 × 2.66\times the original model’s generation throughput.
The implementation is: https://github.com/HanzhiZhang-Ulrica/TileQuant 1 Introduction Efficient large language model (LLM) deployment requires reducing storage and inference costs while preserving quality in long-context understanding Liu et al. (2024a) ; An et al. (2024) , retrieval-augmented generation Lewis et al. (2020) ; Li et al. (2024) , and multi-document reasoning Bai et al. (2024) .
Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces these costs by lowering numerical precision without retraining Yao et al. (2022) ; Xiao et al. (2023) ; Frantar et al. (2022) .
Uniform PTQ Yao et al. (2022) assigns one weight bit-width across a matrix, while importance-aware PTQ Xiao et al. (2023) ; Lin et al. (2024) ; Frantar et al. (2022) adjusts channel scales or reconstructs weights using calibration data within that width.
When selection relies on runtime activation outliers, repeated detection and processing further increase decoding latency Dettmers et al. (2022) .
Figure 1: Comparison of PTQ precision-allocation units for LLMs.
TileQuant assigns precision to 2D tiles by local sensitivity.
Our key insight is to use hardware-aligned 2D weight tiles for local precision allocation.
Each tile covers a local region along both matrix dimensions, allowing precision to vary within a column while preserving a regular tile for GPU matrix multiplication Guide (2020) ; Choquette et al. (2021) ; Kerr et al. (2017) .
Each projection retains its highest-scoring tiles at 16-bit under a prescribed budget and quantizes the rest to 8-bit.
On Llama-3.2-3B, TileQuant reaches 2.66 × 2.66\times the generation throughput of the original 16-bit model while preserving quality across language modeling, reasoning, long-context retrieval, and question answering.
For example, GI with 20% tile retention exceeds Original in 26 of the 27 displayed LV-Eval dataset–length settings.
We assign 16-bit or 8-bit precision to hardware-aligned weight tiles, allowing selected regions within a column to retain higher precision.
2 Related Work We review uniform PTQ, importance-aware PTQ, and protected-subset PTQ through their choices of weight bit-width, calibration criterion, and high-precision retention.
Uniform PTQ applies a single weight bit-width to all weights within a chosen unit, producing a homogeneous low-precision representation for the target tensor Gholami et al. (2022) ; Lang et al. (2024) ; Zhao et al. (2025) ; Sharify et al. (2024) .
Representative examples include rounding and reconstruction methods Yao et al. (2022) ; Chee et al. (2025) ; Kogan (2025) ; Gong et al. (2024) and scaling- or transformation-based methods Xiao et al. (2023) ; Pan et al. (2023) ; Wei et al. (2022b) ; Shao et al. (2023) ; Liu et al. (2023) ; Ashkboos et al. (2024b) ; Liu et al. (2024b) ; Sun et al. (2024) that reduce quantization error in weights and activations.
Importance-aware PTQ addresses these sensitivity differences by estimating which weights or channels are sensitive to quantization error and adjusting scaling factors or reconstruction objectives accordingly Li and Panda (2024) ; Zhang et al. (2025) ; Arai and Ichikawa (2025) .
Calibration-based methods leverage activation or sensitivity statistics to pinpoint salient weights Lin et al. (2024) ; Kim et al. (2023) ; Kim et al. (2024) ; Xue et al. (2025) , while reconstruction-based approaches optimize the quantized tensor to better match original outputs Nagel et al. (2020) ; Li et al. (2021) ; Wei et al. (2022a) ; Frantar et al. (2022) ; Wei et al. (2025) ; Yvinec et al. (2023) .
Protected-subset PTQ further introduces non-uniform precision by preserving selected substructures in higher precision while quantizing the remaining weights Dettmers et al. (2022) ; Ashkboos et al. (2024a) ; Dettmers et al. (2023) ; Guo et al. (2023) ; Shang et al. (2023) ; Yu et al. (2024) .
These methods demonstrate that non-uniform precision can recover accuracy under quantization Li et al. (2023b) ; Saxena et al. (2024) ; Xie et al. (2025) ; Xiao et al. (2025) .
3 Preliminaries We first review quantized linear projections and then introduce their tile-wise decomposition to support local precision allocation.
3.1 PTQ for Transformer Linear Layers During LLM inference, a linear layer computes Y = X ​ W ⊤ Y=XW^{\top} , where X ∈ ℝ N × K X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K} , W ∈ ℝ M × K W\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times K} , and Y ∈ ℝ N × M Y\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times M} .
For a positive quantization scale Δ \Delta , the 8-bit integer weights are W ^ = clip [ − 127,127 ] ⁡ ( ⌊ W / Δ ⌉ ) \widehat{W}=\operatorname{clip}_{[-127,127]}(\lfloor W/\Delta\rceil) , with Δ ​ W ^ ≈ W \Delta\widehat{W}\approx W .
3.2 Tile-Wise Linear Projection To support local precision allocation, we partition W W into a regular grid of fixed-size 2D tiles after any necessary padding.
Let W ( r , c ) W^{(r,c)} denote a tile, where r = 1 , … , n tr r=1,\dots,n_{\mathrm{tr}} indexes output-feature tiles and c = 1 , … , n tc c=1,\dots,n_{\mathrm{tc}} indexes input-feature tiles.
The output-feature tile Y ( r ) Y^{(r)} is then computed as Y ( r ) = \displaystyle Y^{(r)}={} ∑ c = 1 n tc X ( c ) ​ ( W ( r , c ) ) ⊤ , r = 1 , … , n tr . \displaystyle\sum_{c=1}^{n_{\mathrm{tc}}}X^{(c)}\big(W^{(r,c)}\big)^{\top},\qquad r=1,\dots,n_{\mathrm{tr}}.
We define ℱ ⁡ ( W ) \mathcal{F}(W) and 𝒬 ⁡ ( W ) \mathcal{Q}(W) as the tile-index sets assigned 16-bit and 8-bit precision, respectively.
Section 4 determines these assignments from calibration scores.
Figure 2: TileQuant framework for calibration-driven mixed-precision linear layers.
Per-layer top- k k selection converts either tile-score map into a 16-bit/8-bit decision map.
The reconstructed layer supplies the selected weights to two arithmetic paths, whose tile products are combined through output-local 32-bit floating-point accumulation.
4 TileQuant 4.1 TileQuant Overview Building on the tile-wise projection in Section 3 , TileQuant converts attention and feed-forward linear projections into mixed-precision layers.
Figure 2 shows how calibration choices reach the deployed layer.
A common rule retains the highest-scoring tiles within each projection’s budget and records their locations in a binary decision map (Section 4.2 ).
The resulting weight buffers and scales supply the mixed-precision kernel (Section 4.3 ), so the same offline choices govern arithmetic across inference inputs.
4.2 Calibration-Driven Tile Map Construction Calibration ranks local weight regions for each projection’s 16-bit retention budget.
Given a target 16-bit retention ratio ρ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \rho\in(0,1] , that projection retains k = max ⁡ ( 1 , ⌈ ρ ​ | 𝒯 ⁡ ( W ) | ⌉ ) k=\max\!\left(1,\left\lceil\rho\,|\mathcal{T}(W)|\right\rceil\right) tiles.
The k k highest-scoring tiles form ℱ ⁡ ( W ) \mathcal{F}(W) , and the others form 𝒬 ⁡ ( W ) \mathcal{Q}(W) for 8-bit quantization.
For a fixed tile grid and ρ \rho , IAA and GI retain the same number of 16-bit weights, while their scores determine the retained locations.
4.3 Mixed-Precision Reconstruction and Execution The decision map specifies which regions receive each precision.
During checkpoint loading, these IDs locate the 16-bit weights, and their complement identifies the weights to quantize to 8-bit.
IAA also uses its collected channel statistics to select weight scales, as detailed in Appendix D.2 .
Every 8-bit weight tile in column c c reuses this prepared tile, together with its stored weights W ^ ( r , c ) \widehat{W}^{(r,c)} and scale Δ W ( r , c ) \Delta_{W}^{(r,c)} .
The retained tiles multiply the current 16-bit inputs directly.
Each integer tile product first accumulates in 32-bit integers and is then converted to floating point for rescaling.
The kernel loads the corresponding inputs and weights as dense tiles for Tensor Core multiplication Guide (2020) ; Choquette et al. (2021) ; Kerr et al. (2017) .
Products from both precision paths enter a 32-bit floating-point (FP32) accumulator, allowing each program to combine its assigned tile contributions within its registers.

Experiments use NVIDIA A100 GPUs with 40 GB memory.
